Katalog Berita (Halaman 23/57)

#6958. 9 Curb Appeal Trends That Will Take Over in 2027, According to Designers

These are the exterior elements that are getting a major refresh.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Curb appeal is more than having a pretty house.

“It's not just about how a home looks, but how it lives,” says Danielle Faustini, landscape designer and owner of Helix Green Designs. Homeowners and designers alike are stepping up their game when it comes to creating an appealing facade—in some cases, the shift is quite literal, as stairs are becoming an “it” feature.

The latest U.S. Houzz Outdoor Trends study is seeing a shift toward natural elements like wood cladding, beige exteriors, and native plants.

"Professionals on Houzz have shared that homeowners are looking for exteriors that feel grounded and connected to their surroundings," says Annie Thornton, Houzz outdoor trends expert. “Warm beiges and wood siding blend naturally with stone, native plantings and other organic landscape materials, and they suit a wide range of architectural styles. Our studies show that people are moving away from the stark white and cool gray palettes of recent years toward something that feels more timeless, a look that adds a sense of history without tipping into nostalgia.”

Simple upgrades, like a fresh coat of paint on the front door, are also pivoting into colorways anchored in the past. Designer Alexis Warren is seeing a shift toward darker, moodier front doors in shades of green, deep purple, and near-black, like Farrow & Ball’s Paean Black.

Here are 10 curb-appeal-boosting design trends for the year ahead to make your house the envy of the neighborhood.

Introducing a sculptural element—like an outdoor artwork or creatively placed flora—sends a message that your house has a point of view.

“It elevates the sense of arrival and separates your home from other homes in the vicinity,” says designer Lisa Erdmann. To ensure your home truly stands out, look for one-of-a-kind pieces.

For the home pictured above, Robert J. Bell, founder of landscape architecture firm Bell Design, commissioned artisans to create a copper light inspired by a feather. “More than the end of minimalism, the new focus on sculptural shapes brings back a sense of craft, and the expressive and sensory nature that a simple black metal and glass fixture cannot possess,” he says.

To contrast against the patina, Bell chose purple bougainvillea and a sunny yellow paint on the door.

It’s all about adding warmth and texture, and that’s one of the reasons that beige exterior colors have more than doubled in popularity in the last two years, jumping from 10 to 24 percent, according to Houzz.

Think of a palette that extends into the landscape—warm neutrals such as tan, soft cream, khaki, sand, and greige, says Thornton, who also notes that natural wood and wood-look cladding, sandy stucco, and stone are a welcome change.

Adding paneling can mimic the warmth of wood without worrying about upkeep, points out Francisca Alonso, cofounder and CEO of AV Architects + Builders. This trick also helps soften the front elevation. “We like to combine minimalistic modern details with warm tones to make it more inviting and less commercial-looking,” Alonso adds.

A picket fence may be just what your house needs, but that doesn’t mean you have to resort to the predictable white paint.

“Adding a low-profile picket fence is one of the best ways to gently delineate a property line without in any way compromising sightlines of the home,” says Joelle Nesen, principal designer at Maison Inc. “The picket fence has the kind of classical lines that work well with an historic home like this Craftsman in Portland. By not defaulting to the expected white and leaving the fence a natural wood, the picket fence, with its carefully spaced slats that are almost see-through, allows it to blend in with the landscaping and trees that surround the home.”

When you choose gravel over grass or pavers, it’s all about an understated but classy look.

“Gravel and other permeable aggregates like crushed shells are some of my favorite materials for creating more informal outdoor living spaces,” says Faustini. “It is a beautifully tactile and sensory material. Who doesn't love the soft crunch underfoot? And it really softens a space, helping blur the line between outdoor living areas and the surrounding landscape.”

The look works especially well with decking and patinated light fixtures.

Adding native plants around your house feels cohesive, relaxed, and rooted in place, Thornton says.

Rachael Grochowski, founder and principal of RHG Architecture + Design, adds that she expects to see an increase in native landscapes, meadow-type gardens, as well as an integration of clover into lawns.

“The trend to embrace natural landscapes is not only about supporting environment regeneration, but it also supports our wellbeing by instigating wonder, beauty, and randomness,” she says.

Curb appeal is all about first impressions, and homeowners are not taking this feature lightly. Houzz reports that searches for “front walkway” have more than quadrupled (up 357 percent) in the first three quarters of 2026, compared with the same period last year.

Step design, in particular, is one feature that is growing in popularity.

When Sarah Crawford, associate designer with Courtney B. Smith Design, renovated her home, she added steps to her 1920s bungalow to literally and figuratively elevate the experience. The home initially had just one step from the sidewalk to the front door. By adding a series of stairs to gently guide the way to the door, she added a quiet sense of elegance in keeping with the wood-shingled exterior. Key to the transformation was selecting materials that reflect the character and style of the original home, in this case reclaimed granite. Scale and balance were essential to adding a series of stairs to gently guide visitors to the door.

“Steps should never be narrower than the doorway,” Crawford says. “In fact, the wider the steps, the more inviting the approach.”

For a different approach to the front entrance, consider stone pathways, especially those with a casual look, like grass pavers.

Interior designer Darci Hether likes large-format bluestone pavers, which offer a clean, timeless look. “The generous scale of the stones creates a sense of openness, while the greenery between them softens the overall appearance and adds a beautiful, natural element,” she explains.

Erdmann encourages her clients to forgo safe neutrals and treat the front entry door as a place for personality.

“Whether it is hot pink, lipstick red, cobalt blue, or Benjamin Moore’s color of the year, Webster Green—make a statement!” she says. “Don’t match the door to the shutters and choose something fun. A front door is the easiest, lowest-risk place to experiment since it takes one afternoon and a quart of paint.”

Case in point, Elizabeth Cross-Beard, interior designer behind Palm Henri, took to a vibrant peacock blue to accentuate the lines of the above 1925 French manor house. “When paired with natural materials and muted tones, [the paint] brings just enough color to intrigue the eye without disrupting the overall sense of classic grandeur. Unlike other blue tones which can call too much attention to them, this rich color is the front-door sweet spot,” Cross-Beard explains.

Go beyond ho-hum pots that stand sentry at your door. Look for large pieces with personality to anchor the entrance.

“Aged terracotta, weathered stone, or antique urns bring patina and character, helping even a newly built home feel established,” says MK Smith Boykin of Smith Interiors. “With that kind of presence, the planting itself can be quite simple.” The details are especially important when your home has architectural symmetry, like columns.

“Using matching plants only increases the allure of perfect visual balance,” Nesen adds. “The key is that the scale of the planters needs to match the proportions of the door and porch: Selecting pots or containers that are too small will immediately drain the drama and beauty of the front door statement.”

#6943. Everything to Know About Nicholas Hoult’s Wife, Bryana Holly

They just made their red carpet debut at the premiere of Nosferatu.

Nicholas Hoult’s latest flick How to Rob a Bank is about to hit theaters, which means the actor is in the thick of a press tour for the project and making rounds on red carpets.

He made a stop at Jake Shane’s podcast, Therapuss, during his press tour on November 11 and made rare comments about his wife—yes, wife—Bryana Holly.

I know what you’re thinking: How long has it been since Nicholas Hoult and Jennifer Lawrence called it quits, and when did he start dating Bryana? Ahead, find everything we know about Nicholas Hoult’s wife, Bryana Holly.

Bryana is signed to Wilhemina Models and occasionally poses for lingerie, swimsuit, and clothing brands. She also starred in Hoodie Allen’s music video for “Movie” in 2015:

If Bryana looks a little familiar, you may recognize her from her past relationships. The model was romantically linked to Brody Jenner in 2013 before she dated 5 Seconds of Summer drummer Ashton Irwin from 2015 to 2016.

The pair has been together since 2017, and in 2024, Nicholas confirmed they secretly tied the knot.

At the Governors Awards in November 2024, Nicholas called Bryana his “wife” while talking to E! News. “Afterwards, I remember I had my wife’s nails imprinted on my hand,” he said of her reaction to Nosferatu.

A few days later, he confirmed they were married to People, saying, “I feel like it’s fairly clear from my comments.”

Remember that Therapuss interview I mentioned earlier? Well, Nicholas shared the story of how he met his wife in 2016.

“I was shooting a movie,” he said during his convo with Jake. “We were kind of filming and shooting a Halloween party at the same time. And then we had actually a really fun…it was kind of an overcommitment first date.”

He shared that he and Bryana started chatting about their music taste and decided to go to the Desert Trip music festival (which he claimed is “affectionately known” as “Oldchella). They apparently bonded over listening to artists like The Rolling Stones, Paul McCartney, Bob Dylan, and The Who.

“We didn't really know each other, but we both wanted to go,” he explained. “We went to dinner once before just to be like, ‘Let’s make sure neither of us are complete lunatics.’”

Nicholas brought Bryana as his date to the Nosferatu premiere in L.A. back in 2024. And, if you were wondering, this marks their first red carpet appearance after being together for seven years.

Bryana and Nicholas welcomed their first child, Joaquin, in 2018. He’s been seen out and about with the Skins alum and has even made a rare appearance on Bryana’s Instagram (which is now private, womp).

The couple then quietly welcomed their second child in 2022, and according to Nicholas’ How to Rob a Bank co-star, Pete Davidson, they also gave birth to twins at some point.

“He’s a great dad, father of four,” Pete told Entertainment Tonight. “Four under what? 7 [years old] ... which is nuts.”

He also praised Nicholas for how much he catered to his family while filming.

“Elsie was pregnant while we were filming and he just had his twins,” Pete continued. “He would fly back every weekend to go see his family, and that was very inspiring and awesome.”

#6942. Madewell and The Six Bells Bring Storybook Charm to the Holidays

The Hudson Valley inn lends its old-world touch to the brand.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

There’s nothing quite like the holiday season to boost your sense of nostalgia for cozy moments at home—especially when they involve a touch of cold-weather whimsy. Whether you’re sipping on a hot coffee or tea as you watch the changing leaves or you’re curled up next to a warm fireplace with your latest read, the colder months of the year are primed for ample charm. Of course, these moments are not without their accoutrements, which is right where the new Madewell collaboration with The Six Bells—a country inn located in the Hudson Valley—comes in.

The Six Bells Inn, founded by Audrey Gelman, features interiors inspired by the imagined English village known as Barrow’s Green—complete with its own fictional characters and landmarks—giving the space an old-world touch that feels as if it’s been plucked right out of a storybook. It’s this distinct aesthetic, along with Gelman’s way of storytelling, that led to the inn’s collaboration with Madewell.

“Steven [Cateron, Madewell’s creative director,] and the team at Madewell understood our little world at Six Bells from the very first conversation we had. In the early stages of working together on this collaboration, we wanted this collection to feel like stepping through a doorway into another time where things feel a little more enchanted,” Gelman told ELLE.com in an email. “Every piece in the collection was designed to have that sense of wonder. We hope it brings the spirit of The Six Bells into people’s lives and homes this holiday season.”

With a range of apparel, accessories, and home décor—including vintage-inspired needlepoint ornaments and pillows, stockings, and toiletry bags featuring custom prints based on the inn’s interiors—the collection combines Madewell’s laid-back style with The Six Bells’s country charm. “For the first time, we’re really leaning into home accents, which let us extend [the Madewell] world beyond what you wear and into the spaces you live in,” Cateron said in an email. “The inn’s wallpapers and patterns were a huge source of inspiration, and you’ll find bits of them throughout the collection, right down to the lining of our Denim Trucker Jacket.” There are also pajama sets and even a nightgown for those who want to take one of summer’s biggest trends straight into the holiday season. And with prices ranging from $17 to $248, there are plenty of pieces ready for the gift-giving season (and no doubt, whoever receives these will be tickled to unwrap them).

The limited-edition, 23-piece collection officially launches on November 12 at 10 a.m.; however, ELLE.com readers have exclusive access to the collection now. Shop the collection below.

#6939. What Does the Winner of ‘Dancing With the Stars’ Get, Exactly?

It’s a very different situation for the pro vs. the celeb.

Dancing With the Stars is wrapping up season 35 and someone is about to walk away with a glittery mirrorball trophy and a bunch of money. Actually several someones are going to walk away with a bunch of money, because Dancing With the Stars shares the love between multiple contestants when it comes to winnings. If you have questions, we have answers, so let’s get to it.

Nope. The winner of Dancing With the Stars doesn’t get extra money for winning—they just get the Mirrorball Trophy. However, they do get financially compensated for their time on the show—within an established payment structure.

The cast makes money for each week they remain on the show. According to Variety, everyone takes home $125,000 for the rehearsal period, as well as the first two weeks DWTS airs—meaning, even if you’re voted off you still get paid. The remaining celebs then get bonuses each week they continue.

A while back, there were reports (via Variety) that contestants got an additional $10,000 for weeks three and four, an additional $20,000 for weeks five and six, an additional $30,000 for weeks seven and eight, and then another $50,000 for weeks nine and ten.

This means that all the final celebs who make it to the Dancing With the Stars finale earn $295,000, regardless of whether or not they win.

Pros earn a per-episode salary. We have a full breakdown this week, but according to Us Weekly the highest-paid dancers (aka the pros who have been around for the longest) make around $5,000 an episode.

But there’s no extra baked-in “prize” for the pros if they secure a win, though there is a bonus. Jenna Johnson explained how it works to The Morning After podcast, saying “Whoever makes it to the finale, you all get paid up until then. And you do get a nice bonus. If you win, it’s not like you get a million dollars. You’re just getting a cute trophy together.”

Jenna also confirmed that pros who are voted out earlier are “guaranteed until a certain amount of weeks” pay. Which is something Brandon Armstrong’s wife, Brylee, confirmed on TikTok last year, saying “Brandon is still very much a pro on the show. Just because his partner was eliminated doesn’t mean that he just gets the boot and he’s gotta pack his bags and go all the way back to Utah.” Brylee added that “Dancing with the Stars takes care of their people, so no need to worry.”

#6928. What Is an Upper Bleph? Here’s What to Know About the Eyelid Surgery

Doctors explain how the procedure works, when a brow lift may be needed, and nonsurgical options.

In the world of plastic surgery, the nose, breasts, and butt have commanded a lot of time and attention under the knife. But as tech advances and patients seek out more subtle ways to manage aging skin, doctors have shifted their focus to other areas, some once deemed too risky to touch. Blepharoplasty, or bleph, for short, is a plastic surgery procedure that trims, redistributes, or removes excess skin on the eyelids.

The procedure can be done to help improve peripheral vision or for more cosmetic purposes, like removing hooded eyelids. Blephs can be done on the eyelid itself, which is known as an upper bleph, or a lower bleph, which is done below the eye to treat puffy or saggy skin. Even the most effective eye creams and potent skin care formulas sometimes may not be enough to correct excess skin around the eyes, but before you schedule your consultation, see what doctors have to say about the procedure to determine if it’s the best option for you right now.

An upper bleph specifically addresses the eyelid, where skin and sometimes fat closest to the nose are removed. Dr. Lyle Leipziger, chief of plastic surgery at North Shore University Hospital and LIJ Medical Center in New York, explains that the procedure involves removing skin and excising or repositioning deeper fatty tissue to address droopiness, puffiness, and under-eye bags. “As we age, the skin around the eyes naturally loses elasticity, which can cause the upper eyelids to droop, look heavy, or create a tired appearance. This can also be exacerbated by excess upper eyelid fatty tissue,” says Leipziger. During surgery, he explains, excess skin is removed, and surgeons may also remove or reposition small amounts of fat to create a smoother, more defined upper eyelid contour. “The goal is to open up the eyes and create a brighter, more refreshed appearance while preserving the patient’s natural eye shape,” he adds.

Dr. Lesley Rabach, a board-certified facial plastic surgeon and co-founder of Rabach Aesthetics in NYC, says that the best candidates for an upper bleph are people who have a “normal” eyebrow position with excess skin, also known as dermatochalasia. “When considering an upper bleph, your surgeon needs to look at the eyebrow position, eyelid skin, and fat, as well as how much the eye opens when determining if an upper bleph is the correct procedure,” she notes. Rabach says that sometimes a brow lift and/or ptosis repair—which reconstructs the eyelid muscles or connects it to the forehead muscle to lift it—is part of the surgery as well.

Additionally, Leipziger adds that blephs are ideal for individuals who have excess skin or fullness in their upper eyelids that makes their eyes look heavy, tired, or aged. “This is especially common as we get older and lose elasticity in the delicate skin around the eyes,” he points out. “Ideal candidates are generally in good overall health, have realistic expectations, and are bothered by drooping upper eyelid skin and possible fatty tissue.” He recommends having a consultation with a board-certified plastic surgeon to determine whether overhanging skin, excess fatty tissue, brow position, or another anatomical factor is contributing to the appearance of the upper eyelids or if it’s something else.

Rabach says the results are both subtle and obvious at the same time. “They remove the extra skin that often has a crepey texture while restoring the youthful appearance of the eyelid,” she says. “An upper bleph should restore the natural youthful architecture of the upper eyelid that is natural to the person.”

By removing excess skin and, according to Leipziger, when appropriate, repositioning small amounts of fat from the upper eyelids, the procedure helps create a more open and defined eye area. “Patients should experience a significant improvement with their eyes looking brighter and less tired, without ever looking ‘done’” says Leipziger. He also notes that while an upper bleph usually lasts a long time, it doesn’t slow down or stop the way your skin will naturally age. So you may see some sagging or loss of elasticity return over time. “The goal is a natural-looking enhancement rather than a dramatic change in appearance,” Leipziger expresses.

The eye area is very delicate, but blephs are less invasive than you’d think. Rabach performs the procedure at her practice several times a week using just local anesthesia. “However, we use very thin stitches that stay in place for close to one week and must be removed. While there is little to no pain, it’s hard to camouflage the stitches, so we tell our patients they will feel perfectly fine to work from home even after the surgery that day, but not be working from the office,” says Rabach.

Leipziger agrees that recovery from a blepharoplasty is generally minimal compared to other cosmetic surgeries. He says most patients experience some swelling, bruising, and mild discomfort during the first week, which gradually improves. “I typically recommend taking about five to seven days off from work and social activities for an upper blepharoplasty, and a little longer when a lower blepharoplasty is performed,” says Leipziger, adding that most people can resume normal activities within two weeks.

For long-term eye lifting and opening, blephs are the way to go, but Leipziger says several nonsurgical treatments can help improve the appearance of the upper eyelids—even if only temporarily. It all depends on your specific concerns. “Botox can provide a subtle brow lift by relaxing specific muscles, which can help open up the eye area. Certain laser and radiofrequency treatments can also improve skin quality and mild skin laxity by stimulating collagen production and tightening the skin. These options can be beneficial for patients with early signs of aging or minimal excess skin,” says Leipziger. The biggest difference is that the nonsurgical options cannot remove excess eyelid skin or fat. “For patients with significant skin laxity or hooding, an upper blepharoplasty is typically the most effective solution for achieving a more open, refreshed appearance,” Leipziger clarifies.

According to Rabach, a fat transfer is another option to restore hollowness along the orbital bone. This technique can help smooth crepey texture that can set in with age. She also recommends lasers such as CO2 to help improve skin quality, but specifies they aren’t a replacement for surgical removal of excess skin.

As previously mentioned, eyelid skin—even if there’s less of it—will continue to age after an upper bleph. “Long-lasting results come from general health and care of the area. Always wear sunscreen and wear sunglasses to protect the skin, as it’s the thinnest skin on the body and very delicate and prone to sun damage,” says Rabach.

Along with the non-negotiable sunscreen, Leipziger recommends medical-grade skin care products and ingredients such as retinol or peptides to support skin quality. “Additionally, patients may benefit from complementary nonsurgical treatments, such as Botox, laser skin rejuvenation, and/or chemical peels. While surgery cannot stop the aging process, proper skin care and maintenance can help patients enjoy their refreshed appearance for many years,” Leipziger concludes.

#6922. From Chocolate Chrome to Plaid Prints, These Are the Chicest Thanksgiving Nail Ideas to Try This Year

Your fingertips also deserve to be dressed up for the feast.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

We are officially in the thick of November, which means festive nail art is now on the horizon. And with Thanksgiving just a few weeks away, there's no better time to lean into a playful fall manicure than at your next nail appointment.

I rarely need an excuse to get a fresh set. Still, Thanksgiving feels like a particularly good one to experiment with autumnal designs and shades that are perfectly on-theme for the big feast. Between family dinners and Friendsgiving celebrations, there are plenty of opportunities to show off your nails. “The holiday season is the perfect excuse to embrace maximalist nail art,” says celebrity nail artist Max Estrada. “If your nails want to dress up for dinner, let them.”

Ahead, I chatted with nail artists about their favorite Thanksgiving nail ideas, along with polish recommendations to help you recreate a turkey-day-ready look.

“Thanksgiving is all about family gatherings, cozy evenings, and the warmth of the fall season,” says celebrity nail artist Vikki Swan. “I gravitate toward earthy tones and designs that reflect this atmosphere, so I especially love plaid patterns.” You can choose classic stripes or argyle prints, and go for an all over pattern or a subtle French tip. It’s quite literally the manicure equivalent of your favorite knit sweater.

Let the changing colors of fall foliage be the inspo behind your next manicure. “I’m loving gold leaf and metallic foils for Thanksgiving,” says Estrada. “The irregular placement of the leafing is part of the beauty and you don’t need every nail to look identical.” You’ll be sure to have the most on-theme manicure at the holiday table.

“I love a classic French manicure with a seasonal twist, such as chocolate brown, caramel, or burgundy tips,” says Swan. Start off with a milky or nude base, and simply choose your favorite fall hue for the tip of the nail.

Animal prints in the brown hue family, like tiger, cheetah, and fawn, are an ideal pattern to lean into for November. They won’t feel stale days after your Thanksgiving feast is over, either. There are plenty of print variations to play with, but I especially love the look of a milky base with spots or stripes layered on top in the shape of a French tip.

Both Estrada and Swan love a warm brown polish this time of year, “paired with a subtle pearl chrome finish provides beautiful depth and a soft shimmer to the nails,” Swan says. “Cat-eye effects are another big part of the conversation,” Estrada adds. “They give those rich colors movement and dimension, so the manicure becomes interesting through the finish itself.” You can’t go wrong with OPI’s Cliffside Karaoke or Zoya’s Thatcher.

Why settle on one fall hue when you can wear a different shade on every finger? “Using different shades from the same color family across the nails and transitioning from lighter to deeper tones, is a simple yet elegant way to capture the fall mood without intricate nail art,” says Swan. Just choose a few of your favorite polishes, and they don’t need to match perfectly, either.

Jasmine Hyman is the assistant beauty editor at Cosmo who covers all things nails—from the latest design trends to new polish launches. For this story, she consulted celebrity nail artists for their favorite Thanksgiving nail ideas.

Jasmine Hyman is the Assistant Beauty Editor at Cosmopolitan, where she writes about the latest beauty trends and must-have products. Her most prized beauty possessions are a meticulous skincare routine and salon blowouts. You’ll also likely find her in bed reading a good book or endlessly scrolling TikTok (spoiler: it’s usually the latter) while listening to Harry Styles’ entire discography on repeat. Follow her on Insta to be inundated with pictures of her meals.

#6921. 14 Everyday Home Items You Need to Replace More Often Than You Think

These overlooked swaps can make your home cleaner, safer, and fresher.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Your home might be a showcase of timeless design, with stylish accessories, custom artwork, and high-end furniture. But when the everyday things that keep a household functioning are left unattended to for long enough, the space may be less clean and organized (and safe!) than it looks.

“Even the most gorgeous spaces lose their charm if we forget to update essentials,” says Linda Hayslett, principal designer at LH.Designs. “Keeping your home looking and feeling fresh isn’t just about design—it’s also about the little things.”

Caring for your home means looking beyond the interiors. Giving neglected areas the attention they need can keep germs and grime at bay, and replacing certain items can also protect your health and safety. Consider your carbon monoxide alarm: If an aging unit fails during an emergency, the consequences could be deadly.

To help you keep everything in good shape, we asked experts which 14 household items you should replace as soon as possible. Take a look—you can thank us later!

Smoke and carbon monoxide detectors lose their sensitivity over time, which could delay detection during an emergency. The timeframe for when you should replace these will vary by manufacturer. Look up the model you have online to be sure—even a fairly new alarm should be tested once a month. The batteries should be replaced once a year.

Mold and mildew can build up on shower liners, especially in damp environments. A 2024 study by SafeHome.org showed that shower curtains had the most germs of any other surfaces in the bathroom, including the toilet seat. While Hayslett recommends washing them every three months, the material can eventually become brittle over time.

Dingy towels are the antithesis of luxe. Towels lose their absorbency and softness after years of washing.

“Avoid using too much detergent, which can build up on fibers,” Hayslett says. “Use a little vinegar during the rinse cycle to keep towels soft.” If your bath towels feel scratchy or start to smell musty despite washing, it’s time for a refresh.

To prolong the life of your towels, make sure you let them dry properly between uses to prevent mildew growth, noted Vyanca Soto of Market Studio Interiors. And you should definitely swap them out if you notice discoloration from your skincare products.

You likely spend plenty of time in your kitchen and bathroom each day. Make sure you take a few minutes to examine the grout lines around your bathtub and kitchen countertops.

“Grout breaks down over time, and you can end up with water damage to the drywall,” says Rebekah Zaveloff, cofounder and creative director at Imparfait Design Studio. “If you look and there are spots that have pulled away or deteriorated, it’s time to regrout.”

A sagging mattress is a clear sign it’s time for a new one. Mattresses lose support and can accumulate dust mites, allergens, and even mold, which is certainly not a dreamy way to slumber.

“Flip your mattress every six months [unless it's a one-sided design] to help maintain its structure and extend its lifespan,” Hayslette advised.

A mattress protector can extend its lifespan as does regular vacuuming to remove dust and debris. Soto recently bought a bed vacuum cleaner and says, “It works like a charm.” She also uses it on her dog’s bed to help it stay clean and smell fresher.

The lifespan of a pillow mainly depends on its material, whether that be foam, feathers, or another type of synthetic fill. But once a pillow of any kind loses its shape, it's time to say goodbye. Like mattresses, pillows can harbor dust mites, dead skin cells, and sweat over time. You can prolong a pillow's life with a pillow protector—Soto likes the Serta Power Chill—but you should still swap those out every year or so.

When to replace: two to three years for sheets; five to 10 years for duvets

Bed linen care and personal hygiene dictate the condition of your linens. Similar to pillows, sheets and duvets can accumulate dust mites, allergens, and bacteria, especially if you allow kids or pets into your bed.

“Over time, duvets lose their fluffiness and insulating properties due to regular use and washing,” says Rebecca Bobroff of Rebecca Bobroff Design.

If you’re prone to night sweats or use a lot of skin-care products, you may notice that your sheets become quickly discolored. Along with regular washing, you may want to stick to white sheets you can bleach over colors, which will look dingy even when they're clean.

“At a minimum, wash pillowcases once a week,” says Danielle Hurhula, head of home merchandising for Quince. “I’d recommend bumping washes up to twice a week if you suffer from allergies or asthma, sleep with hair or skincare products, or have sensitive or acne-prone skin.”

HVAC maintenance experts, including Energy Star, recommend checking filters monthly and replacing air filters every three months to help your system function properly and keep dust and allergens out of your home.

If you remove your filters and they look extra dirty, you may want to up the frequency. Doing so will not only improve indoor quality but keep your HVAC system from overworking and wasting energy, which could shave a few years off its life.

Water filters can accumulate bacteria, minerals, and other contaminants. This can affect the taste and quality of your drinking water.

“The lifespan of a filter cartridge is influenced by the level of contaminants and particulates in your water,” says Eric Roy, Ph.D., founder and chief scientist at Hydroviv. “Sink filter cartridges should be replaced approximately every six months and are designed to handle up to 720 gallons of water.”

You should also watch for a decrease in the flow rate, which indicates the filter is saturated.

When to replace: five years (or as recommended by the manufacturer)

You likely know that fridge water filters should be replaced every six months, but water lines can build up gunk or start leaking over time.

“Inspect the line for cracks or discoloration every year,” Soto suggests. “Flush it with vinegar and water every six months to prevent mineral buildup and ensure clean, clear water. My mom taught me this and when you see the color of the water when you flush it—you'll never stop doing it.”

Using sharp knives on wooden and plastic cutting boards can create grooves that embed bacteria, even after a dishwasher cycle. One study showed that plastic surfaces have the longest bacteria survival time, and scraping wooden boards with a knife significantly reduced bacterial presence.

Soto disinfects cutting boards with a vinegar solution and uses bleach for plastic boards. “Sand down wooden boards occasionally to remove deep grooves and re-oil them to maintain the wood,” she says.

Sponges harbor bacteria, especially when you use them to clean up raw food such as eggs and meat. In fact, salmonella can thrive on a sponge for more than seven days. Hayslette’s tip: Microwave damp sponges for one minute to sanitize between uses, but toss them after two weeks max.

Keep sponges as dry as possible, or better yet, replace them with dish brushes as these are less likely to breed bacteria, according to the International Journal of Food Microbiology. We like the Curio dish brush because it stands upright on a magnetic pedestal and you can replace the brush heads.

When to replace: three to five years (or sooner if scratched)

The nonstick coating on your cookware can wear off or become damaged over time. This decreases a pan's efficacy and also makes it a health hazard—the coating may start to flake off into your food.

“To extend their life, always use nonmetal utensils and hand-wash your pans instead of placing them in the dishwasher,” Hayslette says.

Not only do latex anti-slip rug pads lose cushioning over time, but they can also start to disintegrate.

“They get tacky and sticky on the floor, which can damage the floor's surface,” Zaveloff explains. Pull up your rug once in a while and if you see that the rug pads are turning dark yellow, start shopping for new ones.

#6920. Netflix Is Stepping Onto the Ice With an Icebreaker Adaptation—Here’s Everything We Know

Hockey romances aren’t just dominating bookshelves anymore; they’ve become television’s newest obsession.

Following the breakout success of recent sports romances like Heated Rivalry and Off Campus, Netflix is officially joining the trend with a series adaptation of Hannah Grace’s bestselling novel Icebreaker. The BookTok sensation, which helped introduce a new generation of readers to the hockey romance genre, is headed to the streamer as a college-set drama centered on a figure skater and a hockey player whose ambitions—and attraction—begin to collide.

Here’s everything we know about Icebreaker so far.

Yes. Netflix announced on June 15 that it has greenlit a television adaptation of Hannah Grace’s novel Icebreaker.

The project was unveiled during the Banff World Media Festival by Jinny Howe, Netflix’s head of scripted series for the U.S. and Canada.

In a statement, Howe said, “Netflix has a long history of turning beloved romance novels into massive global hits. We know our members can’t get enough of these stories, which makes Icebreaker the perfect next chapter for us.”

She added, “By supercharging the hockey sports romance genre, Icebreaker has ignited a passionate fandom and helped redefine the landscape of contemporary romance. We are thrilled to partner with Jade Bartlett, Amanda Lasher, and the team at Unwell Productions to bring this irresistible love story to life.”

The series follows Anastasia Allen, a competitive figure skater focused on reaching the Olympics, and Nate Hawkins, a star hockey player with dreams of going professional.

Their paths cross when an accident forces their teams to share the same rink, creating tension both on and off the ice.

Netflix’s official synopsis reads, “She’s the transfer gunning for Olympic gold. He’s the campus star chasing the NHL. But when an accident forces their teams to share one rink, Anastasia Allen and Nate Hawkins collide—and discipline gives way to obsession, because the only thing more dangerous than wanting to win is wanting each other.”

The adaptation will be set in the same college sports world that readers first fell in love with in Grace’s novel, blending romance, competition, and the pressures of elite athletics.

Yes. The show is based on Grace’s 2022 novel of the same name.

As the first book in Grace’s Maple Hills series, the novel’s popularity eventually expanded into a larger franchise, with follow-up books Wildfire and Daydream taking place in the same university setting.

Amanda Lasher has signed on as showrunner, writer, and executive producer. Her previous television credits include Gossip Girl (2008–2012), Riverdale (2017–2018), and The Bold Type (2018–2019).

Jade Bartlett developed the adaptation and wrote the pilot. She will also write and executive produce the series alongside Lasher. Bartlett’s previous projects include Road House, Doctor Strange in the Multiverse of Madness, and Miller’s Girl.

“I love this genre, and it’s been a joy working with Jade, the team at Unwell, and Netflix to bring Hannah Grace’s beloved Icebreaker novel to the screen,” Lasher said in a statement.

Bartlett added, “It’s been such a joy to play in Hannah Grace’s world of Icebreaker, a story filled with ambitious, talented, and undeniably sexy characters. But the real special sauce is that, above all, they are good, deeply compassionate people.”

The series will be executive produced by Unwell Productions, with Alex Cooper, Matt Kaplan, and Mina Lefevre serving as executive producers.

On September 28, Deadline revealed the show’s two leads. Daisy Kelley and Connor Simos will star as Anastasia “Stassie” Allen and Nate Hawkins, respectively.

On September 29, Gabriel Tierney (Rivals, Enola Holmes 2), Auliʻi Cravalho (Moana), Peter Berry, Chaneil Kular (Sex Education), Devin Craig (Sex Lives of College Girls), and Aidan Shaw (We Were Liars, Heated Rivalry) were also announced as cast members on the show. According to Deadline, “Tierney will play Aaron; Cravalho will portray Lola; Berry is Robbie; Kular will play JJ; Craig will portray Henry; and Shaw is Russ.”

On November 11, Netflix announced that Paul Wesley (The Vampire Diaries) will also be joining the cast of Icebreaker. He’ll star as Coach Neil Faulkner in the series.

The series does not currently have a release date, but the show is currently in production in Vancouver, Canada.

#6917. Does Your Home Need a Porte-Cochère? Architects Explain When the Statement Feature Works Best

This covered entry can be both practical and seriously grand.

Few architectural features make a statement like a porte-cochère. A covered entrance gives a home a certain panache, but your house doesn’t have to look like something out of HBO’s The Gilded Age to pull it off.

The term porte-cochère translates from French as “coach door.” The feature has a highbrow history: horse-drawn carriages once pulled up beneath porte-cochères at King Louis XIV’s château. But it works just as well in the modern era.

“The easy answer as to why we love porte-cocherès is the weather,” says Utah-based designer Kristina Lawrence. “Here in the Park City mountains, a porte-cocherè gives guests a covered place to step out of the car, whether it’s snowing in January or raining in June. And when clients are entertaining, that’s something everyone appreciates.” But how do you know if this structure can actually work on your property?

“Because porte-cochères are relatively large structures, typically ranging from 18 by 22 feet to 25 by 30 feet, their scale and relationship to the main house are critical,” says Seth Ballard of Ballard + Mensua Architecture. “Without careful three-dimensional design studies, a porte-cochère can easily appear out of proportion or overwhelm the architecture.”

The last thing you want to do is rely on the size of your SUV as a guidepost. Mark Caulfield, director of Glass House Architecture, notes that this type of addition works best with Georgian, Regency, Victorian or large contemporary homes with strong horizontal lines. “The eaves should match to the roofline, and the columns should be slightly above the structural minimum to have the proper visual weight,” Caulfield explains.

Ahead, here’s when experts say adding a porte-cochère makes sense to your property.

A porte-cochère is one way to further enhance an already large home.

“Extending a roofline away from the house can help balance a tall entry and gives a spacious driveway a more balanced sense of scale and purpose,” Lawrence says of the above home. “It also creates an incredible feeling of arriving somewhere special.” Not to mention, it’s also incredibly convenient when you pull up to the house with a trunk full of luggage or ski gear, for instance.

“Add a fireplace nearby for warmth, and it’s like the welcome starts before anyone even walks through the front door,” she adds.

With the right architectural detail, perhaps an arch, passing through a porte-cochère reveals an entirely different point of view, whether it’s the focus on the home or the landscape view beyond.

“That unfolding arrival can be one of the most memorable parts of the home,” Lawrence says of another one of her projects above.

You don’t have to live in a manse to add a porte-cochère. Adding one at the entry to the kitchen or mudroom creates a functional arrival point.

“Years of neglect had erased this 1890 home’s Victorian character,” says Dan D’Agostino, founder of Plan Architecture, of the above project. “So, the clients drew on the Craftsman bungalow they grew up in to guide its new details.”

Here, D'Agostino took advantage of the natural slope to create a porte-cochère that actually works as a bridge carport to link the main house to a guest suite.

“It works as the home’s gateway,” he explains. This feature works in any climate, whether you’re escaping from rain or want to provide shade for your car during a heat wave.

Bob Zuber, a partner with Morgante Wilson Architects, created a porte-cochère that seamlessly blended into the overall architecture of the house. Creating an unobtrusive entryway facade makes this opening more like a doorway than a passage. Adding distinctive lighting and landscaping further enhances the look.

#6913. Meet the Cast and Characters of ‘The Good Daughter’

Excited to watch The Good Daughter on Peacock, but didn’t read the book by Karin Slaughter that it’s based on? Here’s everything you need to know about the cast and characters of The Good Daughter, without giving anything away. Well, maybe one thing. There’s a character who only shows up in one timeline for a reason. You might guess why, but I’m going to spoil it later just so we’re all on the same page.

First of all, no... this series is not connected to The Good Wife, The Good Fight, The Good Doctor, or The Good Place. As mentioned above, The Good Daughter is an adaptation of a bestselling novel that was published in 2017. The story is about two adult sisters who are reunited after a violent attack in their Georgia hometown brings up memories of the murder that tore them apart.

Slaughter herself adapted her book for the screen, penning the teleplays for all six episodes. Steph Green stepped in to direct and executive produce the series. As for the cast, here’s who you’ll see on The Good Daughter and who they play.

Sam, quintessential eldest daughter, left town and became a patent lawyer.

Rose Byrne’s breakout role in Hollywood was as a titular bridesmaid in Bridesmaids, though she had already appeared in films like Sunshine, Troy and Marie Antoinette at that time. That same year, 2011, she played Moira MacTaggert in X-Men: First Class and would reprise that role in a later film. She’s still best known for comedies, however, like Spy, Neighbors, and Neighbors 2 as well as horror movies like 28 Weeks Later and the Insidious films. Television-wise, Byrne played Gloria Steinem in Mrs. America and starred in both Physical and Platonic. She was nominated for an Academy Award for If I Had Legs I’d Kick You and a Tony Award for Fallen Angels.

Meanwhile, younger sister Charlie stayed in town and became a defense attorney.

Meghann Fahy was one of the breakout stars of The White Lotus’ second season, though she also hadn’t come out of nowhere. Prior to the HBO seires, Meghann made her Broadway debut in the Pulitzer Prize winning musical Next to Normal as an understudy and replacement for the character Natalie, then played Sutton on The Bold Type, and also acted in 83 episodes of One Life To Live. Since the HBO series, she has starred in the movie Drop and the Netflix series Sirens as well as The Perfect Couple and Your Monster. She is also a two time Emmy nominee, for both Sirens and The White Lotus.

Audrey Grace Marshall and Drew Cheek play Young Sam and Charlie

Flashbacks show who the sisters were before the trauma that shaped them.

Before The Good Daughter, Audrey Grace Marshall played Young Cassie on The Flight Attendent and was supposed to be in the Buffy the Vampire Slayer reboot. She did play Viv in the Fairly Oddparents live action reboot thing in 2022. Drew Cheek, on the other hand, has only one previous credit: a movie called F*ck Valentine’s Day.

Rusty is the sisters’ father and a former defense attorney with a reputation.

Brendan Gleeson is an Academy Award-nominated and Emmy Award-winning actor. He won the latter for playing Winston Churchill in the miniseries Into the Storm. You’ve seen him in the Harry Potter films, Braveheart, 28 Days Later, Gangs of New York, Troy, In Bruges, Edge of Tomorrow, Paddington 2, and The Banshees of Inisherin. Brendan has not done much television, but did lead Mr. Mercedes from 2017 to 2019, appear in the 2022 series State of the Union, and play Silvermane in Spider-Noir.

Lenore is Rusty’s secretary and the girls’ surrogate mother figure.

This is Harper Steele’s first ever professional acting credit. She is an Emmy winning comedy writer who worked at Saturday Night Live for 13 years and was a head writer for four of those years. Her other screenwriting credits include Eurovision Song Contest: The Story of Fire Saga and The Spoils of Babylon. Harper recently appeared with Will Ferrell in the road trip buddy documentary Will & Harper about their friendship before and after she came out as transgender.

Kelly is a suspect in a school shooting who Charlie chooses to represent.

Shayna Bernardo’s previous credits before The Good Daughter include the Lifetime movie Don’t Sweat The Small Stuff, an episode of Doom Patrol, and the horror movie Sorority Secrets.

Judith is an English teacher at a local middle school.

Paula Malcomson is probably best known for playing Katniss’ understandably checked-out mother Asterid Everdeen in The Hunger Games movies. She was also a series regular on the Battlestar Galactica prequel series Caprica, as well as Deadwood and Ray Donovan. She also recurred on Sons of Anarchy.

Huck is a teacher at the local middle school with a grudge against Rusty.

Jake McDorman is best known for leading roles in shows like Quintuplets, Greek, Manhattan Love Story, Limitless, Murphy Brown, The Right Stuff, Mrs. Davis and Dopesick. He also had a recurring role on Shameless. Movie-wise, you’ve seen him in Happiest Season, Lady Bird, American Sniper, Live Free Or Die Hard, Bring It On: All Or Nothing, and, of course, Aquamarine.

Olivia Williams plays Harriet ‘Gamma’ Quinn

First of all, no, this Harriet was the sisters’ mother and a genius physicist. Spoiler alert: she was violently murdered when Sam and Charlie were young girls.

You may know Olivia Williams from films like Rushmore, the 2003 Peter Pan, Anna Karenina, and An Education. Fun fact: Williams played Moira MacTaggert in X-Men: The Last Stand. She played Camilla Parker-Bowles on two seasons of The Crown and her other television credits include Dollhouse, Manhattan, and Dune: Prophecy. She’s also a prolific stage actress and starred as Nurse Ratched in One Flew Over The Cuckoo’s Nest at London’s Old Vic Theatre this year.

Ben is Charlie’s estranged husband, and also a lawyer. So many lawyers!

Michael Dorman is an Australian actor with many starring roles on television to his name, including shows like Wonderland, For All Mankind, Patriot, Territory, and Joe Pickett. His film credits include Leigh Whannell’s The Invisible Man and Pirates of the Caribbean: Dead Men Tell No Tales.

#6912. You Don’t Need a Smartwatch. You Need This $55 Timex.

The Ironman is a no-fuss watch that’ll help you get in shape without overcomplicating it.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Welcome to the Esquire Endorsement. Heavily researched. Thoroughly vetted. These picks are the best way to spend your hard-earned cash.

I’m not trying to run a marathon here. I just want to feel strong, feel healthy in my normal life. In fact, a flight of stairs was my tipping point. It was midsummer. I hit a jog up the stairs—for fun, just to move—coming out of my usual subway stop below the Esquire offices in Manhattan. Two long flights, maybe 50 steps total. I got to the top, step onto the street, and ... what the hell. I was gassed. Hands on my knees on a public sidewalk, gassed. I desperately needed to get back in the gym.

Let me clarify before friends and family text me to call bullshit, I had not let myself go; I was just a little out of shape. No one could tell, except for my older brother—he has been my tiger mom since I was born—who made sure to tell me after he invited my wife and me out for a Fire Island beach day. He could tell, and my lungs could tell—that’s what matters. Prior to that 30-second jog, I’d tried all the smart fitness trackers that are meant to game you into working out. None stuck. Life was too busy, my willpower was too weak. I wasn’t hitting the gym.

Then I bought a Timex Ironman. My thought, a stroke of genius in hindsight, was to have it within reach as much as possible. My workouts got idiot-proof, simple beyond belief: Manchester United’s 20-minute sprint test, kettlebell circuits, push-up and pull-up splits, or some form of lower-body lifting. Am I in the best shape of my life? No. But I could run a 5K right now, no problem. I’m back to basics and in good-enough shape. That’s all because of this watch.

If you are one of those high-achieving men that feels the need to run marathons, hike ultras, or cycle hundreds of miles, your qualm with a Timex Ironman won’t be the durability. “Ironman” does, in fact, come from the idea that this would be your companion over 140 miles of swim-bike-run. It’s water-resistant to 100 meters, plenty for swimming laps. It has a chronograph, alarm, and timer—the only things you actually need to track a workout. Want to know if your VO2 max is getting better? Or your lifts are paying off? Track numbers: Splits get faster, reps get higher. You don’t need all the heart-rate stuff.

And, crucially, there isn’t much to break down on the inside.

But suppose your Timex Ironman does break down. Unless you got an anomaly or wear it into literal war, this shouldn’t happen for about a decade; my dad has owned one Ironman since before I was born in the 90s. Even if you only hit five to seven years, that’s longer than a smartwatch lasts before getting phased out because of software improvements. That’s a $500 replacement. An Ironman is a tenth of that.

It’s also worth saying that I do not have an issue with smartwatches or fitness trackers; they are truly revolutionary when it comes to catching hidden health problems. And if it’s your jam to track steps, count calories, and see little rings that tell you about VO2 max and sleep scores—by all means, do what works for you. But that line of thinking just does not work on me. A screen on my wrist means I’m constantly fiddling with my playlist or thinking about the text messages and emails just on the other side of Do Not Disturb. None of that is what I need when working out.

Since switching over to my Ironman, I’ve found myself not even having my phone on me during a workout. I’ve gone full Luddite—something I always laughed at in the gym but now have huge respect for. Everything becomes harder without a workout playlist, but it also becomes more zen. On a run, you listen to your feet on the pavement. During a lift, you listen to your breathing. You get a lot more in tune with your body.

This, however, is the real crux of my argument. The little smartwatch tracking rings don’t do it for me, and nothing pisses me off more than a notification badge. It’s a nerd with a stat sheet saying, “Well, if you observe last night’s sleep score and this morning’s use of caffeine, you’ll see it’s a great time to get in the gym,” while I’m sitting at the beach reading a book on vacation. I don’t want to be told when to work out or how hard to workout by someone’s algorithm.

What I want is something that sparks my internal motivation. For that, I keep the Ironman where I’ll have to see it every single day. At home, it’s on top of my phone when I wake up in the morning—that’s one time when I can hit the park for a few pull-ups or knock out 15 minutes of pushups in the kitchen. If I miss that, it has to go in my work bag, so that when I drop my laptop in, I see it and make a decision whether or not to hit the gym on the way home. When I travel, it’s on the hotel nightstand.

Reader, let me tell you how much sweeter it feels when you know it was your willpower, not a notification, that got you to move around. I’ve done midnight sprints in a hotel gym, 6 a.m. jump rope on the sidewalk, 6 p.m. squats in the gym. There are times where I go too hard or too light, but I’m learning to listen to my body—not an app—for cues on when it’s ready, and if I skip a workout for drinks with friends or a movie with my wife, I don’t beat myself up. It’s a little part of my brain, my soul that I haven’t given over to the tech overlords. All I needed to get in shape was a good watch.

#6908. The Transformative Experience of Buying Japanese Denim in Japan

I was never a big jeans guy. After a recent trip to Tokyo, I think I’m changed forever.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

A ponytailed man in head-to-toe denim orbits me a third, then a fourth time in quick succession, like his eyes are twin asteroids and my waist a powerful gravitational force. When he stops, suddenly, he calls out, “Thirty-two!” and asks, “Button or zipper?”

I should’ve expected this sort of wizardry when I entered Momotaro Jeans Aoyama, a Tokyo outlet of the highly esteemed Japanese denim purveyor. Though it’s housed on the second floor of a mall just a three-quarter-mile walk from overstimulating, people-packed Shibuya, this tiny shop feels tranquil, purposeful, reverent. Dark indigo pants are folded and piled neatly in wooden cubbies along the walls; thick jackets hang gently from floating racks. Such a scene differs greatly from my earliest jeans-shopping memory.

In my early teens, on an Easter Sunday trip into New York, my mother shooed me into the Times Square Levi’s to buy my first big-boy pair. I never wore jeans. I attended Catholic school for much of my youth, so most mornings I was stuffed into stiff, ugly khakis. In rebellion against forced formality, I’d sport sweatpants or basketball shorts outside school hours.

Naturally, this appalled my fashionable mother, who couldn’t have lived with herself if she dropped her teenage son off at coed social outings wearing swishy Adidas track pants. But why was her remedy to hit a retail store in the busiest neighborhood in America on a holiday weekend? Trying on fur coats in hell would be comparably pleasant.

We dodged shoppers yanking slim cuts off floor displays and dug through unkempt mounds of unfolded denim until Mom found a few sizes in a few washes and forced me into the back changing room to try ’em all on. Of course, I huffed and puffed. Of course, I whinged and complained. But I left with a pair of medium-wash straight legs that at least wouldn’t look embarrassing on a first date.

In the nearly two decades since, as my interest in menswear has graduated from begrudging to professional, mass-market clothing has plunged in quality but spiked in price. Consumers are faced with a harsh reality when buying new: Either replace your decaying polyester-blend garments every few years or buy pricier, sturdier stuff from reputable manufacturers. I diverted toward the latter path a few years ago when I purchased a $150 heavyweight cotton hoodie from American Giant. (Though that may not seem like much to an Esquire reader, it was quite the jump for a J. Crew Factory sales-rack goblin like me.)

I learned of raw, selvedge denim a year later, when a buddy who’s into menswear sent me a Reddit post depicting a pair of jeans standing up all on their own. This was the beauty of raw Japanese denim, my friend told me, which is manufactured in the spirit of heritage American workwear from a time when durability was paramount. Heavyweight. Premium dye. Selvedge, meaning it’s woven on old-school looms from high-quality cotton. (You can also buy cheaper, significantly less impressive Japanese denim, but we’re talking about the reputable specialists here.) I was intrigued until he mentioned how many hundreds of dollars they cost thanks to import fees and general U.S. financial fuckery. But if I were ever on a trip to Japan at a time when the U.S. dollar was stronger than the yen? Maybe I could swing a pair.

In September, I was invited on a work trip to Tokyo, and finally my chance presented itself. When I landed at Narita International Airport, all I could think about was trying on stiff, uncomfortable jeans—talk about a different mindset from that of teenage me in Times Square.

After a week of tight itineraries, I finally sneaked away on my very last day and made my way past the stanchions lining the perimeter of the Momotaro shop. I read online that some folks wait up to 30 minutes to enter; luckily, there is no queue when I arrive.

A handful of employees stand silently behind a broad counter, one of them the man with the ponytail, who approaches me when he sees me checking out the brand’s #100 Standard Straight jeans in 14.7-ounce denim, which is a measurement of weight per square yard of fabric. Instead of asking for my size, he does the circling bit described earlier. I reply to his follow-up question with “zipper,” and he shows me to a narrow changing room toward the back of the space and asks me to remove my shoes. Japanese etiquette. He arrives with two pairs of the very jeans I was checking out. I try them both on, but they don’t feel quite right.

“Anything thicker?” I ask, trying to overcome the language barrier between us. My Japanese is rudimentary; his English is solid in bursts but not completely fluent.

He returns with a straight-cut pair from Momotaro’s classic line that’s 15.7 ounces, but it has a button fly, which I struggle mightily to close. Alas, there are no zippered pairs of this kind left. Yet, of the three, these fit the best around my waist. I’m puzzled until I look closely at the tags. These classic button flies are a 32, but when I return to the standard zippers, I notice he has accidentally brought me a 31—way, way too tight in the crotch—and a 33, a hair too loose.

His eyes widen apologetically when I point out the error, and he rushes back to the stocks to ensure I get a 32 in my hands. They fit absolutely perfectly. Done and done. He’s relieved.

And just like that, I’m ready to walk out a satisfied customer. This is how I’m used to buying jeans, after all. You try ’em, you like ’em, you buy ’em, and if you get home and they’re not quite what you thought they were, you return ’em. But as I will soon learn, there’s more to it at Momotaro. My guy has me put them back on and step out once more.

“Baggy or not baggy?” he asks of how I’d like my jeans to appear.

In front of a mirror, he squats down and folds the bottoms of the legs up to just about my ankle line and slides a transparent clip over the surface to mark its place. An in-house tailor will hem them for me. I change back into my other clothes, and he explains as best he can how to properly take care of my jeans. I shouldn’t wash them too often, but when I do, I should fasten everything and turn them inside out. Oh, and keep them separate from other clothes, because the indigo will definitely run. That’s part of the fun; the jeans are designed to fade handsomely over time based on how and when I wear them. A personalized pair, if you will. Don’t ever dry them in a machine, though. Take ’em out, smooth the creases, and hang to dry in a cool, dark place.

He hands me a little box with a lifetime-guarantee card and explains that if I’m ever back in Japan and need a repair, they’ve got me covered.

A second employee guides me across the hall to a second storefront, where payment and hemming take place. After I show my passport to receive the 10 percent tax reduction for tourists, I swipe my card. $211. That’s it?! By shopping here, I essentially chop a third off the price listed on the Momotaro U.S. website. I’m told to return in 15 or so minutes once the hemming is done, so I dash to a café downstairs for a matcha over which I reflect on my experience.

Whenever I buy anything over a Benjamin, I immediately get buyer’s remorse. That American Giant hoodie? I spent a good few days lamenting that the sleeves were slightly too long before I talked myself out of a return. And yet, when I pick up my Momotaro jeans once they’re ready—they’re bagged neatly and wrapped in plastic to protect them from the day’s ceaseless rain—I feel beyond cared for. When I finally return home and put them on, I don’t fear that I maybe should have purchased a smaller size in case they stretch—they’re not supposed to—because my jeans fit perfectly. They’re right in proportion with my body, sit higher than I’m used to but quite comfortably, and style well with a graphic tee and light jacket.

Maybe I’m shopping at the wrong stores at home. Maybe I need to raise my price floor to get that kind of service. Or maybe we’ve lost something here in the U.S. that’s now attainable only overseas. Maybe I need to get back on a plane to Tokyo ASAP and hit all the stores I missed—Hinoya, Japan Blue, the Flat Head—then take the shinkansen to Osaka and get myself some really thick boys from Iron Heart, where 25-ounce denim is the gold standard.

Or maybe I should just enjoy the jeans I have now, wear them in nicely over the next decade until they’re washed to sky blue and softer than a lamb’s ear. I can look forward to passing them down to whatever kids I have one day. It’ll save us a trip to the Times Square Levi’s.

#6905. The Best Early Black Friday Apple Deals, According to Our Editors

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

The latest AirTags, MacBooks, and AirPods are up to 23 percent off.

Our editors look forward to rare discounts on Apple devices every Black Friday, and this year is no exception. Leading up to Amazon’s official sale (which runs from November 19 to 30), we’re already seeing up to 23 percent off Apple’s most coveted tech.

The items we deem worthy of shopping right away? AirTag tracking devices are on sale for their lowest price ever, and select AirPods headphones like the Pro 3 and Max 2 are up to 20 percent off. That said, discounts on iPads and Apple Watches are worth waiting a little longer for (save for a record-low price on the Watch Ultra 4), but we expect better deals once the official sale begins.

All to say, if you want to get a head start on shopping, there are plenty of options available now. Ahead, see what we’re adding to our carts from Apple this Black Friday.

Lost luggage is a thing of the past with Apple’s ingenious AirTags, which give you peace of mind by tracking the location of your prized possessions. Snag one for a record-low price of $23 ahead of Black Friday.

Apple’s AirPods Pro are worth the hype. Complete with features like Active Noise Cancellation and transparency modes, a water-resistant design, and silicone ear tips for a customizable fit, they’re bound to become your daily headphones. Currently, they’re at a typical sale price of $199, but they’ve been as low as $169 during past sales.

Designed specifically with the athlete in mind, it features Apple’s highest-tech heart rate sensor, a full suite of health and wellness metrics, a GPS monitor, and water resistance up to 100 meters—making it the perfect gift. It just returned to a record-low price of $700, and it could drop even further on Black Friday.

Everyone deserves a pair of headphones that are as chic as they are functional. If you prefer an over-ear style, add this highly covetable pair to your cart, as they tend to sell out quickly when they’re on sale. We expect even steeper savings once Black Friday arrives, but you’ve got a better chance of securing a pair if you shop now.

Updated with an ultra-quick M5 chip, this computer boasts enhanced graphics and a speedier performance than past models. Plus, it boasts a Liquid Retina display for crystal-clear imagery, an all-day battery, and built-in AI tech.

Ahead of Black Friday, the standard iPad is $49 off. Though this is an entry-level option, it boasts features like Touch ID, a 10-hour battery life, and an impressive 11.2-inch Liquid Retina display.

Whether you’re new to the world of wearables or need to upgrade an older device, the Apple Watch SE makes it easy to do so without breaking the bank. It’s not on sale yet, but it was at a record-low price of $199 in July, so we’re expecting a similar price once November 27 comes.

Don’t let this tablet’s tiny size fool you: This is the perfect device for watching Netflix on the go, devouring your latest literary fiction read, or jotting down notes during flights. It’s only $30 off right now, but it was on sale for a record-low price of $349 last Black Friday.

The perfect companion to the Apple iPad, the Pencil Pro allows for seamless note-taking, sketching, and coloring. Just like a real pencil, this updated model is equipped with angle and pressure sensitivity for precise lines.

If you haven’t jumped over to team Wireless Charging, now’s the time to do so. Not only does this MagSafe charger juice up your phone quickly, but it’s longer than your average charging cord. What’s more, it’s also compatible with your AirPods, letting it serve dual purposes on your nightstand.

This case turns your iPad into a laptop while also giving your investment some welcome protection. It comes complete with a smooth Apple keyboard and trackpad.

#6904. Actually, Robert Pattinson Would Make a Perfect James Bond

My colleagues and editors are sick of hearing me say it, but I believe it with my whole chest: The actor playing James Bond should never be more famous than James Bond. There’s something about Bond—his cultural footprint and the dangerous yet seductive life he leads in his stories—that makes him too big to be a footnote in a great actor’s résumé. James Bond creates leading men; James Bond is not for leading men to create.

So you have to know that it’s not easy for me to say this: Robert Pattinson would be fantastic and exactly the new James Bond we’re looking for.

You’ve probably heard by now that Amazon, which acquired full creative control over all things Bond from longtime owners Eon Productions and the Broccoli family, has been searching for the next 007 in a movie to be directed by Denis Villeneuve.

What was once an arena of speculation has exploded into a circus, with different names being bandied about at once—namely Callum Turner, Josh O’Connor, and Jack Lowden. (There is obviously a specific type of white man they’re going for here.) Just when it seemed as if the new Bond would finally be unveiled, there came a rush of two really big names suddenly in the running. Among them is Pattinson, reported by The Hollywood Reporter on November 6, along with Nicholas Hoult. Pattinson secretly auditioned for the role during a break in filming for The Batman: Part II, which halted filming in October when Matt Reeves attended to a personal matter.

No shade to Hoult or any of the other actors, but Pattinson is now the most interesting candidate despite a whiff of overexposure this year. Between The Drama in the spring, The Odyssey in the summer, Primetime in the fall, and soon Dune: Part Three in the dead of winter, 2026 has been shaping up into a banner year for Pattinson. Not since Twilight has the man been so prolific on our screens.

But even before the year began, Pattinson had long ago outgrown his image of a tween heartthrob. He’s a prodigious talent capable of daring choices and executing on them in a way you can’t ever expect. (Who else would say cock like this?) His popular line from The Odyssey—“Somebody get these beggars out of here!”—didn’t become a meme on its own. Pattinson made beggars sound like a slur, and in the same breath, his nervous blinking and quivering smile cemented Antinous as an unconfident weasel whose puffed-out chest was, in fact, filled with hot air. Unlike previous Batman actors, who often played the Caped Crusader like a knight in matte black armor, Pattinson’s interpretation was/is that of an antisocial weirdo everyone stares at like a freak. Which he is.

Like Batman, Bond has also been a role that people misunderstand, sometimes even by the men who play him. (It is not my biases talking here; I really do think Daniel Craig is the only actor to ever nail the nuances and pathology of the character.) Bond is stylish and can say the right thing at the right time, be it to enemies or lovers. But he’s not a dashing or noble hero. He’s a brute in Brunello Cucinelli. A thug with a $9,000 Omega. He literally kills for a living—and had to kill to get that right—and his attachment to his job is downright destabilizing.

Recall in Skyfall, after Judi Dench’s M orders Moneypenny to shoot, Bond copes with endless drinking, womanizing, and gambling. That might sound like a fun weekend in Vegas, but in the context of Bond, his fallout from discovering his expendability led him to a really, really dark path. It is not normal to drink shots with a scorpion on your hand, nor is it healthy to be up in the morning reaching for booze from behind the bar. Bond might live a life many of us men dream of, but it comes at a severe cost. I don’t know what kind of Bond movie Villeneuve has in mind, but if the creative team is lining up like this, we’re in for something special.

You can’t exactly guesstimate what kind of 007 Pattinson might be based on his previous roles. There is truly little common ground between Batman and Bond. But one thing is for sure: If Pattinson gets the part, we are in for a goddamn show.
