#6898. Absolutely Everything You Need to Know About the Citizen Watch Catalog

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

The Japanese brand offers endless variety, appealing affordability, and incredible technological innovation.

Whether you encounter them in multi-brand boutiques and stand-alone stores, in duty-free shops while on vacation, on the Internet, or at auction, Citizen’s watches are everywhere.

Founded in 1918, the brand that began with the simple objective of producing a Japanese-made watch has evolved into a truly global juggernaut, with annual turnover of approximately $2.1 billion for the fiscal year that ended in March 2026. Vertically integrated, it produces everything from mechanical divers of the traditional ilk to tech-forward, satellite-guided modern tool watches that update time zones instantly as one zigzags across the world. Its light-powered Eco-Drive technology essentially constitutes a movement category in its own right, while its wafer-thin Eco-Drive One models—some of the brand’s highest-end wares—command luxury prices.

Like Seiko—another homegrown Japanese brand—Citizen’s considerable scale and power has compounded over more than a century. And also like Seiko, it has grown its manufacturing capabilities to such an extent that it has successfully expanded into industries other than watchmaking. But where Citizen truly shines is in offering a remarkably capable and robust product at a price that elicits few complaints from the watch-buying public. Available in points of sale in roughly 130 countries—500 alone in the U.S.—Citizen’s timepieces are not only affordable but also truly ubiquitous.

For someone in search of a relatively inexpensive watch—any type of watch, really, save perhaps a high complication in precious metal with a dial made by a lone monk living in the Swiss Jura—that will hold up under adverse conditions, Citizen is a natural answer. Whether you’re looking for a feature-laden dive watch (Promaster Aqualand), an elegant, ultrathin dress watch (Eco-Drive One), a luxurious, design-forward everyday watch (Series 8), or a vintage-inspired take on a Genta-esque design (Zenshin), there’s a good chance that there’s a Citizen timepiece that will satisfy your desires.

The only issue is parsing the firm’s gigantic catalog in search of an ideal choice. To help you in this regard, we produced this handy guide.

While not as old as Seiko, Citizen has a long and storied watchmaking history filled with a staggering amount of technological innovation. In fact, it wouldn’t be too bold to claim that in a world based primarily on centuries-old technology, Citizen is one of the most forward-thinking marques.

The brand’s story begins in 1918, when entrepreneur Kamekichi Yamazaki established the Shokosha Watch Research Institute in Tokyo with the goal of providing a domestically produced alternative to imported Swiss and American watches. By 1924, it had produced its first pocket watch, which it named “Citizen” at the behest of Tokyo mayor Count Shinpei Goto, who imagined a watch available to ordinary members of society. Six years later, the Shokosha Watch Research Institute was officially incorporated as the Citizen Watch Company and began producing wristwatches in 1931.

In 1936, the company established its Tanashi factory in Tokyo, allowing it to significantly expand its industrial capacity and begin exporting watches to Southeast Asia, China, and elsewhere. By 1941, it established a machine-tool business that allowed it to move beyond watchmaking and that still exists to this day. Postwar expansion continued at a dizzying rate, with Citizen developing shock-resistant Parashock technology in 1956 (which it demonstrated by dropping watches out of a helicopter). In 1959, it debuted the Parawater, Japan’s first fully waterproof watch, and in 1962 came the Diamond Flake, an ultrathin model equipped with a hand-wound movement.

By the 1960s, Citizen began experimenting with technologies that would come to define its place in the contemporary watch market: First, in 1966, came the X-8, the country’s first electronic wristwatch, followed in 1970 by the X-8 Chronometer, which holds the distinction of being the world’s very first titanium wristwatch. And while the brand wasn’t the first to embrace quartz technology, it did so with vigor by 1973, introducing the Crystron Mega in 1975, which was accurate to an astonishing ±3 seconds per year.

The technology that would eventually evolve into Eco-Drive—the brand’s signature system that converts ambient light into electrical energy, thus reducing the frequency of battery replacements—debuted in 1976 with the advent of its Crystron Solar Cell, the world’s first light-powered, analog-dial quartz watch. Then, in 1982, Citizen released the Professional Diver, which boasted an incredible-for-the-era 1,300 meters of water resistance; it followed up three years later with the Aqualand, the first dive watch with an onboard electronic depth gauge. In 1989, it gathered its diverse sports watch offerings together within the Promaster line.

Innovation continued throughout the 1990s, with Citizen introducing the world’s first multi-zone radio-controlled wristwatch in 1993. In 2011, it debuted the Eco-Drive Satellite Wave, a watch that synchronized with satellites to provide hyper-accurate timekeeping across different time zones. Five years later, in 2016, the Eco-Drive One hit the market with a light-powered movement just 1mm thick, building upon ultrathin offerings from the brand’s back catalog. Then, in 2019, it brought forth the Eco-Drive Caliber 0100, a movement with stunning accuracy of ±1 second per year.

Today, Citizen Group is more than just Citizen, comprising movement maker Miyota, the aforementioned precision machinery business, watch brands Bulova and Frédérique Constant, and much more. It’s a vertically integrated behemoth with more than 12,000 employees worldwide. Founded over a century ago, it’s grown into one of the most important brands within the watchmaking sphere—and beyond.

Like fellow Japanese watchmaker Seiko, Citizen produces such a dizzying array of timepieces that it’s nearly impossible to categorize them in any definitive manner. Many modern collections overlap in remit with older historical collections that are still kicking around, while certain collections are available only in certain markets. (Some frankly defy categorization entirely, and others should probably be cut from the brand’s catalog.)

What follows, therefore, is a helpful (albeit admittedly non-exhaustive) list of Citizen’s most important and recognizable product families, delineated with the understanding that certain watches—including several dedicated ladies collections from the Japanese domestic market—will invariably be left out. Still, the following rough descriptions should help orient the new (or seasoned!) collector.

The Rolan Collection is an excellent place to search for a highly affordable, versatile everyday watch. Using Citizen’s signature Eco-Drive technology, each model is easily powered by ambient light and virtually never requires a battery change. While certain references are paired to matching metal bracelets, others come on stylish straps, and all take their aesthetic inspiration from classic mid-century horological tropes. Simple and handsome, the Rolan Collection watches are perfect for someone in search of their first “nice” watch.

The ref. AW2110-06E is everything you need in a dress/everyday watch and nothing you don’t. Measuring 41mm in brushed stainless steel, it features a radially brushed black sector dial with applied indices and a luminous dauphine handset. Paired to a black leather strap with stainless-steel hardware and powered by Eco-Drive technology, it’s a timepiece that could conceivably be paired with just about anything. Moreover, at just a hair over $300, it makes a highly affordable gift for a recent graduate who needs a solid timepiece to wear on an interview or to a first job.

Notable Reference: AW2110-06EType: DressDiameter: 41mmMovement: Citizen cal. J830 Eco-DrivePrice: $296.25MORE INFO HERE

The Garrison Collection contains military-inspired field watches and chronographs in a variety of styles, most of which are widely available in the United States both online and at authorized dealers. Powered by Eco-Drive technology, they’re reliable, good-looking pieces on bracelets or straps that all retail for less than $500, making them—like their counterparts in the dressier Rolan collection—excellent gifting options. For someone who’s after mil-spec looks but who doesn’t want to shell out for a vintage watch, the Garrison is a perfect choice.

The ref. BM8180-03E is ubiquitous at Citizen points of sale and neatly encapsulates the brand’s ethos. Measuring a comfortable 37mm in brushed stainless steel, it features a black dial with luminous Arabic indices, an outer five-minute track, and a day-date display at three o’clock. Powered by Eco-Drive, highly legible, and wildly affordable, it still boasts 100 meters of water resistance and a two-piece green fabric strap for less than $300. Looking like an issued military watch from the 1960s, it’s ideal for outdoor use while hiking or on vacation

Notable Reference: BM8180-03EType: FieldDiameter: 37mmMovement: Citizen cal. E100 Eco-DrivePrice: $221.25MORE INFO HERE

Established in 1989, the Promaster Collection is where Citizen parks its most formidable tool watches. Organized into Marine, Sky, and Land subcategories, they localize many of the notable innovations that the brand developed over the past 60-plus years, from the early Parawater’s water resistance to the Aqualand’s electronic depth gauge to the Navihawk’s multifunction timekeeping and more. Today the collection encompasses everything from mechanical divers to radio-controlled pilot’s watches to exploration-oriented field watches.

Though Citizen is famous for its Eco-Drive technology, it’s worth noting that the brand has a long history of crafting hardcore mechanical tool watches. The modern Promaster Fujitsubo ref. NB6026-56L—a limited edition based upon a vintage 1977 diver found still ticking on an Australian beach in 1983—is one such timepiece. Crafted from Super Titanium with a platinum DLC coating, it features a marine-blue bezel and dial, a matching triple-link bracelet, 200 meters of water resistance, and an automatic movement with a power reserve of 42 hours.

Notable Reference: NB6026-56LType: DiverDiameter: 41mmMovement: Citizen cal. 9051 automaticPrice: $1,195MORE INFO HERE

Launched in 2008, discontinued in 2014, and relaunched in 2021, the Series 8 Collection is based upon the idea of an angular, eight-part case design containing a mechanical movement. Though the collection has expanded somewhat beyond its original aesthetic remit, it still emphasizes the idea of a contemporary mechanical offering with clean lines and bold geometric forms. Comprising several model families, the Series 8 features integrated-style bracelets, textured dials, and enhanced resistance to magnetic fields of up to 16,000 A/m.

Executed in a tasteful two-tone colorway, the Series 8 831 ref. NB6059-57P offers outsize value from a variety of angles. There’s its 40mm stainless-steel case with surprisingly sophisticated geometry. But there’s also its handsome Champagne dial with rose-gold-tone furniture, its caliber 9051 automatic movement, its tapered H-link bracelet with brushed and finished surfaces, and its 100 meters of water resistance. For someone looking for a handsome and versatile everyday watch that can be worn to the office or to the beach, it’s an excellent, relatively affordable option.

Notable Reference: NB6059-57PType: Everyday Diameter: 40mmMovement: Citizen cal. 9051 automaticPrice: $1,125MORE INFO HERE

Dating to 1987, the Attessa Collection focuses on the marriage of advanced materials with technologies such as Eco-Drive, radio control, and GPS timekeeping. Building upon Citizen’s innovations with titanium from 1970’s X-8 Chronometer, it offers lightweight, function-forward watches in a variety of categories. Sporty, practical, and good-looking, the collection’s tool watches ditch classically inspired looks in favor of aesthetics that balance practical functionality with sleek lines, subdued materials, and elaborate case finishing.

The Attesa F950 ref. CC4107-80H encapsulates many of the technologies that make Citizen’s wares stand out in an extremely crowded marketplace. There’s the 43.2mm Super Titanium case in dark gray DLC with recrystallized titanium accents; the Eco-Drive Satellite Wave GPS movement with time-only signal reception in under three seconds; and the multifunction display with world time, chronograph, perpetual calendar, power reserve, and numerous other features. Fueled by exposure to ambient light, it’s a futuristic tool with streaks of ’70s inspiration.

Notable Reference: CC4107-80HType: Everyday Diameter: 43.2mmMovement: Citizen cal. F950 Eco-DrivePrice: $2,195MORE INFO HERE

While Citizen has several lines that focus on mechanical watchmaking, Tsuyosa is particularly notable for combining this with stainless-steel construction, integrated-bracelet styling, colorful dials, and accessible pricing. Rather than emphasizing advanced materials or modern timekeeping tech, it uses classic 20th-century watchmaking as an inspiration, seemingly adopting the idea that simpler is better. From pastel-hued 37mm models to small-seconds designs with undulating dial patterns, it’s one of the brand’s most compelling offerings.

The Tsuyosa 37mm is truly “everything you need and nothing you don’t” in wristwatch form. Constructed from stainless steel, it features alternating brushed and polished surfaces throughout its multifaceted case and integrated multi-link bracelet, lending it a sophistication that belies its sub-$1,000 pricing. Available in multiple colors, its sunray dial with luminous indices and magnified date window is legible and direct, while its automatic caliber 8210 movement and 50 meters of water resistance offer more than enough utility for daily wear. A set-and-forget watch.

Notable Reference: NJ0200-50MType: Everyday Diameter: 37mmMovement: Citizen cal. 8210 automaticPrice: $371.25MORE INFO HERE

The Zenshin Collection brings together several compelling features in a single product family: Super Titanium (titanium construction with Duratect surface-hardening treatments), automatic or Eco-Drive movements, angular cases with integrated bracelets inspired by Citizen’s 1980s output, textured dials, sapphire crystals, 100 meters of water resistance, and relatively accessible pricing. Available in three-hand and chronograph configurations, Zenshin brings a bit more materials science than Tsuyosa but costs much less than comparable Attesa fare.

Though a 42.4mm chronograph might smack of early-2000s excess, Super Titanium construction and a comfortable integrated bracelet help keep the Zenshin Chrono under control on the wrist. Available in several colorways, the “panda” colorway ref. CA4610-85A with a textured white-silver dial and contrasting black outer tachymeter scale and chronograph registers is particularly attractive and versatile. Powered by Eco-Drive, it features a 24-hour display as well as a date window—and its sub-$600 price is tough to argue with.

Notable Reference: CA4610-85AType: Everyday Diameter: 42.4mmMovement: Citizen cal. B620 Eco-DrivePrice: $506.25MORE INFO HERE

The Citizen is Citizen’s purest expression of horological excellence. Building upon the firm’s legacy of highly accurate mechanical timekeeping in the mid-century Chronomaster Collection, it launched the collection in 1995 to emphasize its commitment to precision, simplicity, and longevity. Today it comprises both mechanical and Eco-Drive movements, marrying them with traditional Japanese craftsmanship in the form of washi paper dials, hand-dyed indigo treatments, and other decorative techniques. Wildly accurate, they’re among Citizen’s most expensive offerings.

If you’re the type of person who values accuracy above all else in a wristwatch—but wouldn’t mind great looks thrown in for good measure—then chances are the Citizen ref. AQ4100-65X might be the timepiece for you. Measuring 38.3mm in the brand’s Super Titanium, it’s powered by the Eco-Drive caliber A060, which is accurate to ±5 seconds per year. (And that innocuous-seeming date window? It’s actually a perpetual date window that doesn’t need manual updating.) Oh, and the red dial? Ultra-thin Tosa Tengujoshi paper. (Hence the price.)

Notable Reference: AQ4100-65XType: Dress/everyday Diameter: 38.3mmMovement: Citizen cal. A060 Eco-DrivePrice: $2,995MORE INFO HERE

If the Citizen Collection is all about accuracy, then the Eco-Drive One Collection is about combining that accuracy—achieved via the brand’s signature Eco-Drive technology—with mind-bogglingly thin case architecture and precision manufacturing. Launched in 2016 to coincide with the 40th anniversary of Eco-Drive, the lineup features several watches powered by the caliber 8826, which was introduced as the world’s thinnest light-powered movement at just 1mm tall. When it’s combined with Super Titanium and other advanced tech, these pieces are truly remarkable.

Not only is the Eco-Drive One a spectacularly thin timepiece at just 4.5mm tall, but it’s also a remarkably handsome dress watch. Measuring 36.6mm wide in stainless steel, it features a flat gray dial with a contrasting dark-gray minute track, applied gold-tone indices with a matching dauphine handset, and a dark-gray sub-seconds display above six o’clock. That its crown is tiny shouldn’t be much of a concern—the caliber 8845 Eco-Drive is so accurate that you’ll hardly ever need to adjust it. (Unless you change time zones, that is.) Truly a one-and-done dress watch.

Notable Reference: AQ5010-01AType: DressDiameter: 36.6mmMovement: Citizen cal. 8845 Eco-DrivePrice: $2,395MORE INFO HERE

A limited-edition offering celebrating 50 years of Eco-Drive technology, the Eco-Drive Photon Collection combines highly geometric case shapes with dials inspired by the physics of light transmission. Currently available in two references, each features a slit dial that dynamically shifts in color as light interacts with its surface. Paired with integrated bracelets and measuring 39.6mm in Super Titanium, they’re more premium offerings than many Citizen core-catalog models but significantly less expensive than Eco-Drive One or Citizen watches.

A distinctive, contemporary take on the luxury sports watch, the Eco-Drive Photon ref. BJ6569-59X combines dark-gray and amber-yellow Super Titanium case construction to eye-catching effect. The three-dimensional dial with overlapping slits creates even more visual interest, while the caliber E036 Eco-Drive movement turns any light source into a consistent source of accurate timekeeping. Water resistant to 50 meters, the Photon is an excellent everyday option for someone who appreciates a bit of Genta-inflected styling in their wardrobe.

#6892. The Biggest Home Trends Buyers Will Want in 2027, According to New Zillow Data

Buyers want homes that feel polished, personal, and efficient.

The new year is on the horizon, and if you’re hoping to have a home that’s on trend for 2027, Zillow’s annual home trends report is a must-read. In the newly released study, the real estate marketplace analyzed the language in millions of for-sale listings to highlight the features most likely to drive the market.

In the year ahead, all signs point to a demand for a more polished aesthetic as Americans look to bring bold color and less tech into their homes.

“Digital detox is the new luxury,” says Amanda Pendleton, Zillow’s home trends expert. “Homes are getting smarter, but buyers also want tech-free zones where nothing needs charging. Taken together, this data signals that buyers value homes that are functional and efficient, but still feel polished and personal.”

Read on to discover the home trends setting the scene for 2027.

The word “cozy” may be among the most common adjectives in listings, but in the new year, folks are after a more refined look. Mentions for “elevated” homes are up by 71 percent, but buyers aren’t necessarily seeking the classic midcentury modern aesthetic. The emerging design scene favors bold expressions of painted brick, board-and-batten siding, and color-drenched interiors, all refined features that make a personal statement.

As the culture becomes increasingly entrenched in all things AI, some homebuyers are going all-in with tech immersion while others are begging to unplug. Mentions of AI are up a staggering 119 percent in listings, but homes that boast “digital detox” spaces are 34 percent more common than last year.

The market shows a decrease in the demand for sustainability features and a greater focus on home efficiency as “green” terms fall 26 percent. While you won’t see these details in listing photos, buyers are enticed by mentions of whole-home batteries, which have risen by 43 percent, water filtration systems, up by 22 percent, and radon mitigation, up by 18 percent.

While homebuyers haven’t completely abandoned the open floor plan, data shows a greater interest in defined spaces within the home as the “sandwich generation” revives the normalcy of multigenerational living. Interestingly, kitchens are not as enticing to buyers as home offices (up by 42 percent), media rooms (up by 37 percent), and nurseries (up by 9 percent) begin to demand greater focus.

This year saw an increased interest in at-home wellness features, and in 2027, the demand is only growing. Mentions of home gyms are up 18 percent, spa-inspired bathrooms have risen to 60 percent, and golf simulators are up 29 percent. Jacuzzis are fading from the design scene, replaced by cold plunge pools, with mentions rising by 44 percent.

Dogs may be man’s best friend, but cats are emerging as America’s pet, as mentions of dog parks are flat and dog-grooming stations have fallen 16 percent. Meanwhile, catios, which are screened-in outdoor spaces for cats, are appearing 32 percent more in for-sale listings.

Follow House Beautiful on Instagram and TikTok.

#6891. The Best Sci-Fi Movies of 2026 (So Far)

Before Avengers: Doomsday and Dune: Part Three compete at the box office, Godzilla returns to remind everyone why he’s called the “King of the Monsters.”

Two of the biggest sci-fi movies of the year are upon us, when Avengers: Doomsday and Dune: Part Three both hit theaters on December 18. We’ll see if either multimillion-dollar blockbuster has the juice to compete with this year’s crop—namely Project Hail Mary, which kicked off the year on a very high note for sci-fi films.

Directed by Phil Lord and Christopher Miller, the Ryan Gosling-starring astronaut film that plays like Interstellar but if Matthew McConaughey had a talking rock for a friend was easily one of the best sci-fi films of the past five years. We also saw The End of Oak Street earlier this summer, and Steven Spielberg returned to the world of aliens with Disclosure Day. Plus, Godzilla Minus Zero just stomped into theaters to remind everyone just why they call him the “King of the Monsters” right before Dunesday.

Until then, these are the best science-fiction movies of 2026.

The atomic monster returns to the screen following the success of Godzilla Minus One, as director Takashi Yamazaki sets up another stellar sequel for Godzilla to pulverize in his fight against King Gedorah. “In this series, Godzilla is a metaphor for war,” the filmmaker told Esquire. “What I wanted to do with this one is see how this family recovers and thrives after having suffered, and then see them survive another challenge.” Plus, leveling some cities with giant monster fights doesn’t hurt either.

The End of Oak Street is the best dinosaur film since Jurassic Park—and yes, I am including all the Jurassic Park sequels in that ranking. David Robert Mitchell’s sci-fi film about a neighborhood that is semi-transported back to prehistoric times stars Ewan McGregor and Anne Hathaway—in yet another great performance this year following The Odyssey—as the parents navigate their new Jumanji-like world.

Don’t let the three-hour runtime fool you, this South Korean monster film by director Na Hong-jin is the best thrill ride of the year. The plot follows police chief Ko Bum-seok as he investigates a brutally destructive monster that has seemingly emerged from the woods to terrify the port-side town of Hope Harbor.

Esquire’s Eric Francisco said it best when he described the beat-’em-up action flick based on the popular video game series as “incredibly stupid fun.” Noah Centineo and Andrew Koji star as friendly rivals Ryu and Ken alongside an oddball and eccentric cast including David Dastmalchian, Roman Reigns, Cody Rhodes, Andrew Schulz, Eric Andre, Orville Peck, 50 Cent, and Jason Momoa as the game’s various fighters. Still, amid all the chaos, it’s probably Callina Liang’s dedication to Chun-Li that shines through the brightest.

Following Barbarian and Weapons, Zach Cregger’s take on Resident Evil marks the horror director as 3-for-3 in my book. The contained yet still incredibly terrifying story follows a medical courier (Austin Abrams) on the night that a violent virus turns everyone into crazed, mutated killers. While the sci-fi/horror film definitely packs in the scares, I was still surprised how funny and accurate the film was to its video game source material. Among all the video game adaptations over the past few years, Resident Evil felt the most like actually playing the game.

One of the biggest movies of 2026 is, without question, Project Hail Mary. Based on the 2021 novel by Andy Weir, the smash hit follows a middle school science teacher (Ryan Gosling) who awakes with amnesia aboard a spacecraft light-years away from Earth. The newest movie from comedy giants Phil Lord and Christopher Miller was the right movie at the right time, its release accidentally coinciding with the Artemis II mission to the moon. That it was still playing in theaters more than a month after its release—an eternity in today’s theatrical landscape—speaks volumes about its out-of-this-world appeal.

The man who invented the modern summer blockbuster came back to theaters in 2026 with his first sci-fi in years: Disclosure Day. Emily Blunt and Josh O’Connor costar as two people who try to reveal the existence of extraterrestrial life, while the government tries to suppress the truth. It may not have been the glorious throwback to the days of E.T. and Close Encounters, but Disclosure Day gets by on career-best work by Blunt and another immaculate score by John Williams.

Alan Ritchson flexes his leading-man muscles in War Machine, a Netflix original that’s actually kinda worth watching. The Reacher star leads the movie as a staff sergeant for the Army Rangers who encounters a massively overpowered alien force during a training exercise. Sure, you’ve seen movies like this before—Predator, anyone?—but this throwback action flick is the best kind of turn-your-brain-off junk food we secretly crave.

You don’t have to be an overgrown ’80s baby to dig Masters of the Universe. This deeply affectionate reboot of the Reagan-era toy and TV franchise once again tells the story of Prince Adam (Nicholas Galitzine), who must save his fallen home of Eternia from the evil Skeletor (Jared Leto). The franchise started out as a cheap competitor to Star Wars, but the 2026 reboot is anything but a pale copy, with its dazzling visual design, snappy humor, and, most of all, a moving story about reasserting one’s place in the world.

How much you want to consider Marvel superhero movies “sci-fi” depends on you, but we safely consider radioactive spiders, mutant telepaths, and gamma-infused monsters to be firmly in the realm of science fiction. The fourth Tom Holland–led Spider-Man movie sees the web slinger working on his own until an invisible menace with the power to hijack people’s consciousness threatens everyone he still loves. It’s a box-office (Hulk) smash, but Spider-Man: Brand New Day remembers that the true story lies in the hearts of heroes.

If Guy Ritchie made Looper, it might look like Mike & Nick & Nick & Alice. Emerging out of SXSW, the Hulu exclusive follows a gangster (Vince Vaughn) who, after regretting murdering his friend (James Marsden), uses a time machine to stop his past self. There are an awful lot of action comedies nowadays, but Mike & Nick & Nick & Alice stands out from the pack with some novel twists on well-worn time-travel formulas.

Sydney Chandler (Alien: Earth) and Takehiro Hira (Giri/Haji) hit the road in this tender and emotional sci-fi road trip from first-time director Brian Tetsuro Ivie. Set in a speculative near future in which one’s consciousness can be uploaded into the cloud, Anima sees Chandler star as an unemployed engineer who drives a wealthy and terminally ill man (Hira) across Connecticut in the hopes of finding his estranged son before the end of his life. Anima premiered to rave reviews at SXSW earlier this year and may soon find wide-release distribution.

Animated children’s films about woodland creatures are usually a mixed bag. For every Bambi or The Wild Robot, you’ll find a dozen films trying to become the next Over the Hedge. Hoppers dodges the repetitive trend by magically turning a young girl into a woodland creature, and the box-office numbers prove that the gimmick has paid off so far. The film follows Mabel Tanaka, who transports her consciousness into a robot beaver. She wants to rally the animals to protect their wilderness from becoming a highway. It’s a crazy idea. But considering the way we’re headed in 2026, I’m down for anything.

This SXSW darling stars Lewis Pullman and Maya Hawke as a couple with the ability to literally shape their environment. After attending a couples-counseling session run by a set of magic wonder twins, the two on-again, off-again lovers learn that their emotions have dire effects on the world around them. The silly, video-game-like premise isn’t Wishful Thinking’s only draw, however. At its heart, director Graham Parkes’s feature debut really touches on a question we all ask ourselves. How do we change for the person we love without sacrificing what makes us who we are?

Ralph Fiennes’s Dr. Ian Kelson experiments with some real science fiction in 28 Years Later: The Bone Temple, which is one of the first zombie flicks I’ve ever seen to reckon with the idea that a cure is physically attainable. This second entry in a planned sequel trilogy to the original 28 Days Later film also features Jack O’Connell (Sinners) as a maniacal cult leader styled after Jimmy Savile—just tearing up the apocalyptic landscape with Danny Boyle’s zany, violent humor.

#6889. Princess Kate Says Nature Helped Her Heal After Her Cancer Diagnosis

“People have long sought comfort in the natural world”

It’s not very often that Princess Catherine sits down for a traditional magazine interview, but in a new story published today in Country Life, the Princess of Wales got candid about her recovery following her 2024 cancer diagnosis and subsequent chemotherapy treatment (she went into remission early last year).

“I was told in hospital that my recovery would not be linear,” Kate says. “[That] there will be good days and bad days, that it will take time.”

She adds: “What is interesting is to see the science now back up what we instinctively know and feel. Humans have always turned to nature. Whether it is the beach for healing and recovery, the countryside for peace and perspective or the mountains to find solitude, people have long sought comfort in the natural world. It is such a restorative environment and incorporating nature into someone’s recovery pathway is vital.”

This past June, Kate completed the National Three Peaks Challenge, a feat that involves climbing the three highest peaks in the United Kingdom in a matter of only 24 hours. Part of the reason she took on the challenge was to highlight “the deeper impact of serious illness and the importance of holistic healthcare.”

The royal has also been teaming up with organizations that focus on outdoor activity and mental health, like Mind Over Mountains and the Dose of Nature charity.

In the wake of these events, interviewer Robert Macfarlane spoke with Kate about how nature tied to her recovery process. Of her “good days and bad days,” Kate noted that nature demonstrates the same ebb and flow. She said, “It shows us that periods of rest, reflection, and renewal are equally as important as moments of growth.”

Kate also spoke about her longstanding love of the outdoors. “I remember from a very early age spending a lot of time in nature, playing in streams, walking in the countryside, blackberry picking or simply time alone in my own world, enjoying the simplicity of ordinary things, such as digging under the laurel bush for what I thought was treasure,” she said. “There was always a sense of freedom I felt in nature, something about being outdoors that is hard to find elsewhere.”

She went on to note how, as adults, we often lose this sense of wonderment, and she hopes to instill the same fascination with the natural world in her three kids: Prince George, 13, Princess Charlotte, 11, and Prince Louis, 8. “We are often so busy we simply forget to pause, slow down and notice the detail in life,” Kate said. “It is something I remind myself to do with my own children; to give them freedom to explore, rather than constantly telling them what to see or what to think.”

#6884. The Sisters Behind Dannijo Are Making Sports Merch Cool

DannijoPro was built on the idea that female fans deserve game-day gear they actually want to wear.

When Knicks frenzy overtook New York City this past summer during the team’s historic championship run, women fans—both long-suffering devotees and bandwagoners alike—went looking for something to wear. And in a city known for its fashion sense and individuality, the same old generic logo shirts and jerseys simply would not do.

DannijoPro answered the call. Seemingly overnight, the brand’s cropped blue satin bombers, bedazzled denim jackets with crochet collars, and artfully embroidered vintage sports tees took over Instagram, with celebrities like Brooke Shields, cool girls, and Ali Brunson, wife of star point guard Jalen Brunson, and Jordyn Woods, who wed center Karl-Anthony Towns shortly after he won the chip, photographed wearing DannijoPro. Knicks royalty Spike Lee even wore a DannijoPro custom jacket courtside at Game 5.

Danielle and Jodie (Danni + Jo, get it?) Snyder, the sisters and co-founders behind the bespoke fanwear company (a sporty spinoff of their original jewelry and fashion brand, Dannijo), say they were just happy to be along for the historic ride. “No one could have predicted the Knicks were going to go as far as they did, but that was amazing for us,” Jodie says of the win that ended a 53-year title drought. “People loved what we were doing.” Their Knicks finals shirts sold out three times; all 800 satin bombers (priced at $395) sold out, too. Overall, the sisters tell me their sales for 2026 are up 200 percent from last year. “I’ve never seen anything like it in my life,” says Danielle, who, along with Jodie, lived in NYC for over a decade and visited during the finals. “The energy was electric and there was such community. People were buying merch because it was so much more than a garment. It’s a piece of history. It’s a relic now.”

The Knicks run may have fueled their virality, but DannijoPro’s popularity also stems from the fact that it is serving an audience that has been ignored for too long by companies that thought that, when designing sports merch for women, it was enough to “pink it and shrink it.” And although 75 percent of women identify as fans of one or more sports, and women’s sports merch is estimated to be a $4 billion market, still a quarter of female fans say they can’t find styles they like. “It’s crazy that we’re in 2026 and you can go to a game and not find anything you want to buy as a female fan,” Jodie says.

Three months after the Knicks took home the trophy, I reach the sisters as they are gearing up for the launch of their new NBA collection for the 2026-27 season and a number of other new partnerships that have come in the wake of their viral turn, including one rolling out this month with their hometown team, the Jacksonville Jaguars—DannijoPro’s first NFL collab. Danielle is in the brand’s atelier in Tiburon in Marin County, California; as we chat on Zoom, I see people hand-stitching various garments in the background. Jodie joins the call from her car in Jacksonville, Florida, where she heads up the company’s retail and business strategy.

The sisters, who are three years apart, grew up as close as could be in Jacksonville. They’ve been creative, crafty, and entrepreneurial from a young age. Their mom, a mental health counselor, didn’t have pierced ears, but she did have a killer clip-on earring collection, and the girls were obsessed with it. In middle school, Jodie took an interest in making jewelry of her own, and Danielle, wanting to do everything her big sister did, followed right along.

Their dad is a cardiologist, and they taught themselves how to do wire work using his medical tools. Soon, they were collaborating on intricately beaded designs and selling them in local stores. “We’d see kids around school wearing our handmade designs and they didn’t even know it was us making them,” Danielle says. “What we were doing was really fresh and cool. Girls and women could take our accessories and update a white tee and jeans, or anything basic, and make it their own. That was our first taste of using style to individualize and to tell stories and we caught the bug.”

They got their big break a few years later when Danielle was in middle school. Neiman Marcus bought 150 of their turquoise nugget necklaces. “I remember staying up until 3 or 4 in the morning cranking them out,” Danielle says. “And I had an exam the next morning.” The Snyders continued to balance school and their design ambitions. Years later, when Jodie was at the University of Florida studying business and public relations and Danielle at Vanderbilt, where she majored in psychology and Spanish, Jodie would fly up and they would do trunk shows at sororities on campus. “We were always making stuff for wherever we were in life,” Jodie explains. “And everything we do is rooted in sisterhood and wanting to be together, wanting to express ourselves,” Danielle adds.

Jodie graduated from college in 2004 and moved to New York City, with Danielle following in 2007. Dannijo was founded the following year and, like the Snyders’ childhood company, quickly became known for its statement jewelry pieces. Their collection was sold in Bergdorf Goodman and Henri Bendel; characters on Gossip Girl and anchors on the Today show wore their necklaces; Beyoncé and Kim Kardashian did too (purchasing them themselves). “I remember the saleswoman calling us and saying, ‘You’re never going to believe who bought the Ruth Celia necklace,’” Danielle says. It was Queen B, who went on to wear it at Coachella, courtside at NBA games, and on a trip with her husband, Jay-Z, in Europe. “So we started getting press before we even really knew what PR was.”

They soon expanded into fashion, designing the “perfect silk slip dress” modeled after Kate Moss in the ’90s. When Jodie’s daughter was 3, she went to a tie-dye party and the next thing they knew, Dannijo was selling tie-dyed hoodies. They styled it over a slip dress and it went viral. “We sold thousands of tie-dye hoodies—it was insane,” Jodie says. “Basically, we just made things that we wanted to wear.” Collabs with Starbucks, Mercedes-Benz, and Swarovski followed.

In 2020, as the pandemic took hold in NYC, the sisters, like many residents, decamped for other parts of the country. Jodie moved back to Florida, while Danielle moved to the Bay Area. The move across the country was hard on Danielle, who was suffering from postpartum depression. “I was lost, lonely, and I didn’t have community,” she notes.

One day, her husband came home with Golden State tickets and told her they were going out. “That first Warriors game changed my life,” Danielle explains. “It changed my perspective. It got me excited again—I was excited to root for a team and to be a part of something.” They started attending games regularly and Danielle began creating her own designs to wear. “I started going through my husband’s closet and cutting up all of his old jerseys and making them into outfits for me,” she says. “My fix is always to be creative, so I was using my hands, doing stitch work, adding crystals, what have you.” Other female fans at the games began taking notice of her creative designs and asking where she had bought them. “Everyone was noticing because there was just nothing like it out there, so I called Jodie and I said, ‘This is really big.’” Soon, Dannijo’s sporty sister brand, DannijoPro, was born.

Launching a company focused on women fans was a “no-brainer,” Danielle says. “When I started making myself stuff to wear, that’s when I noticed what a crazy white space it was. Sports have never been more popular, but the product just had not caught up to that reality.” It took a year and a half to get an NBA license, but once they did, the first DannijoPro collection, which rolled out in 2024 at the Chase Center (where the Warriors play), quickly sold out.

As ever, the sisters are their own inspiration. Jodie has two children and Danielle has three. “Our worlds have changed,” Jodie says. “We’re both in T-shirts and sweatshirts; we’re not getting dressed up every day anymore.” As such, the DannijoPro items they make aren’t just for game day. They are for the 70 percent of women fans who say they’ve bought sports merch, while only 51 percent have bought tickets to attend an event. “We wanted to create items that people want to wear on and off the court or field,” Jodie says. “It's about making flattering silhouettes and clothes we all want to wear, and then applying them to the teams that we’re excited about.”

DannijoPro has three main products: officially licensed merch complete with the signature touches the designers have come to be known for, like crochet collars, hand-stitching, painting, and crystal work; “blanks” that can be customized with the help of its in-house artists (most orders come via DM); and vintage pieces that are reworked by hand.

As Jodie talks, Danielle runs around the atelier pulling out products from their new collections to show me. In August, they launched designs they made for avatars in the NBA 2K game. In September, they debuted a collab with Roger Federer’s Laver Cup tennis tournament. Their new NBA collection, called “Game Day 2.0,” rolled out last month, while their Jacksonville Jaguars line will go on sale on November 17. Future partnerships with other teams and leagues are in the works.

The sisters see their new company as “so much bigger than a fashion brand,” Danielle says. “We are reimagining fandom through a culture lens, and it’s about people. We’re living in a world of AI where everything’s like a bot. For us, it’s like, how do we bring really cool dynamic experiences in real life to people and create connections and community?”

“We think women fans should be celebrated in that there are products out there that speak to us,” Jodie adds. “It’s more than merch. I think it stands for so many other things. We can’t just accept that it’s okay to just slap logos on things, or pink it and shrink it, and women are just going to go with that. And at the end of the day, all these companies forget, but women have the buying power.”

#6881. Rose Byrne Goes Rosy Pink in Straight-Off-the-Runway Dior

Rose Byrne is pretty in pink. Last night, the cast of crime-thriller series The Good Daughter gathered in Times Square for the New York City premiere. And for the occasion, the leading actor showed up to the buzzing area’s Times Center event space in a straight-off-the-runway gown from Jonathan Anderson’s Spring/Summer 2027 presentation for Dior.

On top, the baby-pink look was crafted from lightweight silk chiffon, which had been finely pleated into a textured peplum bodice with a sleeveless silhouette. The neckline and arm holes were trimmed with little ruffles, but at the hem, Anderson left things raw—giving a rough edge to an otherwise soft and delicate number.

From the flared tunic, airy pale-blush chiffon floated down to the ankle, creating a two-tone mid-length skirt that was finished with a second raw hem. On the runway, the piece was paired with cherry-red heels with white piping, but at the premiere, Byrne went with a simple pointed-toe white alternative.

When it came to jewelry, the Bridesmaids actor left her wrists and ears bare, selecting only a statement necklace with crystal-embellished leaf motifs. Her shoulder-length hair was blown out with a deep side part, and for makeup, she went rosy—with pink blush and warm coral-red lips.

Next to Byrne, costar Meghann Fahy went for an opposite look, wearing a silky black midi dress with a high neckline, three-quarter sleeves, and a slim fit. She added over-the-knee velvet boots and slicked her hair back into an updo while keeping her makeup simple.

#6880. The Woven Tote Bag Trend Is Taking Over Runways and Workdays

Woven bags have officially reached icon status.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Is there any other bag that works as hard in real life as the tote bag? When it comes to being on duty and looking good, probably not. The humble tote has come in all iterations: lightweight cotton, supple leather, and suede. But more recently, the runways have been obsessed with making the oversize tote-style bags in luscious jumbo woven iterations. We saw them on the spring/summer 2027 runways at Dries Van Noten, Courrèges, Bottega Veneta, and Altuzarra. And then, labels like Bembien, Naghedi, and Dragon Diffusion are pumping out woven totes like there’s no tomorrow.

A big woven bag offers up texture and interest in a sea of ubiquitous canvas logo totes. It also gives you a bit of resilience so you don’t have to baby your bag on, say, a subway commute or a brisk morning walk where the risk of bumping into others is second nature. It is the working girl’s best friend. That’s just one of the reasons why fashion retailers have fallen head over heels for the style lately.

“Regardless of lifestyle, everyone needs a bag that carries a lot, and potentially the kitchen sink,” Ryan Kleman, Moda Operandi’s VP of buying, accessories, tells ELLE. The great news about a woven bag is that it also seamlessly goes with everything. Tone it down for a day at the office with a button-down top or a casual dress, or take it out for a nice dinner; a silk scarf tied about the strap can be the pop of color or embellishment you might crave for a more formal occasion.

In the case of some of these brands, the woven tote is literal fashion history. Bottega Veneta, for example, first introduced its signature “Intrecciato” woven leather technique in 1975. The fashion house’s spring/summer 2027 collection modernized the technique by applying it to enormous, hulking bags that swayed when worn over the shoulder. Paired with layered sweaters and matching leather belts, the bags felt like a decidedly fresh take on wearable artisanship by creative director Louise Trotter.

In the case of Dries Van Noten, one model carried an absolutely huge woven raffia-effect foldover clutch. Styled with a heather gray hoodie rolled up to reveal leather bubblegum-pink opera gloves with a biker-style cutout and buckle at the hand, the bag itself proved just how versatile a woven piece can be.

If you’re indeed thinking of upgrading your everyday work bag for a woven tote, it’s better to think bigger. “The oversized scale of the bags and the woven construction provide the perfect details to convert a chore into a fashion statement,” says Kleman. “At Moda, we have dubbed this trend the Big A** Bag and our customers are loving it.” This, too, is true: the bigger the weave, the better the carryall.

#6877. Here’s Your Complete Release Schedule for The Rings of Power Season 3

Because we know your time is ... precious.

One does not simply walk into Mordor, but it is rather easy to binge a new season. The Lord of the Rings: The Rings of Power is back on Prime Video for season 3 to continue the Second Age of Middle-earth. The Rings have been forged, yet there is still one more ring to rule them all. But how can anyone from the realms of men keep up with Rings of Power?

In The Lord of the Rings: The Rings of Power season 3, Sauron (Charlie Vickers) has finally dropped his facade. No longer hiding behind fake names like Halbrand or Annatar, he is truly himself: Sauron, the Dark Lord. And the Dark Lord now races to forge the fabled One Ring from his seat in Barad-dûr.

Meanwhile, as his orc armies eclipse Middle-earth, his war rages with the Elves—concentrated in Lindon and Rivendell—who begin an alliance with Dwarves and men. And a few wizards thrown in for good measure.

Four years since it premiered on Prime Video, The Rings of Power is officially at the halfway mark of its predetermined five-season run. While it’s still hard for the show to escape the shadow of Peter Jackson’s immortal trilogy, Rings of Power is still a handsome effort at probing all that went down before a Hobbit picked up a ring in a cave.

You don’t need Palantir to know when The Rings of Power drops new episodes on Prime Video. Here’s how and when you can watch new episodes of the Lord of the Rings series.

The Lord of the Rings: The Rings of Power season 3 premiered its first four episodes on November 11, exclusively on Prime Video. They are available to watch right now. The rest of the season will roll out weekly in batches, with two episodes per week until November 25. Which is awesome! Once you’ve seen every episode, you can roll right into your annual post-Thanksgiving trilogy rewatch.

As a quick note: With Prime Video originals, there’s a favorable chance you can watch new episodes a few hours earlier. Most of the platform’s shows actually drop new episodes the night before. That’s what happened with The Rings of Power season 3, as the first few episodes were made available to stream on November 10 at 8:00 p.m. ET even though Prime Video promoted November 11 as the official date. So if you’re really impatient, check for new episodes the night before.

Here’s when you can watch every new episode of The Rings of Power season 3.

Episode 3: “The Secret Fire” — November 11

#6876. The 40 Best Beauty Products Under $30

#6875. Misha & Puff’s First Womenswear Collection Gives Knitwear a New Point of View

The cult-favorite children’s knitwear brand is officially expanding to adults.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Women’s fashion has some of the best designs out there—full stop. That said, every now and then I see a well-dressed kid and think, “Damn, I wish I had something like that.” This is especially true when I see a little one sporting a design from none other than Misha & Puff, a brand known for its ethically crafted, handmade, vintage-inspired children’s clothes. And while my wardrobe carries a playful touch, I’m not necessarily looking for a supersized version of a child’s sweater, but rather the essence of that whimsy. Thankfully, I don’t have to look too far; Misha & Puff just launched its first-ever women’s collection—and it’s more than I could have hoped for.

“Knits are hot. Knits are sexy. The way that they drape, the way that they accentuate your neck, I really do feel like we can do a lot with a knit,” Sophia Roe told ELLE.com over the phone. “For me, I feel really beautiful when I put these clothes on.” The James Beard Award-winning chef—along with her daughter, Mizu—came together with artist Mara Hoffman behind the lens to create a campaign that captures the essence of Misha & Puff, highlighting the craftsmanship and expression of the brand’s new womenswear collection.

“Everything I do with the brand is very authentic and really about what I’m interested in...That’s how the kids’ brand started, with me having my son and then growing up,” Misha & Puff’s founder, Anna Wallack, said on a phone call. “The choice to launch women’s in a bigger way really comes from the pull that I’m feeling as a woman. For me, dressing women and dressing myself is about how we’re working these pieces together. How do I feel in these silhouettes? Who is this character today? I want to keep playing with that in a bigger way.”

Wallack went on to tell me that designing for the collection—which launched today—started with what she described as a “fiber-first” approach. “We work with a Pima cotton in Peru that worked so well for kids because it’s incredibly soft and it’s so wearable, and then to find that I want to wear that all the time for myself; I want to wear our merino wool,” she explained. “Our decision to launch women’s with a bigger collection really was a commitment to doing it instead of just making one sweater here and there.” The result? A range of expressive knits in a color palette reminiscent of the ’70s that encourage the wearer to be who she is as a woman today. With every solid- or heather-hued piece there’s another featuring an eye-catching print or embroidery detail. “I feel a little more permission to explore other ideas in the adult collection and what it means to be a women, too.”

This exploration extends into Misha & Puff’s campaign. Roe shared on a phone call that the idea of partnering with Misha & Puff stemmed from just being a fan of the brand. “I said, ‘I don’t know what we can do but I really want to do something with Anna.’ Little did I know that in the background she was working on this unbelievable adult collection,” she said. Though this isn’t the first project Roe has starred in with her daughter, she explained it was the first where she was shot by another mother, making the experience feel truly special—especially as she comes into her body 15 months postpartum. “I feel kind of hot as a mom. There are a lot of narratives out there about getting back to whatever that pre-baby thing is, and I just kind of rebuke that in every single way. I think what I am now is kind of amazing and I really do feel like Mara was able to bring that out in a way that no one else could have.”

On the vision for the campaign, Hoffman shared that the focus was “seeing how does a woman exist through a space or on the street, and how does she live in it?” Working to capture Roe in a documentarian way, the artist wanted to make sure the imagery felt authentic to both the brand and the people wearing it. “We are lucky to be able to capture little shots of that along the way.” Of course, shooting with a baby can come with its own unpredictability. Luckily, as Roe shared, working with someone like Hoffman helped her relax on set with the reminder, “This is just life.”

#6868. The Ultimate Guide to the Rolex Daytona, the World’s Most Wanted Watch

From the hand-wound watches dealers struggled to sell to the steel model that commands an impressive premium today.

In September, four Rolex Daytonas sold at a charity auction in London for a combined £37.3 million (about $49.5 million). The most expensive, at £10.3 ($13.7) million, had a turquoise dial and a ring of pink sapphires. That same month, Rolex unveiled a steel-and-platinum Daytona with a white enamel dial and a speed scale that recalled the original from 1963.

The regular steel Daytona with a white dial costs $16,900 from Rolex, if you can get one. In September, WatchCharts put its secondary-market value around $35,000. It wasn’t always like this. In its early years, according to Sotheby’s, retailers discounted Daytonas to clear stock.

Rolex launched the Cosmograph in 1963. An early advert called it the “Le Mans,” after the French endurance race. The name that stuck came from Daytona, Florida, where cars raced on hard-packed sand before the city had a speedway.

The first model, reference 6239, had a movement you wound by hand and two buttons to operate the stopwatch. Rolex put the speed scale on the steel bezel, where a driver could read it at a glance. The three small counters stood out against the dial, black on a light face or light on a black one.

By the 1970s, two models had emerged that collectors still prize today. The 6263 had a black acrylic bezel, and the 6265 had a metal one. Both had screw-down collars around the stopwatch buttons. On some, the word Daytona curves in red above the bottom counter. Collectors call these “Big Reds.”

Joanne Woodward gave Newman a 6239 around the time he made Winning, the 1969 film that sparked his interest in racing. She had “DRIVE CAREFULLY ME” engraved on the back. “There’s something almost magical about it,” James Cox, who later owned the watch, told Phillips. In 2017 it sold for $17.75 million, then an auction record for a wristwatch.

A “Paul Newman” usually refers to a type of dial. Look for the unusual numerals and small square-ended markers in the counters. The dials were unpopular when new. By 2013, Christie’s could sell 50 Daytonas in a single Geneva auction for $13.2 million. The top lot was a steel watch with a Paul Newman dial. It fetched $1.09 million, four years before the actor’s own watch went to auction.

On one particularly rare black-dial 6263, the word Oyster appears below Rolex Cosmograph. Collectors call it the Oyster Sotto, from the Italian for under. Phillips sold one in 2025 for 1.39 million Swiss francs (approximately $1.67 million). “One mistake people make is that they expect everything to fit neatly into a specific set of rules,” Geoff Hess, global head of watches at Sotheby’s, once explained. A dial can be genuine Rolex and still have come from a different watch.

In 1988 the steel Daytona became the 16520. It grew to 40mm, gained raised gold hour markers and a glossier dial, and wound itself as you wore it. Rolex extensively reworked Zenith’s El Primero movement for the watch. It was the first automatic Daytona and the model that established the basic dimensions of the watch Rolex still makes today.

On some early examples, a gap makes the word Cosmograph appear to float beneath the rest of the lettering. On certain black dials, the rings around the counters have turned brown with age. Those are known as Patrizzi dials, after the auctioneer Osvaldo Patrizzi, who noticed the change.

Three steel references are worth knowing. The 116520, introduced in 2000, has a steel bezel and the first chronograph movement Rolex developed and made itself. The 116500LN, from 2016, brought back the look of the black acrylic bezel in ceramic. The current 126500LN, introduced in 2023, has a slim steel rim around that ceramic bezel, a revised dial, and a new movement that runs for about three days when fully wound. The two ceramic models can look remarkably similar in listing photographs.

The unicorn is a hand-wound 6265 that Phillips described in 2018 as the only known vintage Daytona in white gold. It sold for 5.94 million Swiss francs (about $7.1 million). The Rainbow gets its name from the graduated sapphires around its bezel. A white-gold example made around 1994, nearly two decades before the production versions appeared, sold in 2024 for 5.505 million Swiss francs (approximately $6.1 million).

Sixty years after that early advertisement, Rolex returned to Le Mans. Its 2023 watch for the race’s centenary has a stopwatch that counts the full 24 hours. The regular Daytona counts to 12.

An official Rolex retailer is the place to start if you want a new one at list price. Retailers manage their own allocations, so availability varies. Rolex addressed the shortage in 2021. “The scarcity of our products is not a strategy on our part,” it said, explaining that it could not meet demand without compromising quality.

On the secondary market, compare the same reference and dial. The WatchCharts September estimate for the current black-dial steel model was about $29,000, roughly $6,000 less than the white. If you’re buying vintage, ask a specialist dealer or auction house for a condition report. Find out whether the case has been heavily polished, which parts have been replaced, and whether the dial is correct for that particular watch. On a Daytona, those details can explain more about the price than the reference number.

#6865. The Best Hostess Gifts for Holiday Parties, Dinners, and Gift Exchanges

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

We’re not showing up empty-handed this season.

It’s officially “what can I bring?” season. The next two months are packed with parties, family dinners, and white elephant gift exchanges. For every potluck, there’s at least one stressed-out host. So when you’re deciding what to bring—because you wouldn’t dare show up empty-handed—don’t forget about the person throwing the party. As the shopping-obsessed design editors we are, we’ve got you covered on the very best hostess gifts.

There's no better time to score the perfect present for your friends and family eager to put on a soirée. The good news is that anyone who likes to host does it often enough (not just around the holidays) to use your gifts at least a few times throughout the year.

The best gifts will do one of two things: make hosting easier, or help them relax when the festivities are over. Below, you’ll find over 30 picks that fall into these two camps, from genius kitchen supplies to a silk eye mask for resting after the party. What's more, if you fall into the hostess camp from time to time, you may even spot a few things your own home could use, like a French-country-inspired utensil crock or a set of pale green Champagne flutes. We won’t judge if you shop for yourself, too.

Any host worth their salt needs a good set of cocktail napkins, and this set of four is extra special. Not only is the linen embroidered with delicate flowers, but the napkin is extra long, so you could hold a larger glass or an hors d'oeuvres with ease.

This is no mere wine coaster. Yes, it’s perfect for a bottle of pinot, but it’s super multi-purpose. It can hold bar nuts or charcuterie, serve as a trinket dish, or you can even place a kenzan inside for flower arrangements.

Doesn’t every hotel room feel a little fancier with a door hanger? Bring some of that boutique flair to your host’s home with this tassel. It’s hiding a secret: a fragrance diffuser scented with juniper, spruce, and black currant, perfect for a festive gathering.

The humble doorstop. Rarely thought of, but essential for any host when guests are a revolving door. This quirky-yet-sturdy cast aluminum snail will help them leave the door open for guests to mill about with ease.

When the party is winding down, the host is usually exhausted. Help them out with the dishes and give them a soft place to land with a new silk pillowcase and a cushy eye mask. They’ll wake up refreshed after an evening on 100 percent Mulberry silk.

Associate Shopping Editor Janae McKenzie keeps this reed diffuser at her desk in the HB office because “it’s a soft, crowd-pleasing smell.” The notes of rice milk, tonka bean, and sandalwood are like a warm hug, and the throw is gentle enough not to offend sensitive palates.

If the host is a dear friend or family member, remind them how much they mean to you with a thoughtful framed photo. This one is super versatile; it can be displayed horizontally or vertically. The center glass display rotates and gives the illusion of a floating image.

A utensil crock is one of those utilitarian things that everyone needs, but no one wants to buy for themselves. That's where you come in. Give them the item any cook needs: a place to store all their cooking utensils. Because it's out and on display, make sure it's cute. This one checks both boxes: It's attractive and big enough to store at least six or seven utensils.

No at-home fête is complete without an array of cheeses for which a set of knives is a must. This one, which includes five specialized knives, is a steal for $60. "I actually bought this set the first time I hosted my book club, and I have never looked back. I love that each handle is a slightly different shade of blue," says tester Jessica Cherner.

Nothing, and we mean nothing, ruins a beautiful tablescape like paper towels as napkins. It's like getting dressed up and wearing running shoes. If you've noticed your hostess ripping off pieces of paper towel when setting the table, get her a set of cloth dinner napkins that she can throw in the washing machine after the meal.

Calling all hostesses who make a mess in the kitchen: You're not alone. Lots of hostesses tend to cover their countertops in a light dusting of flour and splatter their brand-new white tees with red sauce. It happens to the best of us. Don't let it happen to the generous hostess who's cooking you dinner. Protect her look with this beautiful floral apron.

You may have noticed that most restaurants serve bread shortly after you sit down at the table. That's because offering guests bread is actually considered a sign of good hospitality, so if you're headed to someone's home for a brunch, luncheon, or dinner party, bring a beautiful bread basket with you. (Bonus points if there's bread in it!) The host can pop it in the middle of the table as a welcoming symbol to their guests.

Though no one needs a reason to throw a party, hosting friends and family to celebrate something exciting is always a little more fun than a no-occasion soirée. And no celebration is complete without Champagne. Saber a cork and pour some bubbles into these beautiful flutes, which are crafted from recycled South African glass.

Le Creuset debuted a new color last year, appropriately dubbed Pêche, which means "peach" in French. We don't like to play favorites, but this hue is definitely at the top of our list of Le Creuset launches. If you want to gift it to the hostess in your life, opt for something small, like this mini cocotte.

Mood lighting is key to setting the vibe at home, and nothing beats candlelight. These tapers not only add ambience, but they also enhance the tablescape with their twisty shape.

An ice bucket is an essential when it comes to party-hosting goods. This is one of our favorites because it has that old-school vintage look. In fact, it was designed with an heirloom quality in mind.

Another must-have? A trio of miniature bowls for nuts, candies, or anything else guests can easily pop into their mouth while they sip handcrafted libations or wait for the main course to arrive. We particularly love this set because they're all attached by a tiny handle.

After the hostess is done physically cleaning up after the party, let her clean the energy out too. Palo santo is the perfect way to do that. Simply light one end until the flame is deep within the stick, blow it out, and let the fragrant plumes waft through the air.

This marble wine chiller is another practical gift, but it doesn't skimp on beauty. Perhaps more than any natural stone, marble has an ability to keep liquids at their optimal temperature, which is why, back in the day, many bathtubs in wealthy homes were made entirely of marble. This wine cooler is a lot lighter and easier to maneuver than a tub, but it works the same way: keeping whatever is in it at peak temperature.

Backgammon has allegedly been around for millennia, but our earliest recordings stretch only as far back as 17th-century England. No matter, this clever game has clearly stood the test of time, as it's still a fun way to pass the time today. This set, designed by famed photographer Gray Malin, is one of the prettiest we've seen.

#6859. 3 Major Awards Shows Won’t Nominate Amanda Seyfried for 'You Can See Everything'

Awards season is almost upon us, which means celebrities are about to start campaigning hard—including Amanda Seyfried, whose brilliant performance as Elizabeth Holmes in You Can See Everything has earned a ton of Best Supporting Actress buzz.

While Variety previously reported that Amanda can be nominated for a Best Supporting Actress Oscar, apparently there are three major awards she will be ineligible for.

Per The Hollywood Reporter, Amanda is unable to be nominated for a Golden Globe, a BAFTA, or an Actor award due to her performance technically being in a documentary—though apparently SAG-AFTRA has told A24 they can appeal the decision.

Amanda told THR that she fully views her participation in the film as a performance, saying “The reenactments were definitely a challenge. But it’s not like I ever considered that I would be up for any kind of accolade. I understand the politics and the luck and the timing of awards stuff. I don’t really know if we can fully consider it a doc—it’s very genre-bending.”

Amanda added that she’s “fully present” for the awards season campaign, noting that “the amount of events and the amount of presence you need to have when campaigning for these things is pretty extreme—you saw me go through it last year—but it’s still all very positive because getting a seat at the table is the thing.”

She went on to explain that “A campaign is a lot. It’s time-consuming. It takes all your energy. And I’m totally willing to do it. A24 is great. They’re excited, and I’m very proud of the film, so I’m fully ready to engage in that. And I love these filmmakers! I had a great time with this tiny crew. And I think because [the experience] is so rare, and it will never happen again, I’m doubled-down on it.”
