#6857. Tommy Lee Jones Couldn’t Care Less About His Legacy

The screen legend has more interesting things going on—including a stellar turn on The Lowdown season 2, as an estranged, crusty old father.

Tommy Lee Jones, 80, an Oscar winner and a star of the second season of The Lowdown, the noirish Tulsa crime story undercut with terrific dime-store waggery, sits flat-footed in the green room at Good Morning America, in downtown Manhattan. He has just done an on-camera interview with Ethan Hawke, who stars as the show’s protagonist. In the next dressing room are many adorable, pawing puppies being primped for a segment on National Adopt-a-Dog Month. Jones is adorable too, in his avuncular way, his face an old-dog scowl that softens when you ask the right question, as if you’d scratched him in the right spot.

On The Lowdown, he plays Hawke’s estranged father, a crusty old bastard who holds secrets. There’s a bad history between them, and Hawke’s character, an impulsive, truth-seeking investigative journalist, at first wants nothing to do with his dad, but that situation evolves. They’re both named Lee Raybon.

Jones wears a glen-plaid sport coat, a blue dress shirt, and comfortable sneakers. When the interview begins, he looks at you, ready but giving nothing until he’s asked to.

ESQUIRE: I just watched the first six episodes of this season, and man, I wasn’t ready for [REDACTED].

TOMMY LEE JONES: Oh, you watched all those episodes? The [REDACTED] scene was—I hope that was kind of shocking.

We didn’t see it coming, which I guess is a testament to you all.

That’s the whole point. You didn’t see it coming. That’s the reason we did it.

The way you developed your character’s way of speaking, the way he trails off here and there, tells us that this guy knows some stuff he’s not giving away. He’s shifty. It’s just brilliant. I’m just trying to give a compliment, not really asking a question.

You said to Charlie Rose in an interview a long time ago that there’s no through line that runs through the characters you play. At the same time, you don’t take just any role that comes along. Do you ever think about legacy? How are they going to remember Tommy Lee Jones? Or is it really just “This role would be a challenge to play, so I’m going to take it?”

I don’t have long-term thinking, and I don’t even think in a term like legacy. I think more like a dog. I’m absorbed with what’s in front of me. I might run off and chase a squirrel.

I believe you. It’s just that I’ve started thinking recently, What am I writing about? Is there a body of work here? I hadn’t thought that way before. With each story it was just This is a cool story. I’ll do this story.

That’s what I thought. I thought this show was really cool. The people that were working on it were cool, and Tulsa is an extremely cool place. Ethan made a good point on TV just now when he said good noir stories always have a sense of place, so the location was very important. It’s true about film noir, and this show really qualifies as part of that genre. A keen sense of place, leading to inarguable originality.

How far are Tulsa and eastern Oklahoma from where you live in Texas?

Oh, I don’t know. It’s northeast of Dallas. So I think the drive up—

[He turns to his wife of of 25 years, Dawn, who sits in the corner looking at her phone.]

How long would it take to drive to Tulsa from San Antonio?

When I was in prep school, we went up to Oklahoma to play a school in football, and they always cheated. Also when I was in high school, they would sell 3.2 beer to minors, and it was fun, a big adventure every once in a while, to pile into a car and drive to Oklahoma to buy a beer.

Ethan’s hilarious on The Lowdown, but you’re also very funny! Always have been. The warden in Natural Born Killers, Dwight McClusky, is your funniest role, I feel. Samuel Gerard in The Fugitive. And then I’m watching this show and was reminded how funny you can be. How would you describe your sense of humor? What’s funny to you?

People. People are hilarious. The best humor is found in human behavior. Any student of human behavior is going to be highly amused, when they’re not horrified.

Did you watch stuff as a kid that made you laugh?

The Three Stooges. Slapstick. Warner Brothers cartoons. That’s fundamental humor. I mean, if I see a fat lady slip on a banana peel, I swear I’m going to laugh.

You share some beautiful scenes with the young actress who plays your granddaughter, Ryan Kiera Armstrong. I couldn’t think of many films in which we see you spend a lot of time with children.

There was a movie called River Rats, and I was on a boat for a good part of this movie with two children, one of whom was Martha Plimpton, as a little slip of a girl, playing my daughter. We became good friends on that, and I was happy to see her on The Lowdown.

You and Ethan, and Martha now too, are seasoned veterans. Is the approach different when you’re working with young Ryan, who doesn’t have 50 films under her belt? Is there a greater need with a child actor to get to know each other before filming?

Oh, actors are actors, so it’s not difficult for me to relate to an actor regardless of their age. And she’s a pro. Actors, they have a way of getting to know one another very quickly.

Yeah, she is. And she’s a fearless and admirable young lady.

[Shit.] Not from you. I don’t mean in your scenes. I mean her other scenes.

Getting back to the idea of a through line: If I may point out one commonality across your performances, it’s that your characters have wisdom. You yourself are obviously well-read. You went to Harvard, and, for example, you’ve compared the satire of Natural Born Killers to Molière. Is reading still a big part of your life?

I’ve been making a study of Texas history, which begins with—well, there is no beginning. It’s always been there. But its experience as an American territory, for me, begins with James K. Polk. I read a political biography of Polk. That’s a good place to start a study of modern Texas history.

Yeah, well, he was Andrew Jackson’s bitch.

Getting back to Lee Raybon Sr., whose nickname is Frog, and his son. Fathers and sons are always rich territory.

Ethan’s character changes his attitude toward you. It gets better. Did you see that as Frog’s project, what this character is after—his son’s love?

[Mildly annoyed.] Well, I compared myself to a dog earlier because I really deal with what’s in front of me.

And all the qualities that you see in that character are ones that Sterlin Harjo, the show’s creator, wrote. We learn that this character is dying. That slowly begins to dawn on us, and we see that he’s trying to somehow reconcile life with his son and get to know his granddaughter. But that quest in this character is not advertised. It’s discovered as we watch things unfold in an often amusing way, and always original. That was appealing. I mean, I think that’s the way it works.

Can I, at the risk of pissing you off: I know you say that you’re like a dog. Are you telling me that—

I don’t want to belabor that too much. I’m a little bit like a dog. [Laughs, kind of.]

But this is what I’m getting at. I’ve seen every film you’ve ever made, and you don’t give a lot of interviews, but in the ones I’ve seen over the years you’ve made it clear that each character is its own separate project. No connections. But at the same time, I know how smart you are, and so part of me thinks, Come on. Of course you must see some continuum along the characters you play. We see similarities among characters in literature, even across different authors in different centuries. The through line of all your parts is that you chose them. So pushing back a tiny bit, I’m suggesting that your performance as Lee Raybon has inherited something from these previous men that you’ve inhabited in the last 50 years. Is that fair at all?

[Breathing a sigh of relief, and perhaps satisfaction.] Okay.

[Pauses, hoping this line of questioning was over.] Oh. Well, I don’t know. I’m not aware of any continuum. [Any sense of Esquire’s relief or satisfaction disappears.] I don’t think it’s in my best interest, and I’m not inclined to think about what all the characters I play have in common. The only thing they have in common is that I’m playing them. And they’re all built out of experience. Let me refer you to an essay by T.S. Eliot called “Tradition and the Individual Talent.” It’s an indication of what happens to actors, really. Their performances are made out of the shards and the pieces and the bits of your experience of life and your thinking about it. One of the reasons you read that essay is so you have a better understanding of The Waste Land or even James Joyce. Modern art is comprised of the shards of experience, reassembled in a way that has a dramatic form, with a beginning, a middle, an end, and a purpose.

Do you see a performance such as this one as a piece of art?

No. Not really. I don’t know what it—you know, this is the thing I never give any thought to: What is art?

Compare your view of God when you were six or seven years old to now.

[Coming to life a bit.] Oh! Well? I never imagined a man in a white robe with a long white beard sitting on a throne mounted on a cloud with some lightning bolts in his hand. That’s one image. It’s probably almost as good as any other, but the question is, you either believe in such a thing as God or you don’t. I do.

Has your view or understanding changed over your life?

Not really. My grandmother and my aunts and my mother always took me to a Southern Baptist church. I was baptized in the Southern Baptist church. And, like all organized religions, there’s a good deal of nonsense in it. But it also leads to an awareness of the deity, which by definition exceeds our capability to define it.

[A long pause as Jones stares at the floor, then looks up, eyebrows raised and smiling.]

That was pretty good. I’m gonna write that down.

If you had to cook a meal for dinner, what’s the best thing you could come up with?

[For the first time during the interview, Jones’s body relaxes—arm over the back of the chair.]

I’d probably buy some pork ribs, an entire side of a little young pig, and slowly barbecue that over a mesquite fire and serve it with a mixture of sautéed yellow squash and onions and probably a sweet potato. You can scratch them with a fork and rub them down with butter, wrap them up in tinfoil and cook them alongside the pig right over the fire. You can feed a lot of people that way, and it tastes good. And it’s fun to eat.

There’s a product you can buy in San Saba, Texas, called Bill’s Season All. Bill’s Season All is something you sprinkle on pork ribs and it makes them taste real good. I don’t know what all’s in it, but it’s delectable.

Texas chili: Do you have strong opinions?

[Jones snaps to even more, speaking a little louder and with some of the gusto and urgency of U.S. Marshal Samuel Gerard saying, “Listen up, ladies and gentlemen. Our fugitive has been on the run for 90 minutes. Average foot speed over uneven ground barring injuries is four miles an hour, and that gives us a radius of six miles. What I want out of each and every one of you is a hard-target search of every gas station, residence, warehouse, farmhouse, henhouse, outhouse, and doghouse in that area. Checkpoints go up at 15 miles. Your fugitive’s name is Dr. Richard Kimble. Go get him.”]

Don’t use ground beef. You need to cube it. Cube that beef into pieces about the size of the dice that you play Monopoly with. And after that, your own creativity comes in with the spices. But it’s important to avoid ground beef. And you don’t put beans in chili. That’s on the side. That’s at the discretion of the diner. Some people just like chili, but they just don’t necessarily want to see a bunch of beans floating in it. You’ll find one recipe in the Hill Country of Texas and another one in the hills above Santa Fe. Quite different.

Last night, I was watching either the fifth or sixth episode of The Lowdown, and Martha Plimpton’s character talks about putting a couple spoonfuls of Tang in chili.

I can’t imagine putting Tang in chili. But chili is like gumbo. Or Kentucky burgoo. Or Irish stew. It’s a food that’s in and of the people, so the way of cooking it and the ingredients vary from place to place and from circumstance to circumstance. Real gumbo has always got—well, it can have anything in it. Duck … goose … whatever crawled across the porch last night. Chili’s the same kind of thing. It’s the food of the people, and as such, the main ingredients are whatever you’ve got.

I don’t think so. I’m closer to profligate than frugal. We spend a lot of money on horses. My wife is one of the better female polo players in the United States. I played polo for 40-some-odd years. I’m a bit too old for it now. “Old and worn-out” is what they would call it.

Where did you live when you lived here in New York?

Yeah, it was. When I first came here in 1969, the streets were covered in dog shit. And it really got to be bad. Land mines. They passed some ordinances in the city that people had to clean up after their dogs. You’d have to be very careful walking down the street. Nothing more embarrassing than going into a meeting with dog shit on your shoes.

So now that you’ve turned in this astonishingly beautiful performance as Lee Raybon—

—and are there hills left to conquer as an actor? Are there types of roles you have yet to play?

I don’t know. There’s a schedule for the day. And I looked at it, but I don’t really—I’d have to ask her. That’s the reason I don’t have to know. I have her.

Okay, well, I appreciate this. I hope it wasn’t too painful and I didn’t—

I know talking about acting is not the most interesting thing.

Well, it’s breakfast time. When it’s first thing in the morning at home, if your cook’s not there and you’re hungry, what could you whip up?

I have a French press for coffee, a toaster for a bagel. And we’ve always got cream cheese and smoked salmon. Bagel, lox, cream cheese, coffee, and a grapefruit. That’s what I ordinarily eat for breakfast.

You’re right, this is more interesting than talking about acting.

Oh, breakfast. Breakfast is more interesting than anything.

#6855. Zimmermann Hosts a Star-Studded Soirée in Downtown New York City

Katie Holmes, Uma Thurman, and Naomi Watts came out to celebrate the brand’s cruise 2027 collection.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Inside Indochine’s red-lit, retro-inspired French-Vietnamese dining room in New York City, Zimmermann gathered friends of the brand to celebrate its cruise 2027 collection. Presented on the sun-drenched Côte d’Azur in June, the label’s latest served as the perfect bit of vacation and occasion-ready dressing for November in New York. Now that the lineup has officially hit stores, fashion insiders and fans of the brand alike (including Uma Thurman, Eve Hewson, Katie Holmes, and Naomi Watts) showed up dripping in ruffled gowns, sailor-inspired stripes, chunky shearlings, and ethereal lace and silks from the collection. Against the dark, 1940s-inspired tropical salon interior of Indochine, it was the ideal mix of downtown energy and New York glamour.

As champagne flowed through the bar area, glasses clinked while miniature crab cakes and spring rolls were passed. Zimmermann’s sister duo Nicky and Simone Zimmermann could be spotted catching up with friends new and old. “We have a very, very close connection with New York,” Nicky told ELLE in between greeting guests. “It was pretty much when I started the business, one of the first places internationally that I went to in the ’90s, and it blew my mind. It’s been inspirational to us 100 percent.” It was a rare occasion for the brand; for years, Zimmermann has shown at Paris Fashion Week (after presenting in New York in the mid-2010s) and the duo oscillates between Sydney and Paris for most of the year.

Zimmermann’s painterly and intrinsically bohemian flourishes could be seen in the many outfits of guests, all highlighting the vivid cruise collection. Think: decadent silky draped drop-waisted gowns and paneled lace maxi dresses, or, for the more demure, sleek all-black looks that captured the New York essence. “For me, there’s a little bit of a sense of humor behind it—without insulting anybody—it was about the story when Australia actually beat America in the America’s Cup, which is a very famous sailing race,” Nicky told ELLE of the collection’s inspiration.

“I think it was in 1983, and America had not lost the America’s Cup in something like 173 years; it was a ridiculous winning streak, and Australia came along. The inspiration is more about a David and Goliath kind of story,” she continued. “We came along as the Aussies, not established in the sailing world. They were very cheeky, rogue, kind of Aussie young guys up against this amazing, sophisticated establishment of the New York Yacht Club. And they never lost—and we beat them. So in Australia, it’s a legendary story.” Nicky combined the sportiness of sailing gear with the sort of elite New York Yacht Club preppiness, resulting in colorful silk scarf embellishment or sweaters decked out in accessorized pins. “They would hold the most incredible parties and the women were so glamorous and sophisticated and beautiful and not anything that Australia could relate to at the time, so I loved the contrast.”

Conversation was flowing and blooming anthuriums covered the tables as guests took their seats. Nicky kicked off the dinner with a speech, musing on her early days in New York on the Lower East Side and the many parties she attended in Tribeca before sharing more inspiration behind the collection: “It’s about establishment, and then it’s about people, you know, working really hard, coming together as a team, defeating the undefeatable,” she said, with a laugh.

Next came an intimate performance by the singer and Bad Monkey actress Charlotte Lawrence, who sang two of her original songs, then led a folksy sing-along to “Dreams” by Fleetwood Mac. Even the most guarded of celebs and fashion’s elite couldn’t help but bob their heads and sing a tune. As guests dined on a family-style mix of kale salad, spicy shrimp, grilled salmon, barbecued chicken, and steak, the champagne glass clinks slowed as candles slowly flickered and the evening closed out with lemon tarts.

A little yacht-club polish, a little Aussie irreverence: Zimmermann celebrated cruise 2027 in high style.

#6854. Faux Fur, Tuxedo Dressing, and Other Coat Trends Taking Over This Winter

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

These are the styles to know as the weather gets colder

A great winter outfit deserves an equally great coat, especially when it’s an essential layer for the next several months. Beyond the practical need for warmth, outerwear often sets the tone for an entire look as the first layer you see. A sharply tailored wool coat can make jeans and a sweater feel more polished, while an oversize silhouette or rich texture brings dimension and new proportions to otherwise clean-lined looks.

For fall/winter 2026, designers are rethinking classic outerwear through distinctive silhouettes, textures, and details. At Ferragamo, Tory Burch, and Fendi, precise tailoring took a dressier turn, with slim silhouettes, tuxedo-inspired designs, and satin lapels bringing eveningwear polish to wool coats. Meanwhile, Ralph Lauren, Mugler, and Burberry embraced leather for a sleeker city-ready take on winter dressing. Faux fur took on a distinctly vintage feel at Stella McCartney, Bottega Veneta, and Khaite, while plush collars added texture to everything from elegant overcoats to more everyday designs.

Whether you’re looking to invest in a forever piece or simply want something fresher than the coat you’ve worn for the past few winters, these six trends offer plenty of inspiration.

Faux fur is taking a distinctly retro turn this winter, with plush textures and elegant silhouettes reminiscent of vintage finds. Stella McCartney’s floor-grazing belted coat and Bottega Veneta’s voluminous fur both recall the glamour of the ’30s and ’40s, while Khaite’s shaggy cream jacket has more of a ’70s feel, particularly when dressed down with jeans. Of course, we love a faux-fur coat as the finishing touch to a dressy outfit, but there’s something even chicer about pairing one with everyday denim and boots.

Arma Verena Reversible Shearling and Leather Coat

For fall/winter 2026, the black evening coat is having a moment. Designers showcased styles with luxe updates to keep things more interesting. Think sharp, menswear-inspired tailoring with a more formal finish, as seen at Celine, to elegant satin coats at Dries Van Noten. It’s the go-to coat to wear to upcoming holiday parties and formal dinners, or if you just want to upgrade your traditional winter coat for something a bit more luxurious this season.

Polo Ralph Lauren Wool and Silk Crepe Tuxedo Duster Coat

The Row Erdene Single-Breasted Wool Long Coat

There’s a certain refinement to a beautifully tailored coat, especially when the details borrow from formalwear. Think precise cuts, elongated silhouettes, and tuxedo-inspired touches that feel polished enough for an evening out. There’s a more dressed-up sensibility to these classic designs—as seen at Ferragamo, Tory Burch, and Fendi—from the precise fit to the smallest finishing details. This attention to detail brings a beautiful sense of sophistication to workwear and when paired with simple everyday separates.

Gap x Victoria Beckham Wool-Blend Chesterfield Coat

Mackage Elodie-R Double-Breasted Wool-Blend Coat

When a puffer or classic wool coat feels too predictable, a leather coat offers a much bolder alternative. Ralph Lauren, Mugler, and Burberry all embraced waist-defining silhouettes, from classic double-breasted trenches to softer robe-like shapes. In dramatic maxi lengths, the effect is especially sleek, with just the right amount of edge, bringing a bit of bite to winter dressing.

Nili Lotan Arsene Leather Double-Breasted Trench Coat

The Frankie Shop Edra Shearling-Trimmed Wrap Coat

Gray was ubiquitous on runways from New York to Paris, appearing in everything from textured tweed to draped wool and sharply tailored shapes. Whether you prefer deep charcoal or soft dove, it’s an easy neutral to wear in tonal layers or to balance bright colors and prints.

Banana Republic Herringbone Wool-Blend Top Coat

Marant Etoile Pilda Herringbone Double-Breasted Coat

’S Max Mara Scarf-Detail Virgin-Wool Wrap Coat

Faux fur was all over the fall/winter 2026 runways, even in smaller doses like plush collars. If you know that feeling of a sharp chill hitting your neck when the rest of you is bundled up, these collars offer the warmth you need in an ultra-elegant way. On everything from sporty puffers and classic toggle-front wool coats, as seen at Louis Vuitton, to the patterned and pretty pastel trims at Chanel and Gabriela Hearst, it’s a detail that’s as practical as it is beautiful.

Max Mara Medusa Belted Puffer Coat with Faux-Fur Trim

#6851. 34 Unique Christmas Tree Themes That'll Inspire You This Holiday Season

From bejeweled ornaments to popcorn garlands, these ideas bring major holiday cheer.

When it comes to Christmas tree decor, some folks keep it classic, using the same ornaments every year, while others choose new themes. Both approaches capture the charm and spirit of the season. However, if you find yourself in the latter cohort, you know how hard it can be to find yearly inspiration that will wow your guests and blend with your existing decor. So, if you’re in need of a muse, we’ve curated a list of tree themes so stunning, you’ll want to repeat them next year.

While many people stick to the traditional charm of classic hues, this year offers bold alternatives. From glittering jeweled ornaments to vintage-inspired touches like tinsel, popcorn garlands, and flocked branches, social feeds are buzzing with fresh, trendy ideas and new possibilities. There's a lot of Christmas creativity to spread the cheer this year.

To spark your imagination, we've compiled 34 merry and bright Christmas tree themes that are sure to bring jolly spirits. Whether you're drawn to the rustic farmhouse aesthetic or punchy plum color trends, these seasonal styles will infuse your home with magic and joy—and as a bonus, they look fantastic on both real and faux firs.

A Christmas tree can quickly become crowded, so take inspiration from Anthropologie and Terrain's holiday house and swap your standard ornaments for oversized baubles. These larger, multicolored orbs make a true statement, especially when paired with vibrant lights or flocked branches.

Enjoying a warm drink after a long day of skiing was the inspiration for this miniature tree. Featuring gloves, boots, and a Santa with his own pair of skis, take inspiration and decorate a tree dedicated to your favorite seasonal hobby.

For those who like their ornaments to be matchy-matchy, it might be time to start collecting. Anthropologie displayed an adorable miniature tree decked out with festive mice. It's accompanied by a village fit for their Christmas-ready friends.

Ideal for the girly-girl in your life, or maybe even a coastal holiday setting, think outside of the box and abandon the evergreen altogether this year. Instead, take inspiration from design firm BEFoundCollective's white Christmas tree, decorated with cheerful pastel ornaments.

There are few things cozier than a flocked Christmas tree. This one by Pottery Barn is adorned with cozy and metallic ornaments like stars, birds, and even pinecones. This Christmas tree theme is great for a mountain or woodsy cabin, but you can even bring that countryside Christmas energy into your city apartment.

A soothing yet vibrant shade, you can't go wrong with a Christmas tree decked out in peach and orange tones. The silver tinsel contrasts perfectly while adding some glitz and glam. This tree, by the design firm BEFoundCollective in Virginia, showcases vintage ornaments among the lush foliage.

Interior designer Beata Heuman ties dozens of bows on the branches of her showroom's Christmas tree for easy and low-cost DIY decorations. Use ribbons in the traditional Christmas color scheme, match your interior's decor, or, like Heuman, choose hues from the entire rainbow.

Perfect for warmer climates (or those wishing spring would circle back around already), forgo traditional ornaments and adorn your tree with faux florals. Take inspiration from the Winterthur Museum, Garden, and Library in Delaware and use more voluminous blooms like hydrangeas as filler between smaller flowers like mums or daffodils.

A classic color scheme beloved by modern, vintage, and even Art Deco lovers, a black and gold palette is timeless. Make your tree stand tall with a bold trunk cover and top with a snowflake-inspired star to tie your entire decor scheme together.

Topped with a cheerful snowman, this Christmas tree theme is all about the seasonal icons we've come to love. Gnomes and mini Santas stand out against the lush foliage of the tree while striped baubles and twinkling stars glam up the traditional red-and-white color palette.

If your little one is still running around singing "Part of Your World" from The Little Mermaid, pay tribute to their favorite princess with a sophisticated Christmas tree theme. Chock-full of glittering mermaids, chic metal whales, and glossy sea turtles, this tree designed by Pottery Barn is the perfect way to bring the film to life for the holidays.

While winter whites always get the bulk of the attention, blue is the other color of the season we're suggesting you embrace. Opt for chinoiserie-style ornaments and a decadent velvet ribbon garland to make it feel just as cozy as red and green.

Perfect for the diva of the family, this Anthropologie-designed Christmas tree is all about glamorous, textured accents like peacock feathers, Depression-style glass, and velvet accents. Christmas is allowed to be over-the-top!

The summer of Barbie may be over, but that joyous love of all things pink can (and should) continue all year long. If you're feeling as bold as Margot Robbie's iconic character, go all in on a bright pink tree with gold and white accents. Like Barbie, your Christmas tree can be anything you want it to.

If you're always looking at moon phases and tarot cards, bring that love of all things astrological to your Christmas decorations this year. Golden crescent moons, flying doves, and iron-star-studded wreaths are incorporated in this flocked tree. Add warm-hued string lights, and the decorations take on an ethereal glow.

Classics are classics for a reason—and nothing is more instantly festive than a hallmark red-and-white Christmas tree. This bountiful beauty was decked by Inspired by Charm, who included lodge-inspired elements like buffalo check wrapping paper and wooden skis to make it feel fresh and fanciful.

If a streamlined and sleek aesthetic is more your style, use this Christmas tree by For the Home as inspiration. The intentionally sparse look nods to traditional Scandinavian spruces, which feature less dense branches and are left mostly bare save for a few basic ball ornaments and twinkling white lights.

Why choose just two colors when you can have all of them? This Technicolor tree created by Serretta Style leans into a more-is-more aesthetic with tiers of ornaments in every color of the rainbow. While decorations in the same shapes and designs are used here (just in different shades), you could get the same effect by hanging your existing ornaments by color from top to bottom.

The weather outside may be frightful, but it certainly inspires a delightful Christmas tree design! Nod to all things snow and ice with a winter-wonderland-themed evergreen. This scene designed by Lolly Jane combines a flocked tree with an assortment of white and silver ornaments—like plush "snowballs" and frosty pinecones—for a snowy and chic scene. Plenty of warm white lights and a pair of ice skates complete the charming look.

#6850. Culture Watch: What to See and Where to Go in November

Culture Watch is a series spotlighting the intersection of fashion and art, bringing you the latest must-see exhibitions, pop-ups, installations, and more.

November is a good month to make a little room in your calendar. As the holiday season starts to gather pace, galleries, brands, and creative spaces are offering plenty of reasons to get out and look around. In New York, Salon Art + Design returns for its 15th year, while Loro Piana presents its “Master of Cashmere” installation at The Brant Foundation; farther afield, international exhibitions offer even more to explore. Check out all of this month’s best cultural happenings below.

Louis Vuitton Presents ‘La Valise Bruno Aveillan’ at LV Dream

For 13 years, photographer and filmmaker Bruno Aveillan has taken his Louis Vuitton Alzer suitcase to more than 100 destinations, making it a recurring presence in his work. Now, the well-traveled case is at the center of “La Valise Bruno Aveillan,” an immersive exhibition at LV Dream, the house’s encompassing destination in Paris. Aveillan’s photographs appear alongside historic Louis Vuitton trunks and a closer look at the craftsmanship behind the Alzer, the brand’s hard-sided suitcase built around a sturdy wooden frame. The exhibition marks the final chapter in the house’s yearlong celebration of the monogram’s 130th anniversary.

Loro Piana Names Its Cashmere of the Year and Unveils a ‘Master of Cashmere’ Installation

Loro Piana put cashmere in the spotlight at The Brant Foundation in New York, where it marked the 11th edition of its Cashmere of the Year Award. Established in 2015 and held stateside for the first time, the prize recognizes the herders who produce the finest cashmere fiber. Coinciding with the honor, which went to three couples of cashmere herders from Inner Mongolia, is “Master of Cashmere: The Quest for Fiber Excellence,” an installation that traces the material from its origins to the Maison’s work with it. The multi-room experience features archival sketches, explanatory timelines, and, of course, soft-to-the-touch cashmere.

Salon Art + Design Returns to the Park Avenue Armory for Its 15th Year

The 15th edition of Salon Art + Design is now on view at the Park Avenue Armory—and it’s one of its most immersive, expansive editions yet. As is tradition, the event brings together collectible design, fine art, and jewelry. The event is open through November 8. Standouts include Jean Dunand’s 1929 La Forêt lacquered screen, Michel Dufet’s 1918 daybeds, and Jean Royère’s illuminated desk. Fernando Mastrangelo’s totem lamps add a sculptural note, while the jewelry lineup includes pieces from Fernando Jorge, Mellerio, Cora Sheibani, and Viltier.

Lara Saget Debuts Her First New York Solo Show, ‘And the World Came Apart’

Lara Saget brings more than a decade of material experimentation to her first solo exhibition in New York. At Trotter&Sholer’s new Elizabeth Street space, her sculptures and wearable works fuse glass, stone, and metal through heat, pressure, and time. Drawing on geological processes and NASA data, the show moves from ancient natural formations to humanity’s rapid impact on the planet.

The Phillips Collection Opens ‘Avery, Gottlieb & Rothko: By the Sea’

Milton Avery went to Gloucester, Massachusetts, to “live like an artist,” then brought Adolph Gottlieb and Mark Rothko along for summers of creative exchange. In Washington, D.C., The Phillips Collection’s “Avery, Gottlieb & Rothko: By the Sea” brings together watercolors and color-drenched canvases, tracing what those Cape Ann days set in motion.

Tate St Ives Spotlights Wilhelmina Barns-Graham

What does a landscape feel like in the body? Scottish artist Wilhelmina Barns-Graham spent eight decades exploring that question through art shaped by sensation and memory. The retrospective at Tate St Ives in Cornwall, England, is the most comprehensive survey of her work, moving from early landscapes and studio interiors to later works alive with bold color and sweeping gestures.

Taylor Swift, take note: famously hard-to-get-into New York City hotspot The Corner Store has officially landed at The Cosmopolitan of Las Vegas, marking its first-ever location outside Manhattan. Expect its adventurous martinis, signature wood-paneled interiors by Rockwell Group, and a handful of Vegas-exclusive menu additions, including a Butcher’s Counter serving up wagyu tomahawk and porterhouse steaks.

#6848. How Olivia Munn Takes the Edge Off

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

The actress shares the beauty and self-care essentials she swears by, including a $75 volumizing cream

Welcome to Take the Edge Off, a series that breaks down the beauty and self-care routines of influencers, CEOs, experts, and celebrities. Find out how they unwind and decompress, and take a closer look at the holy-grail essentials they’re raving about.

Life can throw unexpected surprises—good and bad—your way, and many lean on wellness practices to stay grounded in chaos. Olivia Munn knows this firsthand.

The actress was diagnosed with early-stage Luminal B breast cancer in early 2023, thanks to what she refers to as the “life-saving” risk assessment test with renowned obstetric and gynecologic surgeon Thaïs Aliabadi at the helm. With a toddler and fresh off a double mastectomy, Munn turned to meditation to find her footing through it all.

“I always associated meditation with having to quiet your mind, and I couldn’t imagine how anyone actually did that, but over time, I’ve come to understand that it isn’t about stopping the noise in your head; it’s about learning not to be carried away by it,” Munn tells Bazaar. “I don’t feel the need to chase every thought or immediately make sense of everything. I can just let things settle without feeling responsible for figuring them all out.”

That same strength and balance are exactly what fashion label Anne Klein wanted to embody when the brand enlisted Munn as ambassador for its fall/winter 2026 collection and to continue spreading her message.

Ahead, we caught up with Munn about what else keeps her present during times of stress, from the manifestation candle she loves to light to words she lives by.

“The future you is depending on the you today to do the right thing.” This means the me today depended on the me three years ago to go to the doctor, get the MRI, and face cancer head-on. I was depending on the me last week, month, or year to start working out more, learn that new language, or organize my closet. When I think about how fast time goes by, and how tomorrow, next month, and next year come in the blink of an eye, it motivates me to take the first step toward whatever I want to do.

A couple of months ago, I began using prescription-strength azelaic acid and tretinoin. I start with azelaic acid, then tretinoin, and finish with the Volume Cream by Adipeau, my holy-grail moisturizer.

My favorite cream right now is Adipeau’s Volume Cream. I’ve reached the point in my life where my hands and neck are showing signs of being crepey. A friend told me about this cream and promised me that if I used it every day for a month, I’d see a difference, and she was right. The science behind it is incredible, so incredible that I often worry about whether the company is doing well because I need them to stay in business forever.

My makeup goal has always been no-makeup makeup. But it is incredibly difficult to master, especially on yourself. My hat’s off to all the influencers who do it so beautifully; I see you and wish I were you.

Shige brand cuticle oil. The amazing manicurist Shige [Taylor] created her own nail line, beginning with an incredible cuticle oil made in Japan. [My] nail bed has never looked or felt so hydrated.

Yes, I light candles from Aloha Elixir. They’re made in Hawaii and are really special, intentional candles.

When I start getting anxiety, which I often do, or have difficulty falling asleep, I do this breathing technique that activates the parasympathetic nervous system, which controls our “fight-or-flight” response: Breathe in through your nose for four beats, hold for seven, breathe out through your nose for eight beats. It calms my system down, and the anxiety goes away, or I drift off to sleep. I do it three to six times, sometimes a little more. But it always works for me.

#6846. Stop Hunting for Pricey Antiques—Designers Say These 9 Humble Finds Give a Home So Much Character

They’re affordable, widely available, and stylish.

It’s any avid thrifter’s dream to stumble upon an extremely valuable piece of decor while browsing the shelves of their favorite antique store. But while you’re hunting for rare treasures, don’t discount the less-expensive finds—they can be the secret to a home that looks instantly filled with character.

“To me, a collected home is much more interesting when everything isn’t perfect or chosen for its monetary value,” designer Lauren Sullivan of Well by Design tells House Beautiful. “Often, it’s the less precious pieces that give a room its personality.”

Even as older generations are continuing to downsize and offload unwanted things to local thrift stores, it still might be hard to find the diamonds in the rough. Keep reading to find out what Sullivan, and a handful of other designers, say you should be on the lookout for on your next thrifting visit.

The most valuable antiques are the ones that have been passed down through generations. In Sullivan’s own bathroom, the designer keeps her great-grandmother’s vanity set on the countertop, its aged finish providing beautiful contrast with the more contemporary marble and finishes. In her bedroom, the same great-grandmother’s small bedside clock found a place on Sullivan’s nightstand.

“Neither is particularly valuable monetarily, but I love incorporating pieces that have been passed down through generations into a home filled with both old and new,” Sullivan says.

Designers love incorporating artwork into homes to add personality and dimension, but Sullivan, as well as designer Becca Meyer of BB Meyer Design, believe that antique shops are where you can really find some hidden gems.

“Some of my favorite pieces of art have come from flea markets or antique shops for very little money,” Meyer says. “I much prefer these to mass produced prints.” You’re more likely to find pieces with aged textures at these types of shops, and that patinaed quality creates an entirely unique look.

Sullivan explains that she’s actually particularly drawn to prints, as well as smaller paintings and silhouettes. She even has an inexpensive print of The Skating Minister in a vintage frame in one of her guest rooms, pictured above. “These smaller, or sometimes quirky, pieces can bring so much personality to a space without requiring a significant investment,” she adds. They may not be originals, but they’re definitely unique.

Old pottery and ceramics are widely available, and they are usually affordable as well. Designer Brooke Snyder of Design Hutch loves how they make any place feel more curated.

The imperfections these handmade pieces carry, from uneven shapes, variations in glaze, or evidence of years of use, are what help these items bring a warmth to a room, particularly when placed near more refined materials, according to Sullivan. They feel humble, maybe even utilitarian, especially when displayed in a kitchen. Or, better yet, used in a kitchen (as long as they’re safe).

“They're beautiful on their own but also so functional for holding utensils or branches or flowers,” Meyer adds. “They make any space feel a little more lived-in and loved and help tell a story—and they don't break the bank!”

“I love incorporating vintage plates, old copper pots, and woven baskets into a home,” explains designer Joe Waroquier. Though these are run-of-the-mill, everyday objects, they can take on and provide an entirely different life when displayed in the home as decor. Mismatched plates on a wall, a collection of copper pots hanging on a rod or resting on shelves in the kitchen, an old crock filled with branches—these items invite in warmth, texture, and more personality.

“I’m particularly drawn to pieces that show their age, whether it’s a little patina, a worn finish, or an imperfect glaze,” he adds. “I love mixing these finds with more contemporary furnishings because that contrast makes a room feel collected and personal rather than overly designed.”

Mirrors can be intriguing focal points of a room, or they can add dimension to a gallery wall. However you want to style them, Snyder loves finding these reflective pieces at antique malls and vintage shops.

“Purchasing something that you don’t see every day from a retailer allows this unique piece to stand out on its own, without feeling like something you see everywhere or feels too simple,” she explains.

As Snyder puts it, antique table lamps provide high visual impact with low investment. Adding one to your accent table or on a bookshelf is an easy way to make your space feel more curated while inviting in another level of ambience with the lighting.

These vintage lamps in the above space, for instance, were bought from a collector and make the primary bedroom feel much more charming and curated. And unlike antique sconces, there’s no need to hardwire these into the wall.

Besides pottery and lamps, designer Ryann Swan Hackett of Ryann Swan Design loves to find and collect other small tabletop decor for her projects. Think candleholders, vintage coffee table books, and trays—things that can layer a little history and individuality into a room or simple vignette.

“If you’re lucky,” Swan Hackett says of the vintage coffee table books, “you’ll find an old inscription inside, which gives the book a story of its own.”

In terms of midsize accent pieces, Meyer says that she has a soft spot for finding antique stools “because you can pop them into any space to add depth and dimension.” Tuck them beside an armchair in the living room, add them to an empty kitchen corner and place a plant on top, or stick one in a bathroom by the tub for somewhere to keep a drink while relaxing. And if you find an upholstered one that could use new fabric, switching it out for a pattern you love is probably worth it.

“These bring a natural warmth that can be difficult to replicate with something new, and their small size makes them fairly reasonable in price for a vintage furnishing piece,” Meyer says.

A larger piece of furniture is likely always going to be more expensive than a smaller one, but designer Yena Jung of By Yena Designs thinks vintage hutches are the versatile antiques people should pay more attention to. They come in many different shapes, styles, and sizes, and can easily make a home in any room of the house.

“A beautiful vintage hutch can add warmth, charm, and a sense of history to a space,” Jung says. “I especially love how their imperfections and natural patina make them feel collected over time.”

#6842. Is Astrology Right or Left?

Like many aspects of American life, astrology is experiencing a political reckoning

In 1988, a former aide to President Reagan alleged that Nancy Reagan relied heavily on the astrologer Joan Quigley to guide key moments in her husband’s presidency. The Reagans denied that characterization, but the image of an easily swayed Nancy Reagan using some irrational force to push through Reagan’s policy decisions entered general pop culture, fueled in part by the misogyny of the political press toward most women in politics, left or right, in the 1980s.

The story of Joan Quigley and Nancy Reagan is part of a larger history of people turning to the stars to help explain, and potentially influence, political events. This is an impulse that doesn’t belong to any particular political ideology. But in current pop culture, astrology is often used as shorthand for liberal or leftist worldviews. Leading up to the 2024 U.S. presidential election, some online astrologers confidently celebrated a potential Harris win. Their despair over the actual outcome was reported in numerous outlets. It seemed to confirm the stereotype of who follows astrology: millennials who are deeply disturbed by the current state of the world.

Today, astrology, like weightlifting, holistic medicine, and organic farming, has become less a hobby and more a political signifier. So many practices that used to be read as lifestyle choices—tracking your macros, for instance, or taking up ceramics—have, in our hyperpolarized culture, become stand-ins for a person’s political leanings. But what’s curious about astrology is that it reads as liberal-coded to those outside the field and potentially conservative to those inside it.

In a 2022 piece titled “Astrology and the Search for Meaning” in The American Conservative, Bradford Tuckfield attributed the rise in astrology to the fact that cultural discourse had “damage[d] or destroy[ed] the …positive myths that used to animate people,” adding, “The patriotic idea of America as a great nation has been undermined by many efforts for decades, the most notable recent example being the ‘1619 Project.’ ” For conservatives like Tuckfield, astrology is an attempt to fill a vacuum left in the American psyche. The absence of religion and the demand that citizens grapple with uncomfortable historic truths supposedly left young people ideologically lost. “The myth of social justice seems to have absorbed much of the energy” of young people, Tuckfield claimed. And astrology is just like that “myth.” For conservatives, then, astrology is just one of many cultural challenges to a homogeneous culture built on the myth of the hero. Astrology, in their reading, leads to challenges to the status quo, which is, by definition, the antithesis of conservatism.

But that’s how outsiders view the field. Astrology also has its own organizational bodies that strive to define the discipline and often are administered by older, more politically and culturally conservative practitioners. But those groups’ influence over astrology has waned as it has become more accessible through the internet. Once-esoteric knowledge is now widely distilled through TikToks and live streams, and this has led to a political divide for many astrologers.

“To be able to study astrology historically has been a very privileged thing to do. And so it’s been dominated by white folks,” explains the astrologer Chani Nicholas. Nicholas is a popular astrologer who trained with those unaffiliated with traditional astrology organizations. “There’s very explicit reasons why I’ve not invested a tremendous amount of time in general astrology communities, even though I am massively indebted to the community and I have a deep appreciation for it,” she says.

Nicholas’s brand of astrology makes explicit connections between planetary transits and real-world political events. She directly cites progressive and leftist political goals in the context of astrology. Nicholas traces the political divide in astrology to post-pandemic America and links it to the same divide seen in alternative health spaces and yoga practices, where many practitioners became enthralled with MAGA and MAHA politics while others did not.

Before the pandemic, organized astrology was dominated by “a group of white people that are mostly able-bodied, mostly heterosexual, mostly cisgendered, that have never had to confront their own racism, sexism, homophobia, transphobia,” she notes. “In the pandemic, a lot of millennials started taking classes with a lot of establishment teachers, and a lot of the racism started to come out.… Millennials were like, ‘I’m not going to sit here and have you say that. You can’t say that.’ And then the more establishment teachers were like, ‘Why are you coming for me?’ ”

This tension between a more conservative old guard in astrology and a younger group of practitioners came to a head this summer when Stephen Forrest, an older practitioner of astrology, appeared on the Lila Astrology podcast. In the episode, he spoke about a satirical character he had once employed to make fun of racism, using the N-word in doing so. Forrest also brought up white-supremacist beliefs he had previously held on the inferiority of Black people’s intelligence; claimed to be related to Nathan Forrester, the founder of the KKK; and repeated the debunked myth that the KKK originally formed as a charity organization and not a terrorist one.

In response, younger astrology adherents asked that Forrest be removed as a speaker from an upcoming conference. Other older practitioners who were more deeply invested in astrology organizations cautioned against “cancellation.” Chani Nicholas, who was scheduled to appear at a conference that was also planning to host Forrest, pulled out of her appearance.

“Astrology attracts all these different types of people. It’s fascinating, but it doesn’t mean that because you’re interested in the human condition that you’re actually interested in interrogating your own biases and your own way of enacting violence on behalf of the system,” Nicholas says.

Astrology organizations, like many institutions currently, are caught between a younger generation, which is asking an older one to abide by the values of fairness and universal acceptance many purport, and older generations that are refusing to see the contradictions and biases in many of their organizations’ stances.

“There was no shared reality about where we had been working,” says Nicholas. “This younger generation of people that were really trying to understand identity and politics and location and power and privilege started to make this robust astrology community [of their own] and really push back against a lot of stuff that had just been existing unchecked for decades.”

Samuel F. Reynolds is an African American astrologer who, for more than 35 years, has chosen to work within astrological organizations. He originally came to astrology as a skeptic. One of the biases he pushes against is the tendency to ground all interpretations in Western culture. “When Uranus went into Libra, an older Western astrologer might say to you, ‘Oh yeah, that led to a time where there was an acceleration of divorces and where people started to gain more power related to marriage,’” Reynolds explains. “When they say things like that, I’m like, ‘Oh, okay, did that happen in Africa? And did that happen in India? Was that true in most of Asia?’ And the data actually is no, it didn’t happen during that same time frame.… It does bring up a philosophical question of what are we talking about and what are we looking at? Because the framework by which we talk about humanity is not quite as vast as astrologers like to believe when they talk about it. That’s the truth of it. You’re talking about the people who are like you or in some way who are adjacent to you.”

He remembers astrology conferences 20 years ago where “every other talk, someone would bring up the charts of the Beatles, Princess Di.” Astrology is an art of interpretation, which rests on the practitioner’s frame of reference. The limited references at conferences spoke to “the insularity in general of white people,” Reynolds says. He does note, though, that at a conference recently, he saw someone comparing the chart of Toni Morrison with Agatha Christie. “Why? I’m not exactly sure,” he jokes.

All of this conflict is happening in the wider context of big cultural and political shifts and the crumbling of any sort of authority or governing bodies. In extreme instability, the desire to understand why everything is changing makes astrology even more attractive. And astrology offers a lens for many to understand themselves. You may not be able to name why you have anxiety or why you tend to have chaotic romantic relationships or why you have trouble forming deep community bonds, but your planetary signature (and those of your friends and lovers) can provide you with something that looks like an answer.

But Nicholas cautions against giving too much sway to the question of transcendence and self-interrogation that astrology can sometimes provide. “I really want to talk about how we can build power and be effective now,” she says. “I don’t want to be out in outer space. I actually really want to be on earth.”

#6841. Here’s Everything We Know About ‘Campus Drivers’ Season 2 (and Which Couple It’ll Be About)

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

Hello to everyone who watched all of Campus Drivers in one day and now urgently need to know what’s going on with season 2: We have answers about what comes next for Lois and Lane and their delightfully trope-y love story.

The first season of Campus Drivers saw Lois break up with her longtime boyfriend and have no choice but to move in with “campus bad boy” Lane in his ultra-fancy apartment. At first Lois can’t stand him, then they become fast friends, then there’s the whole “let’s make out in front of your ex to make him jealous” phase, and finally the season ends with Lane and Lois fully getting together...only for Lane’s mom to enroll him in a new school. Lois literally comes home to a bunch of movers and literally no information from her boyfriend, so yeah, obviously season 2 can’t come fast enough! Here’s what we know.

Not yet. Prime is likely in the “wait and see how it performs” stage of things, but the show is in their top 10, so a renewal seems pretty inevitable at this point.

Lane and Lois obviously have a lot of issues to resolve, but reminder: Campus Drivers is based on a book series. And if season 2 is based on book 2 (aka Crash Course), then a brand-new couple will take center stage: “college bad boy trying to change gears” Donovan and “romance-loving good girl” Carrie. Their general vibe? Let’s dust off the book description.“When Donovan, basketball star and notorious heartthrob of the Campus Drivers, realizes the damage his womanizing behavior has caused, he decides to become the perfect boyfriend. And who better to coach him than romance-obsessed, all-around good girl Carrie? Carrie wants nothing to do with Donovan. She’d much rather lose herself in the heroes from her romance novels. They’re uncomplicated and drama-free, just the way she likes it. If Donovan wants her help, he’ll have to endure a tough, no-nonsense crash course in romance. But one can’t just become a romance hero overnight—especially when reality refuses to stick to the script.”

Time to do some math. According to IMDb, Campus Drivers’ first season was filmed from October 2025 to January 2026. It then dropped in November. This is a pretty fast turnaround, so assuming the show is renewed and filming takes place in early 2027, we could be looking at a late 2027 or early 2028 release date. So, about a year from now (or a little longer).

#6840. The 6 Smartphones to Buy in 2026

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

We don’t care if you’re on team Apple or Android. We’ve found you the phone to upgrade to.

You’ve landed here, so let me guess: You’re an iPhone person and you want a new iPhone. Or ... you’re an Android person, dedicated to your brand of choice already, be it Google or Samsung. Here’s the thing: Your choice of phone says a lot about you, but realistically you’ve already locked yourself into choosing between the same three phones all your friends are getting. We are humans, so our brains are finely tuned to love habits. The result is not too many people want to adjust to a new way of interfacing with daily life every other year; they just want the same thing. There’s a chance you are the rare third kind of person who wants a change. In all three cases, there’s good news. You kinda already know what you want (and don’t want) out of a smartphone.

My fellow editors and I have tried the latest in what Apple, Samsung, and Google have to offer, and have determined for ourselves which are the greatest. This year, the biggest shake-up to the race is foldable phones. Not entirely a new category, but the iPhone Duo is the capstone on a past 12 months that have legitimized the form across both Apple and Android devices. You’ll be paying a premium, and not all the software is properly optimized yet, but the tech is finally there. For everyone else, there’s still the new pro model iPhone or the latest A series Pixel for the value hunters. Regardless of whether you’re after a bold reinvention, or just an improved camera and a slightly shinier display, you’ll find something to love in these six smartphones.

I’ve made vocal arguments in favor of going against the grain and getting a Windows device instead of a MacBook, but at the end of the day it’s all too easy to stick with it if you’re on team iPhone already. The 18 Pro is the iPhone to get if you’re a daily power user, on your phone 24/7 for both work and personal reasons. It’s got a glorious 120-hertz-refresh-rate retina screen, exactly the smoothness and crispness you’ve come to expect from a pro level phone.

It’s not merely the one we’ve seen the most in the wild, it’s the best bet for an iPhone that will last you another four years. And while we can't say we love the new design, it’s been easy to get used to. And functionally, it’s the iPhone Pro we know and love. It is fast, it’s got a smooth 120 hertz screen and it has one of the best cameras you can get on a phone. If there’s one upgrade we truly feel each time we get a new phone, it’s the camera. This year’s is no exception.

Compared with the other Android offerings out there, the Pixel 11 Pro is in elite territory. It’s not technically as beefy as a Samsung Galaxy S26 Ultra or ZFold 8 Ultra, but it’s notably a lot cheaper than either of those phones.

It’s fast and it’s got that smooth refresh rate a high-end phone should. Best of all, holy shit is the camera something else? It can take one incredible photo after another and it’s probably equipped with the best selfie camera on a phone. Our editors who’ve tested this one also won’t shut up about how much better Gemini is than every other AI assistant. Finally, a “smart” feature that lives up to the moniker. And compared to the equivalent iPhones and Samsung phones, the battery life reigns supreme—even if, as a trade-off, it takes the longest to charge.

If you’re of the Android persuasion, it’s easy to see the iPhone 18 Pro as the other team’s Google Pixel 11 Pro, instead of the other way around. On a pure specs level it’s a better phone—just probably not better enough to get Apple diehards over the OS gap. Serious sarcasm aside, it’s not the iPhone users who need this phone. It’s everyone currently suffering on an older Pixel model or, worse, some no-name Android phone.

It was only a couple of years ago you could read me advising you not to buy a renewed iPhone. But with how expensive all technology has become recently, there is now a wide enough gulf between something like this and a new iPhone to make buying refurbished worth the cost savings. Instead of buying a 17e new for $700, pick up this refurbished 16e—it’s half the price, and you won’t be living too far in the past.

The 16e is pretty much everything a phone should be and nothing extraneous. This phone is light, yet durable. It’s got the Action Button from the 16 lineup but does lack the Camera Control.

The other omission of note is the lack of MagSafe functionality. The 16e supports wireless Qi charging, but it does not magnetize the way every iPhone since the 12 (barring the SE line) has. Once again, we don’t think this matters to the core audience for this phone, so it’s less of a flaw and more of a way Apple has honed in on exactly the audience it wants to hit for this one.

For $600, this is the most affordable big-name smartphone. There are plenty of Androids in the $200 to $300 range, but by and large they aren’t meant to last. They are poorly made stopgaps to get you to something like the Pixel 10a. Right now for only $400, this is the best value you can get on a brand-new smartphone.

It has a lot of what we love from the newest Pixel phones—including the latest OS features like Gemini and the invaluable Call Screening feature that automatically detects spam. And while the front-facing camera isn’t mind-blowing compared with the 10 series Pixels, it does still rock a very impressive wide-angle back camera that offers more variation than the e series of iPhones.

The iPhone Duo is the most innovative and groundbreaking phone release of the year. We said as much in our gadget awards and editor Luke Gullory has been giving his updates along the way. He’s tested and tested, and the verdict is in. This one is a keeper and, most importantly, it’s the best foldable phone to hit the market yet.

The genius touch here is the aspect ratio. For the Duo, Apple went with a √2:1 (or 1.414:1) aspect ratio. That is slightly different from the other foldable, but the subtle change makes a difference. On the Duo, the small outside screen exactly mirrors the size of half the large inner screen, but it also exactly mirrors the total aspect ratio of the entire large inner screen. The matte screen seems odd at first, but it’s the solution Apple has found to showing any creasing and, once again, this gives it that design edge. There’s a reason people start to care when Apple does it.

I’ll also note it has the same chipset and specs as the 18 Pro, including RAM. Normally, this would put it on par power-wise, but the real-world performance lags behind due to the amount of thermal throttling needed to pull off the design miracle of the form factor.

If you want a flip phone, you can shop vintage for pennies but when it comes to using those like daily phones, the truth is they don’t support modern cellular networks. They are as good as a toy. But if you still want that flip phone experience, Samsung’s most affordable new folding phone gives you something of the sort.

Don’t get me wrong, the Z Flip8 is still expensive, starting at $1,200. But compared to all the other foldables from Samsung and Google, it is technically the least expensive one. That’s largely because it’s got the least amount of screen space. Really it’s just a normal-sized Galaxy phone with a front-screen, not something that has a unique aspect ratio like the Duo or the Galaxy Z Fold 8 phones. On a specs level, it’s on par with these phones. Crisp 120 hertz screen, solid front and rear cameras, and all the Android app support you’d want. Really, though, if you’re committing it’s a commitment to having a wacky, folding flip phone that you can set up like a tent or show off all the different camera modes.

#6835. Taylor Swift’s 2026 CMA Performance Has Swifties Convinced Something Big Is About to Happen

Happy Friday the 13th to all who celebrate, but especially Taylor Swift, who just announced her major return to the Country Music Association Awards. This year’s show is set to take place at the Bridgestone Arena in Nashville on Wednesday, November 18.

#6834. This Colorful South Carolina Home Marries Mediterranean Elegance With Lowcountry Charm

Designer Megan Molten worked handmade details and custom elements into every room.

How do you blend the magical ease of Mediterranean life with the coastal charm of South Carolina’s lowcountry? After an enchanting vacation in Greece with their three daughters, Megan Molten’s clients wanted their new build on Daniel Island to capture that unique, transportive feeling they fell in love with—and they asked the Charleston-based designer to help them achieve it.

Alongside builder Wes Whittle of EasTech Property Development, Molten was involved from the very beginning, crafting the blank canvas into a residence that balanced the sun-drenched elegance of the Greek islands with traditional Lowcountry living. The designer went all in on every detail, relying on high-quality craftsmanship, custom pieces, and handmade elements to bring life to every room.

Upon entry, guests are greeted by a hand-painted, botanical-themed mural by local artist Jenn Griffith. “The mural’s objective was very specific,” says Molten. “The homeowners wanted a design that captured elements of both the Mediterranean and the Lowcountry.” Turn to the other side and find a plaster-covered stairwell by artist Emily Pope Harris, an addition that sets the tone for the whole home. Move further into the home and find a hand-painted floor that shines in the craft room, created using a stencil from Etsy and a few Sherwin-Williams paint colors—it’s playfully fabulous and still somehow timeless. “Clients do not typically splurge in rooms that are not front and center, so this was a real treat to bring to life, especially knowing how much their daughters love to craft,” the designer adds.

Elsewhere, a larger portion of the budget was split between the lighting and the kitchen. “Statement lighting in the house was always prioritized,” Molten says. “The homeowners also agreed that the lighting was important and needed to be special. I was finally able to use some light fixtures that I have been eyeing for years!”

As for the kitchen, it was more difficult to know where to splurge and where to save, a predicament the designer finds herself with on most projects. However, looking now at the honed limestone backsplash, green quartzite countertops with an integrated sink, and a plaster hood by Pope Harris, it’s clear that she made the right choices.

Even with all the home’s beautiful elements, the fan-favorite among Molten’s team ended up being the pool just outside the kitchen. It’s lower than most Molten has worked on in Charleston, making it one of the most unique she’s seen. Flanked by archways and paired with two spas and a raised hot tub, it fully captures a Grecian grotto feel.

Even with all the one-of-a-kind features and elevated furnishings, the home is a place where life is meant to be experienced to the fullest. “The spaces are to be enjoyed without hesitation,” Molten explains, “creating a beautiful, practical environment for a life well lived.”

FAST FACTSDesigner: Megan MoltenArchitect: DLB Custom Home DesignBuilder: EasTech Property DevelopmentLocation: Daniel Island, South CarolinaThe Space: A six-bedroom, seven-bathroom new build spanning 6,000 square feet.

The beauty of this entryway starts outside with the front door. Made locally by Maebeck Doors, it’s an arched shape with a grid pattern that serves as a charming “wow” moment upon entering the home.

Once inside the house, the awe continues, from the curved plaster stairwell to the custom botanical mural by artist Jenn Griffith. “The homeowners wanted a design that captured elements of both the Mediterranean and the Lowcountry,” Molten adds. The final touch? A stone table in the notch at the base of the stairs, perfect for placing keys or bags at the end of the day.

Molten’s client wanted a large, comfortable sectional that could easily seat her family and any guests, so when the team saw these two at High Point Market, they knew they were perfect for the space. A travertine table, nestled between the two sectionals, made the pieces feel like one long, continuous design, and both were upholstered in Crypton performance fabric for added durability.

To complement the space, Molten added a plush armchair and topped the room off with a modern light fixture that doesn’t add much noise. “The artwork above the sectional is from the homeowner’s own collection,” Molten says. “It served as the catalyst for the colorways used throughout the home.”

A calming, functional space, the brightness of the kitchen is achieved not through a classic all-white kitchen, but through shades of light green, from the quartzite countertops to the greenish-gray cabinets. Rattan pendant lights bring in that casual Mediterranean feel, especially since the floor-to-ceiling windows on the left overlook the pool below.

To keep it family-friendly, Molten applied one of her favorite tricks: leather on the counter stool seats and fabric on the back. “Wiping off messes and letting the leather patina is both practical and pretty,” she adds.

This dining nook really needed to be functional, since it’s where Molten’s clients and their three children sit for breakfast every morning and dinner every night. Durability for daily use played a huge part in the furniture choices; they sourced a long wooden table and used the same seating upholstery trick here as they did with the counter stools in the kitchen. For a touch of whimsy, Molten crowned the nook with a coco bead chandelier.

Instead of plaster on the double-sided fireplace as she had initially intended, Molten went with a zellige tile façade that produces a lovely shine.

Easily accessible from the living room, kitchen, and screened-in porch, this breakfast area—complete with a full bar—is perfect for hosting. Anyone can come in and make themselves a drink whenever they want.

Arched upper cabinets with lattice details create more movement in this space, as well as add in charm, and the chandelier has been on the designer’s list of favorites for a while. “I particularly love the contrast of the black countertop with the coral cabinets,” Molten says.

The first thing Molten selected for the scullery was the charming lattice window over the sink looking out onto the yard. Then came the bold wallpaper, which inspired the vibrant cabinet paint color, balanced out with a light Calacatta marble countertop with hints of yellow and gold throughout.

“This scullery is a very functional space that houses their microwave, coffee maker, refrigerator, and drawers with snacks, all built-in and panel-ready,” Molten says.

When her clients told her they wanted a dedicated craft room for their girls, Molten was just as excited at the prospect as their daughters. “A dedicated specialty room like this typically provides a great opportunity for lots of creativity and color,” she explains.

They started with the colorful striped wallpaper on the ceiling, pulling colors from it for the built-in cubby shelf and the painted floors. A neutral light fixture keeps the room from feeling too busy, giving it a more timeless feel. “The floor design is so special, and a bit of a splurge,” Molten adds. “I was so pleased the client embraced this idea!”

The unique wallpaper served as the starting point for this powder bath, as Molten designed everything else around it. “The Jamie Young mirror looks like an oyster shell, and the floating stone was a nice feature,” she says. “Painting the trim and ceiling the pink shell color made the wallpaper, tile, and stone come together.”

Molten had never done a wallpaper border before, but she adores how it turned out in one of the daughter’s bedrooms. “It’s a great way to add thoughtful design details to a room and is cost-effective too, as opposed to covering the full room in wallpaper,” she says. The trim color was pulled from this border, which she paired with a signature shade of pink from her own collection with Sherwin-Williams.

“For the daughter’s bathroom, we opted for a tub/shower combo to accommodate young children,” Molten explains. These children-friendly details are found elsewhere in the room too, like a pull-out step stool under the sink cabinets.

The pink-and-green palette from the daughter’s bedroom is continued here, though in slightly more saturated shades. A local Charleston brand, Mirth Studio, created the painted wood valance to pair with the Pepper Home shower curtain, and Pope Harris continued her plaster work here, covering a Visual Comfort & Co. sconce over the mirror in a custom pink hue.

Located on the small third floor, the only rooms that occupy this level are the office, the office den, and a guest suite, making it a quiet place to escape when the clients, who share this space, need to get work done. “Our client loved the Tiger Palm wallpaper, so we applied that on all of the walls and at the low part of the angled ceiling to help mask all of the ceiling lines in this room,” Molten says. They even have their own wet bar up here for post-work happy hour.

Designed as a dedicated no-kids zone off the office, this private room is where Molten’s clients get away to watch movies after bedtime, decompress after a long day, and just retreat to when they need time to themselves.

“We opted for a performance velvet on the sofa with velvet textured pillows to create a luxurious, cozy feel,” the designer says. “I love the Currey & Co. chandelier with green glass—it works so perfectly in this small room.”

“Like the primary bedroom and primary bathroom, the closet is not huge, but it’s efficient and thoughtfully designed,” Molten explains. With her team, they designed a wall of built-ins and paid close attention to the functionality of each drawer and shelf while maximizing the space; then a local cabinet maker brought them to life.

Molten chose soft, luxurious carpet that feels like stepping into a high-end department store and a fabulous Made Goods light fixture.

The architect who worked on this house, Damien Busillo of DLB Custom Home Design, had this lower-level pool incorporated in the original plans, and Molten was able to assist with the tile selections.

“This is such a creative way to use empty space under the house,” she says. “Homes in Charleston are often raised when built due to coastal flooding; however, this was the most interesting and exciting design concept I have seen for this area of the home.” It truly captures that Grecian grotto vibe.

Megan Molten has always been creative, but her love of interiors didn’t fully manifest until she bought and decorated her first house in Charleston, South Carolina. From there, she started experimenting with design as a hobby, sharing her eye with friends and family, before devoting all her time to it and finally deciding to open her own eponymous firm. Now, she shares her elevated coastal style and luxurious Lowcountry vibe with clients across the country.

#6833. Amazon Has Some Incredible Deals on Levi’s Right Now

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

It's easy for me, the style writer, to say you shouldn't determine whether or not you buy something based on how much it is (or is not) on sale. I know how the real world works, though. I write about denim all the time, and I use every opportunity I get to recommend the fancy stuff. Honestly though, I spend most days in a pair of $40 Wranglers that I bought on sale for $25, because I also am a regular American trying to get by. Used to be, good denim only cost a few bucks. Now, it's lower quality and costs more money. Reproductions of what used to be American working clothes now easily cost $300 or more. C'est la vie.

But I'm not here to talk about all that. I'm here to say like with my Wranglers, it's OK to wear cheap jeans. You can still be stylish if your pants cost $50. This month’s early Black Friday Amazon deals on Levi’s prove it.

We've got the 505, the modern Levi's fit I think works for most men, that's selling for between $45 and $55—depending on the wash you want and size you need. The 517, Levi's take on a Western cut, is going for a discount. And the 501—which I don't love in its modern iteration, but cannot deny the popularity of—is hovering just below $50, depending on the size and wash. Depending on the jeans you buy, you’re getting a discount of between 19 and 43 percent for the best cheap denim out there. If you're looking for a new jacket, which Levi's still makes decent versions of, Amazon has deals on two sherpa trucker jackets. Go with the classic denim for $63 or opt for the corduroy for $85.

Alright, that's enough explaining. Pick what speaks to you. Buy it. Then, go forth a new man with refreshed style.
