#6829. The Secret to Kendall Jenner’s ‘Me Mode’

The model and founder shares her favorite ways to unwind and how the Therabody TheraFace Mask Glo fits into her nightly routine.

For Kendall Jenner, “Me Mode” is a time to unplug from the daily demands of work and social obligations and simply be still. “I have a really busy mind, so switching off has never come naturally to me,” says the model and entrepreneur. “I’ve had to build little rituals that help me slow down.”

One of those rituals involves Therabody’s TheraFace Mask Glo, an LED face mask that bridges skincare with wellness. Delivering full LED coverage across the entire face, including the lips and under eyes, the cordless device features 504 red, infrared, and blue LEDs, plus a relaxing scalp massage to help relieve tension. Best of all, treatment time is only 12 minutes, giving Jenner a quick, easy, and much-needed pause from her busy schedule.

“After a long day, it’s sweatpants, my dogs, and zero plans,” Jenner says. “Putting on the Mask Glo has kind of become my signal that the day is over. For those 12 minutes, my brain finally goes quiet.”

One thing Jenner loves about the TheraFace Mask Glo is how effortlessly it fits into her routine. “It’s hands-free, so I just put it on and do whatever I feel like that night,” Jenner says. “Sometimes that’s music or journaling, and a lot of the time it’s doing absolutely nothing. The scalp massage is my favorite part. It feels less like a skincare step and more like actually relaxing.”

It offers legitimate skincare benefits, too. Backed by the largest clinical study of any LED mask on the market, the TheraFace Mask Glo was shown to reduce under-eye wrinkles and fine lines, enhance radiance, and lift sagging skin in as little as eight weeks.

The TheraFace Mask Glo offers three LED settings to target different skin concerns. Red light helps improve the appearance of fine lines, wrinkles, and dark spots, while red and infrared light work together to boost collagen production. Blue light targets acne-causing bacteria, helping prevent future breakouts.

The key is consistent use, which Jenner has found easy to maintain. “If a routine is too complicated, I’m not going to stick with it, and I’ve learned that consistency matters more than anything,” she says. “With the Mask Glo, I pick red, red and infrared, or blue light depending on how my skin is acting, and that’s it. It’s easy enough that I actually use it, which is half the battle for me. It comes with me when I travel now, too.”

The LED lights are placed across the entire face, even covering overlooked areas like the mouth and eyes. The preset treatment runs for 12 minutes, cycling through four minutes of each light setting. Four-minute individual treatments are also available.

Combining advanced skincare and relaxation into one simple step, the TheraFace Mask Glo is ideal for anyone who wants to level up their nighttime routine with minimal effort. As Jenner has learned over the years, less is more when it comes to beauty and wellness.

“I’ve been sitting in makeup chairs since I was 14, so I went through a phase of trying everything,” she says. “Now I do a lot less, and I’m more intentional about what I put on my skin. I try to give it a break when I can, and I’ll use the Mask Glo before glam on shoot days.”

Jenner’s nighttime routine is just one aspect in living a more holistic lifestyle. “Wellness for me is mostly about balance now: being at the barn with my horses, getting outside, and protecting my nights,” she says. “My friends make fun of me for going to bed so early, but I’m not changing.”

#6828. How Does Diplo Survive a Weekend Running, DJing, and Partying? His Whoop Data Tells the Story.

The 47-year-old DJ shared his health stats from a frenzied weekend that could’ve killed any normal person. Here’s how he does it without coming apart at the seams.

On a Saturday morning in August, at exactly 10:00 a.m., August Thomas Wesley Pentz, the DJ known as Diplo, stood outside the Barlume Beach Club in Montauk, Long Island, and delivered a sermon to hundreds of runners. “I’m very tired; I slept three hours for you guys,” he shouted through a megaphone, dressed in gray cargo shorts, a tank top, and pink aviator shades. “But now we’re ready to run. Let’s have a good time.”

Then one of the world’s most prolific DJs launched himself into a 5K through the sand and rocks of Gin Beach. It was a run-club event organized by A Team, a dating app for the wellness obsessed, and Diplo’s Run Club, which stages nine group runs across the U.S. (and, soon, Europe).

As I watched him that sunny and hot morning, I couldn’t stop thinking: How the hell is this guy still standing, let alone leading a run? It was only Saturday, and Diplo was already deep into a frenzied weekend. He landed in New York City on Thursday morning after taking the overnight flight from his home in Malibu. That evening he DJed from 12:30 a.m. to 3:00 a.m. at the Edge, a venue 100 floors above the city. On Friday, he boarded a seaplane for the Hamptons, where he performed two more sets: a sunset show at Surf Lodge, where Jimmy Buffett and Snoop Dogg have played to crowds of models and billionaires, followed by an impromptu set at Mary Lou’s, a Montauk club on the harbor where he only meant to hang out but ended up taking the decks. He didn’t leave until around 1:00 a.m.

I was at both venues and felt deeply, existentially hungover the next morning. I figured—hoped, really—the run would include some walking. Diplo, however, led the pack. He wore a Whoop, the health tracker with which he has a partnership, and reached a heart rate of 167 beats per minute. “He wasn’t taking it easy at all,” said Greg Grosicki, a staff research scientist at Whoop who reviewed Diplo’s data from his weekend in Montauk.

I didn’t understand how a 47-year-old pulled off this feat—the travel, the DJ sets, the cavorting, the running—without collapsing and retreating to his hotel suite for the rest of the weekend. On Saturday evening he had two more sets, one at the annual gala of the Surfrider Foundation, which supports ocean conservation and includes Sean McPherson and John Slattery as supporters. After that he DJed a private party for investment executive Michael Dorrell before flying private to Ibiza overnight for his next gig.

After the run—which, miraculously, I did finish—I talked with Diplo about how he survives this lifestyle. He even shared his Whoop data with me from the weekend so I could compare his body’s reaction with how he told me he felt. “I’m actually fully cracked out every day, doing way more than running and DJing, more than anybody else,” he said.

He likes to sound nonchalant about it. “When I did an interview with some of the Whoop counselors, they were like, your body is very similar mentally to a Navy SEAL or an ER nurse. You’re able to just get up and go do whatever,” he said. “I can always get up and get shit done, unless I’m sick.” Okay, so maybe Diplo was just born with it. But I learned that he does more for his physical and mental health than he likes to let on. This is the story of how a 47-year-old, internationally renowned DJ, who works and parties into the predawn hours, travels the world, and leads packs of people on runs, pulls it off without coming apart at the seams.

Running appeals to him because it travels well. All you need is a pair of shoes and the will to put them on. “I do things I can do anywhere,” he said. “Running is free.” He aims for 15 miles a week across three or four runs, averaging a seven-and-a-half to eight-minute mile. His fastest mile, six and a half minutes, came after his run club appeared on the prediction platform Polymarket, where people bet against him. “That’s what motivated me to run pretty fast,” he said with a laugh.

DJing counts as cardio too. After all, his sets typically run at least two hours. “It’s a mix of dancing and standing,” he said. “It’s almost like a little air squat.” He’s baffled that his dancers, several from the group Major Lazer, stay in shape without a gym. “I’m like, ‘What do you guys do?’ ” he said. “They’re like, ‘We don’t do anything. We just dance at your shows.’ ”

The numbers back him up: During his Surf Lodge set, his max heart rate hit 117 bpm. “If you consider these sets are two hours long, maintaining a heart rate of 120 is a real physical activity,” Greg Grosicki, the research scientist at Whoop, told me. “It’s equivalent to riding a bike for two hours. You feel pretty wrecked after that, and he’s getting equivalent strain from his sets.”

Diplo mixes cardio and weights in HIIT sessions and does 100 to 200 push-ups a day, several times a week, usually in his hotel room. “It takes five minutes of your life,” he said.

Once a week he wears an electrical muscle stimulation, or EMS, suit for 20 minutes. “It does pulsing with simple moves,” he said, naming a brand called Catalyst and a newer one, Pepper, he’s about to try. He credits the suit, half jokingly, for his arm muscles.

He also takes hot Pilates classes with light weights and works out at Barry’s Bootcamp near his home in Malibu. “There’s an unwritten rule about group fitness in California: You don’t bother celebrities,” he said. “You let them get their job done, then you tell people you saw Chris Martin. Every time I go to Pilates, he’s next to me.”

According to the data, long flights, especially red-eyes, hit Diplo hard, said Grosicki. After an overnight flight to Ibiza, Diplo’s recovery score—a measure of how ready the body is to perform—dropped to 21 percent, and his sleep score to 29 percent. That’s well below average for him. “He’s human, and he’s responding to these stressors,” Grosicki said.

“I do like to get the red-eyes, which probably messes up my sleep the most, but I’d rather be outside in the day,” Diplo said. “Being on a plane during the day is so depressing.” He said he struggles to hold a circadian rhythm and sometimes crashes in the midafternoon. On Thursday, for example, he slept five hours in the middle of the day ahead of his set at the Edge.

Winding down after a set is its own challenge. “I might take a Xanax if I have to,” he said. “I got to stop thinking about things.” He also puts his phone away, listens to audiobooks and history podcasts, skips food before bed (though he does chug electrolytes), and uses PEMF mats, which send low-frequency electromagnetic waves through the body to aid in relaxation. Mostly, though, he just takes what sleep he can get.

Saturday morning, after two Friday-night sets in Montauk, his recovery score was 67 percent and his sleep score 65 percent, both above average. On Monday, after he spent the night in Ibiza, his recovery score was 56 percent and sleep score was 62 percent. As Grosicki put it, “He bounces back.”

Diplo avoids drinking before shows and on travel days, and he hasn’t done recreational drugs in six months. When he does, it’s acid. “I would run on acid, just try things on acid,” he said. “I could fly a plane on acid.”

“Mental health is pretty much everything,” Diplo said. “But you have to get there sometimes by exercising. Finding your body, being in tune with your body, is probably the first thing you can do to have a clear mind, to get rid of any sickness you have, whatever’s keeping you down. That’s the first way to start.”

He’s tried more unconventional resets too. Seven years ago, he smoked toad venom. “I’ll never do it again, but it was pretty hardcore,” he said. “It’s a really big ego death. Being humbled is very good wellness.”

Whatever he’s doing, it seems to be working, at least by the numbers. Whether the rest of us should try it is a different question entirely.

#6826. The Saga of Former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang’s Fake Leather Jacket

The Internet loved his outrageous new look. There was just one problem.

There are several ways to respond when you’ve admitted to an affair, your company’s shares are tumbling, and even the White House is discussing your private life. Turning up in a spectacularly studded Saint Laurent leather jacket is one of them. Resigning is another.

For a while, it seemed as if Jensen Huang, the 63-year-old former CEO of Nvidia, had opted for the former. A photograph showing the tech billionaire in an archival Hedi Slimane YSL biker jacket went viral this week. The Internet was delighted. There was just one problem: The photograph was fake.

The resignation, which came just this morning, was real.

As CNN reported, the image had been doctored from a genuine photograph taken at a Seoul airport in June. Nvidia, which one might imagine has other things to deal with right now, confirmed it wasn’t real.

But perhaps the reason so many people believed it was that Huang already dresses like this. Leather jackets are his uniform. He reportedly owns 30 or 40 of them, including a snazzy reptile-effect number worn to deliver his company’s keynote at the Consumer Electronics Show.

And Hedi Slimane was the perfect designer to bring into the fantasy. The Frenchman, who ran Saint Laurent from 2012 to 2016, made his name dressing skinny rock stars and turning their disheveled, devil-may-care attitude—later rebranded as so-called “indie sleaze”—into expensive luxury fashion. His leather jackets were practically a uniform for men who were determined to look as though they didn’t care what anyone thought and/or having a midlife crisis.

For a billionaire in the middle of a spectacularly public scandal, it was quite a look. One Instagram commenter described Huang as the first person to achieve “negative aura” in archival Hedi.

There’s another irony. Huang has previously said his wife, Lori—i.e., the wronged party in all this—helps choose his clothes.

And then there’s the technology itself. Nvidia makes the chips that power the AI revolution, including the tools capable of ... producing convincing fake photographs.

In conclusion, then, the image wasn’t real. But as a portrait of a billionaire in the middle of a spectacularly public crisis, we so wanted it to be.

#6823. Birkenstock Clogs Are Easier to Dress Up Than You Think

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Birkenstock clogs have never really gone out of style, but when skinny jeans were replaced with wide-leg and bootcut jeans, they firmly reclaimed their place on the shoe rack. Now that sneakers go way more places than the gym and the grocery store, you should give your Birkenstocks another glance and see your Bostons as a fashion opportunity.

Plus, Birkenstocks have gone far beyond the taupe Boston—the label has made clog variations like the Mary Jane-inspired Maria and modern Loma. Or if you’re still partial to the Boston, you can get the clog style in a variety of cool colors, like Oyster and Thyme, and variations, like big buckles or braided straps.

If you’re not sure how you can transition your Bostons from your throw-on, afterthought shoe, we’re here to help. Below, I’ve compiled some outfit inspiration for you and the pieces you need to re-create it.

Birkenstocks at the office? Absolutely—they are clogs after all, and people wear sneakers to the office all the time. Why wear those flats that give you blisters when your Bostons are right there? Pair them with a coordinating trench and white wide-leg jeans (because we haven’t been following that archaic white-after-Labor-Day rule all fall).

Bernardo Belted Stretch Cotton Trench Coat

Even supermodel and Guest In Residence founder Gigi Hadid can’t resist a comfy pair of Birks. For a perfectly executed, effortless, and casual look, pair your Bostons with a graphic tee and bootcut jeans. Create some visual interest by tying your cardigan or sweatshirt like Hadid, instead of simply around your neck. Now that Bostons are everywhere again, opt for a color like Oyster to set yourself apart from the Taupe pack.

Birkenstocks with a matching sweater set? Hear me out—I’m going to cite stylist Allison Bornstein’s “wrong shoe theory.” It’s been around for a while, but wearing a historically “wrong” shoe with an outfit is often so, so right. You probably have never thought about pairing your Birkenstocks with a skirt, but if you go monochrome, like I’m suggesting, it creates a visually interesting outfit. I suggest opting for Birkenstock’s Maria, a Mary Jane-esque variation on the Boston, if you want a sleeker silhouette.

Everyone has a comfy errands uniform—myself included—but instead of slipping on those track pants or leggings with your Birks for the umpteenth time, maybe elevate your look with some silk pants and a designer suede tote or deviate from the Birkenstock Boston. I recommend checking out the Loma, a new wrap-style clog silhouette from the brand, a modern and playful take on the classic Boston.

Take a page out of this off-duty model’s playbook. Don’t be afraid to mix neutral tones, like cream, black, and gray. The mélange of textures—the suede clogs, colored denim, heather polo, and quilted bag—creates an effortlessly cozy look. Trust me, you’ll be wearing this on repeat on the weekends this fall and winter.

#6821. You're Shopping for Mattresses All Wrong—an Expert Says You Need to Try the "One-Roll" Test

It’s the best way to mimic how you’d actually use it at home.

Buying a mattress is not an easy decision, especially if you want a change from your current model or you’re upgrading to a different style to address aches and pains. The process feels high-stakes, which is why our editors are enthusiastic about testing and recommending mattresses, plus sharing any tips and tricks we learn along the way to prevent buyer’s remorse. Recently, we learned that picking out a bed simply based on its designated labels—twin, full, queen, king, and Cal king—may not be the most efficient way to buy a mattress.

Instead, Paul Keenan, store manager at U.K.-based brand Linthorpe Beds, suggested several sleep tests to gain more insight into buying the perfect mattress for your needs. “Most people know whether they sleep on a [full, queen or king-size mattress], but far fewer have ever checked whether those dimensions actually suit their body, sleeping habits and bedroom,” he notes. “Bed size is really easy to overlook because most of us shop using familiar labels like queen or king rather than thinking about the actual measurements we need.”

He goes on to suggest that your height, how much you move at night, and whether you share the mattress with someone else can all affect how much space feels comfortable. “There are some very simple checks you can carry out at home before deciding whether your current setup is right for you,” he says. Read ahead to try out his easy “tests” before you buy your next mattress.

This is a test you can try in stores if you’re testing the mattresses there, or at home if you’re doing a trial run with the mattress you just bought. These days, most brands offer at least 30 days of testing to see if you like the bed. To start, position yourself in the part of your bed you’d normally sleep on. Then, roll naturally into another comfortable position, such as from your back onto your side.

“Don’t deliberately tuck your arms or legs in to make yourself fit; the aim is simply to recreate the sort of movement you would make during the night,” says Keenan. “We move around far more in our sleep than we realize, so whether you can simply lie on a mattress isn’t the only thing to think about.

Most people, when testing a mattress in stores, just plop down horizontally and get up versus tossing and turning. The key is to shift your whole body into different positions, ideally with your partner next to you, to see if you both fit on the bed. If you sleep alone, you’re in the clear.

“If one normal turn takes you straight to the edge or into the person next to you, that can be a useful sign that the amount of usable space is more restricted than it feels when you’re lying still,” Keenan says. “It isn’t a strict pass or fail test, but it gives you another reason to look at the actual width you have available [versus relying on the categories of full, queen, king, etc].”

This one-roll test is helpful because, while mattress sizes are standardized, the physical size of two people sharing the bed changes how spacious a mattress can feel. Thus, what works for one couple may not work for another, so the one-roll test will specifically tell you if your mattress is too small, too big, or just the right size for your needs.

According to Keenan, there are several other tests you can try to determine if a mattress is right for you. One is called the 30-second “elbow test.” Both sleepers should lie on their backs, place their hands behind their heads, and allow their elbows to point naturally outwards. “If their elbows immediately touch or overlap, it may be a sign they would benefit from more width,” says Keenan. “It takes about 30 seconds, but it gives you a quick visual indication of how much personal space you actually have.”

Both the aforementioned tactics address width, but we can’t forget about height. As a general rule, add at least six inches to the tallest person in bed to ensure their feet do not hang off or press against a footboard. “The easiest way to check is to measure your own height against the length of the mattress, which will show exactly how much clearance is left between your height and the end of the bed,” says Keenan. “If you’re approaching six feet or taller, measure the mattress rather than relying on its label. You ideally want some room beyond your actual height, and the test takes less than a minute.”

Lastly, if you want a simple way to visualize how much space two people will actually share on the mattress, just place two standard pillows side by side on the bed. “The two-pillow test is a really simple way of putting the dimensions into perspective because most people have probably never thought about the width of their pillows in relation to the mattress underneath them,” Keenan explains. “If you already feel like you’re competing for space, seeing that comparison for yourself can make the dimensions much easier to understand. It doesn’t mean everybody sleeping on a double needs to size up, but it does show why the name of a bed size doesn’t necessarily tell you how spacious it will feel when two people are sharing it.”

Before you buy your next mattress, try these simple tests. They’re easy to do and could save you the headache of buying the wrong size for your sleep needs.

#6820. The 17 Best Comedy Movies of 2026

Thanks to The Invite, Coyote vs. Acme, and Stop! That! Train!, big-screen comedy is making a comeback.

Are comedies making a comeback? Over the past decade, you couldn’t get a laugh into theaters even if it was forced. Hollywood decided that comedies weren’t worth the same treatment as superhero tentpoles or low-budget horror, and just about every legacy franchise sequel of a former ‘90s comedy disappointed audiences because they were basically cash grabs that played on nostalgia. Comedy needed help.

So, in 2026, some fresh films forced Hollywood to come to its senses. Coyote vs. Acme, a Looney Tunes film that Warner Bros. originally dropped to claim a tax write-off, grossed over $126 million at the box office. Olivia Wilde’s The Invite might just receive nominations for every major award category at the Oscars, and Airplane!-esque humor made a comeback in RuPaul’s hilarious disaster flick Stop! That! Train!

If you’re in need of a belly laugh, check out the best comedies of 2026 below.

Don’t get me wrong, Coyote vs. Acme is no Space Jam. But the Looney Tunes film that triumphantly won the box office—even after Warner Bros. gave up on it—is still one of the funniest movies of the year. The story follows Wile E. Coyote as he partners with a lowly injury attorney (Will Forte) to sue the ACME Corporation for its defective products. The comedy also stars John Cena as ACME’s defense attorney, who probably has the funniest line of the year when he tells Tweety Bird: “I don’t pay you to tink you taw, I pay you to taw.”

Olivia Colman stars in this romantic comedy drama about a lonely fisherwoman who grows tired of the town’s harassment due to her smell. So, she asks a magical basket weaver (Peter Dinklage) to make her a husband (Alexander Skarsgård) out of wicker. What follows is a bizarre yet hilarious story about the follies of marriage, jealousy, and tons of sex with a man made out of basket material.

Sam Rockwell leads this sci-fi comedy by former Pirates of the Caribbean director Gore Verbinski, about a time-traveler who travels to the same diner over and over again in a Groundhog Day–esque loop because he believes that if he can take the right configuration of people from the diner back to the future with him then he may be able to save the world from AI’s takeover. I know, it’s strange. But Verbinski makes the comedy work, especially when you learn about each diner patron via small vignettes emphasizing various doomsday scenarios.

Real estate agent Rudd Landy (John Cena) is called to the hospital when his brother gets into a car accident. Turns out, the man in emergency care is not his real brother (Christopher Meloni), but someone that he used to mentor when he was younger as part of a Big Brothers Big Sisters program. Without anywhere else to go, Rudd agrees to help take care of his “little brother” (Eric André) while he recovers, even if his little brother might just ruin his life.

When Gail Daughtry’s (Zoey Deutch) husband takes their celebrity sex pass joke literally, Gail sets out to get even by tracking down her celebrity sex pass—John Hamm—to have sex with him and even out the score. The comedy film also stars “Weird Al” Yankovic, Ben Wang, Sabrina Impacciatore, Joe Lo Truglio, Fred Melamed, and Mad Men’s John Slattery as himself.

Johnny Knoxville, Steve-O, and the whole Jackass crew return for one last ride in Jackass: Best and Last, another compilation of skits, stunts, and pranks from some of the most fearless people on the planet. They hit each other in the groin with wooden mallets, hook their penises up to shock collars, and reminisce about some of their most iconic sketches throughout the years.

Half RuPaul’s Drag Race, half Airplane! for Bravo fans, Stop! That! Train! is the dumbest movie I’ve seen all year (complimentary). The film follows RuPaul and her Drag Race stars as they attempt to stop a speeding bullet train heading straight toward the storm of the century, aka a Stormaganza. Even if you’re so out of your depth that you have no idea what’s going on, Stop! That! Train! argues that that’s okay. It’s comedy for comedy’s sake, and we need more of that at the theater.

Mel Brooks just turned 100 years old (!!!). But before that, he was 99 in Mel Brooks: The 99-Year-Old Man!—a two-part HBO documentary directed by Judd Apatow. The film, which chronicles the comedian’s career throughout The Producers, Blazing Saddles, Spaceballs, Young Frankenstein, and many more, also touches on his work as a film producer. Still, it’s the testimonies from his friendship with Carl Reiner and his marriage to Anne Bancroft that really pull at the heartstrings after a good laugh down memory lane.

Olivia Wilde is not afraid of Virginia Woolf. In the actress’s latest film, The Invite, the classic dinner-party setup full of playful talking and relationship arguments somehow morphs into a sex comedy. The whole film takes place in Angela (Wilde) and Joe’s (Seth Rogen) apartment as they welcome the raucous upstairs neighbors Pina (Penelope Cruz) and Hawk (Edward Norton). But unlike Who’s Afraid of Virginia Woolf? and its descent into anger and grief, The Invite asks more questions about love, commitment, and everyone’s stance on orgies.

The struggling music duo always works for me. Whether it’s Flight of the Conchords or Ishtar, there’s something funny about people with no talent still trying to make it to the top. Nirvanna the Band the Show the Movie takes the genre to maybe its lowest budget yet, as the Canadian web-series comedians somehow also make room to spoof Back to the Future and incorporate real Nathan Fielder–like guerrilla filmmaking.

One of the coolest movies to premiere out of SXSW was the Hulu original Mike & Nick & Nick & Alice, from writer/director BenDavid Grabinski. A loan shark (Vince Vaughn) uses a time machine to undo one bad night involving his close friend (James Marsden) and his wife (Eiza González). Mike & Nick & Nick & Alice is the kind of movie you want when the pizza is hot, the beers are cold, and your friends are indecisive about what you all want to watch.

After years of creative and financial slump, Pixar has found its groove again with Hoppers. Essentially Animal Farm meets Avatar, Hoppers tells the story of a genius college student whose participation in a breakthrough science experiment lets her become a beaver who can communicate with animals. Though her efforts to help animals seem promising at first, she quickly realizes what really happens when animals (and bugs!) fight back against mankind. Directed by We Bare Bears creator Daniel Chong, Hoppers is an easygoing, family-friendly romp through the wilderness.

Liam Neeson continues his funny streak in the action comedy Cold Storage. Joe Keery (Stranger Things) plays a slacker employee of a storage facility who, along with his cute coworker (Georgina Campbell), discovers a deadly, mutating fungus. Teaming up with a grizzled federal agent (Neeson), the three work to save the world. Basically a modern-day Outbreak, Cold Storage isn’t one to leave dormant on your watch list.

Dave Bautista and Jason Momoa make up the ultimate action-movie tag team in The Wrecking Crew. The guys play estranged brothers in Hawaii who work together to piece together who killed their father and how on earth the Japanese yakuza is involved.

Boots Riley, the writer and director of Sorry to Bother You, does it again with his crime caper I Love Boosters. In this anticapitalist satire, a crew of professional thieves works to take down a ruthless fashion giant (Demi Moore). Keke Palmer, Naomi Ackie, Taylour Paige, Poppy Liu, Eiza González, and more make up this delirious ensemble that takes Ocean’s Eleven and dresses it in hyper-surreal shades.

Sometimes we need a nauseatingly cute rom-com. Kevin James (yes, The King of Queens) stars in this breezy and uplifting comedy about a man who, after being left at the altar, goes on his Italian honeymoon solo. While there, he makes new friends and even deeper connections with strangers halfway around the world. A feel-good flick about new beginnings, Solo Mio can lift you high when you’re feeling down.

A stoner comedy? In 2026? In this economy? Well, the streaming generation can finally know what it’s like to have their own Harold & Kumar with Pizza Movie. Gaten Matarazzo and Sean Giambrone star as college roommates and social pariahs who, while high on a new hallucinogenic drug, embark on a psychedelic odyssey to make it downstairs and get their pizza delivery. Despite a popular meme mocking the movie’s marketing, Pizza Movie performed well with critics at its SXSW premiere.

#6816. I Can Feel the Tremors of History Rumbling Beneath Scott Colom’s Victory

The senator-elect from Mississippi invoked Medger Evers in his winning speech, which reminded me of how far we’ve come—and how far we still have to go.

(Optional Musical Accompaniment to This Post)

Okay! Now we can celebrate. From the Mississippi Free Press:

Oh, to hell with the numbers. Let’s just celebrate a win by a guy cool enough to receive the good news of his election while wearing a Richard Pryor hoodie. From his victory speech:

One day in 1994, in the employ of another magazine, I went down to Jackson on assignment for a story about the death penalty that ultimately never ran. It was the day after the Republicans, led by Representative Newt Gingrich, won control of the entire Congress for the first time since 1952 and control of the House of Representatives for the first time since 1954. It can be argued, and I have, that these results scared the courage out of the Democratic party as thoroughly as George McGovern’s landslide loss to Richard Nixon in 1972 did. And when the modern Democratic party gets scared, it inevitably runs away from its most sacred commitments and the proudest moments in its history. This result caused Democratic president Bill Clinton to sign grotesque legislation like the Defense of Marriage Act and a crime bill that, relevant to my mission to Mississippi, added 28 new death-penalty crimes to the federal code. The bar in the Walthall Hotel, where I was staying, was filled every night by gloating Republicans, which drove me to room service for the balance of my stay.

On my last day in Jackson, I took a drive across town to what was then called 2332 Guynes Street in the Elraine subdivision, which had been developed in the 1950s by men named Winston Thompson and Leroy Burnett in order to service the rising Black middle class of the city. One of the houses in the development was purchased by Medgar and Myrlie Evers. On the night of June 12, 1963, Myrlie and their children were watching the speech in which President John F. Kennedy finally got off the dime on civil rights when they heard Medgar’s car coming up the driveway. The kids ran out to greet him. From behind a bush across the street, a back-shooting coward named Byron De La Beckwith sighted down his .30–06 Enfield and murdered Medgar Evers in front of his family.

I parked my car and walked slowly from De La Beckwith’s sniper’s nest to the driveway at 2332 Guynes Street, tracing the route of the bullet that killed Medgar Evers on that night in 1963. I was strangely aware of every step I took. I was deeply aware of the rise and fall of my feet, one after the other. It was as though there were a tiny tremor of history beneath me the closer I came to the spot under the carport where he fell, bleeding, before his family. It felt very much like a pilgrimage to some ancient shrine. There were sad and solemn bells tolling in my mind.

Listening to Scott Colom accept his victory on Wednesday night, as he name-checked Medgar Evers, along with Fannie Lou Hamer and Hiram Revels, I heard those bells again in my mind. Loud and raucous this time, they drowned out the crack of the whip and the crack of a .30–06 Enfield rifle fired with murderous intent. This time, they rang with joy, and the tremors of history I felt beneath my feet that day in 1994 felt like an earthquake.

#6814. This Historic Napa Valley Estate Features 18th-Century Charm, Fig Trees, and a Luxe Spa

Preserving the past while readying the property for a new chapter was the goal.

At Vine & Fig, each moment takes on a bit of everyday grandeur, spurred on by the sun-drenched warmth and crisp air of Napa County. The aptly named estate, set among vineyards, olive groves, and fig trees in St. Helena, California, has always had the makings of a particularly good gathering place; it just needed a little help catching up with the times.

Enter designer Jaime Rummerfield, who spent four years transforming the 19th-century property into a 10,500-square-foot retreat that feels both comfortable and reverential to its past. The undertaking was hardly a straightforward process. Between fires, floods, and the delicate logistics of preserving the estate’s historic architecture and geothermal streams, Rummerfield had to tackle the renovation with equal parts imagination and restraint. “You cannot approach a place like this as a blank canvas,” she explains. “Understanding what is already there becomes part of the design narrative.”

Fortunately, she had a willing creative partner in homeowner Olivia Marciano, whose background as artistic director of the Marciano Art Foundation brought an especially discerning perspective to the process. The two spent hours considering materials, palettes, and custom details, often allowing a single piece to inform the direction of an entire room. The resulting interiors are richly layered yet approachably livable: antique-style cabinetry with exposed hinges, hand-crafted furnishings, expressive stone, and artwork that lends the rooms a sense of collected history. “Everything shouldn’t feel as though it arrived on the same truck,” Rummerfield says.

Given that this is Napa Valley, it goes without saying that the perks of the property extend well beyond its walls. A chef’s kitchen opens onto the surrounding gardens, while the historic pool house was reimagined as an immersive hammam, complete with Moroccan mosaics, Taj Mahal stone, and copper hot and cold plunge tubs. But for Rummerfield, the property’s greatest luxury isn’t any single amenity, but the new chapter unfolding around it. “There are vineyards, olive groves, gardens, food being prepared, and people coming together,” she says. "The interiors are the stage and participate in all of that.” And at Vine & Fig, there's always room for one more act.

FAST FACTSDesigner: Jaime RummerfieldBuilder: JMA Uncommon BuildersArchitect: 4C Design GroupLandscape Architect: Surfacedesign, Inc Location: St. Helena, CaliforniaThe Space: A six-bedroom, ten-bathroom home across 10,500 square feet

Designed to accommodate everything from quiet family dinners to spirited parties, the kitchen embraces the property’s farm-to-table ethos. Antique-style cabinetry with exposed butt hinges lends the space a sense of history, while a white oak island, stone backsplash, and French trestle table keep things grounded. Beyond the windows, views of the mountains and gardens reinforce the connection between the home and its surroundings.

“We wanted the practical decisions and the decorative decisions to support one another,” adds Rummerfield. “The cabinetry details, hidden technology, ample storage, textiles, and connection to the outdoors keep it feeling like a room in a home. It is a chef’s delight, but it is also a very welcoming heart of the house.”

“We wanted this multi-use kitchen to have a personality appropriate to its setting while still feeling connected to the wider property, hammam, and pool,” says Rummerfield of this bonus cook space. “The proportions and material relationships become especially important. It ended up being one of our favorite spaces—the rich chocolate zellige tile back splash really made it unique and divine.”

Few amenities feel more appropriate to a Napa Valley estate than a dedicated wine cave. Here, Rummerfield let the architecture and natural materials establish the atmosphere, incorporating honed black soapstone countertops to create a space that feels equally suited to storing prized bottles and sharing a favorite vintage with friends.

Rummerfield bypassed a rigid furniture arrangement in favor of several intimate seating areas, creating a room that feels equally suited to lively conversation and afternoon lounging. “I wanted the living room to have a sense of occasion while remaining completely comfortable,” she says.

To that end, a generously proportioned, low-slung sectional offers a particularly inviting place to land, while custom cabinetry conceals modern technology, including a drop-down projector screen, without interrupting the room’s collected aesthetic.

“Passageways are wonderful places to create a moment of discovery,” Rummerfield says of the dramatic entryway and curved staircase, which features the client’s art colleciton. “I want these spaces to feel as personal, stylish and welcoming as the rooms they connect to.”

An arched ceiling and soft lighting sets the tone for the journey into the property’s reimagined pool house, where Rummerfield wanted to create “a completely different experience” within the property. “The hammam is about slowing down and enjoying the beauty of the materials,” she adds. “The barrel ceiling, Moroccan mosaic, and Taj Mahal stone give the space a wonderful sense of intimacy and ritual.”

“Powder rooms offer a lovely opportunity for personalization; the relationship between the vanity, sink, mirror, and light can give the room a distinct personality,” says Rummerfield. In here, she sources a Turkish sink from Olive Ateliers, incorporated a 1940’s glass sconce, inlaid an antique mirror, and brought the terra cotta paver floors from outside in. “Each element is deliberately chosen and work beautifully together,” she adds.

Affectionately dubbed the “Strawberry Room” after the pattern on the BDDW chair, this bedroom offers comfort and relaxation in space. Rummerfield leaned into the room’s cozy proportions with a tactile Phillip Jeffries wallcovering, layered fabrics, and sculptural lighting by Ken Fulk for Urban Electric.

“I like a bathroom to feel considered as a room in its own right,” says Rummerfield. “The vanity, mirror, lighting, and tub should have a pleasing relationship, with the materials doing much of the decorative work. The atmosphere should feel quiet and indulgent.”

“I enjoy giving utility spaces some personality,” Rummerfield says of the laundry room, which she coated in Benjamin Moore’s Spring Valley, a fresh green that nods to the surrounding landscape. “Many people spend time in them, so the light, finishes, and everyday experience deserve attention.”

Jaime Rummerfield is a Los Angeles–based interior designer known for her richly layered spaces that balance timeless elegance with a distinct sense of personality. Her work celebrates craftsmanship, architectural character, and the art of collecting, combining vintage finds, custom furnishings, and expressive materials to create interiors that feel both sophisticated and lived-in.

#6813. Ana de Armas’s Complete Dating History

From Ben Affleck to Tom Cruise and beyond.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Ana de Armas has experienced a stratospheric rise in Hollywood over the past decade. 2015’s Knock Knock—a thriller starring Keanu Reeves—provided the Cuban, American, and Spanish actress with her stateside breakout role. Since then, de Armas has appeared in numerous box office and critical hits, such as 2017’s Blade Runner 2049, 2019’s Knives Out, 2021’s No Time to Die, 2022’s Blonde, and 2025’s John Wick spin-off Ballerina.

Offscreen, the actress has had several public romances, too. Most recently, she has been linked to Venezuelan singer Beto Montenegro, with the pair photographed kissing during a summer getaway in Spain. In November 2026, they made their first red carpet appearance together at the Latin Recording Academy’s Person of the Year Gala in Las Vegas.

Here’s what you need to know about Ana de Armas’s dating history.

Prior to making it big as a Hollywood actress, de Armas relocated from Havana to Spain, where she met her first husband, Marc Clotet. The Ghosted star began dating the Spanish model and actor in 2010, with the pair marrying the following year in July 2011, Hola! magazine reported.

Unfortunately, the couple’s marriage didn’t last. During an appearance at the 27th Goya Awards in Spain on Feb. 17, 2013, Clotet revealed, “Yes, we’ve broken up. It was by mutual agreement. I’m calm.” According to Diez Minutos, Clotet also shared that he and de Armas were “still friends” despite the split.

According to La Razón, de Armas started dating Spanish screenwriter and director David Victori in August 2013. The publication also noted that the couple lived together in Los Angeles, but had returned to Spain in March 2014 for the Málaga Film Festival. Although it’s unclear when Victori and de Armas ended their relationship, it’s been reported that they split sometime in 2014.

Following her breakups from Clotet and Victori, de Armas decided to move to Los Angeles full time to further her career, telling The Hollywood Reporter, “I don’t like feeling stuck, like I’m not going anywhere. I wanted more.”

After permanently relocating to America, de Armas began “a serious relationship with agent Franklin Latt,” Vanity Fair reported. During her interview with the publication, the War Dogs actress was quick to dispel the idea that dating a successful Hollywood agent—Latt now manages Pedro Pascal at Creative Artists Agency—aided her career in any way. “For anyone who ever questions...how did I get to do this or that, fuck them,” she said. “They will not get to spend their New Year’s with me. They are not the people whose opinion I should care about. They are not the people I share my happiness with.”

In 2017, de Armas started dating artist Alejandro Piñeiro Bello, who was also born in Havana. During their relationship, the pair regularly posted photos of one another on social media, which have since been deleted from their respective accounts.

In February 2018, de Armas marked the launch of Piñeiro Bello’s pop-up exhibition in Los Angeles by writing on Instagram, “So proud of you,” and he returned the favor, thanking the actress for all of her “hard work and love,” according to Antena 3.

Porter later confirmed that de Armas had split from Piñeiro Bello in 2019 after two years of dating.

After meeting on the set of thriller Deep Water in late 2019, de Armas started a very public relationship with Ben Affleck. In March 2020, a source told People that the pair had “great chemistry right from the start,” but “there were no signs of romance” until filming had wrapped. In a March 2020 interview with Vogue Spain (translated by People), de Armas discussed working with the Oscar winner, saying, “His talent is infinite.” The same month, de Armas made her relationship with Affleck official when she shared photos of him on Instagram.

Affleck and de Armas were regularly photographed walking their dogs together in Los Angeles, where the couple quarantined together during the early days of the COVID-19 pandemic in 2020.

In June 2020, de Armas and Affleck attended a Black Lives Matter march together, per People. Following a double date with Affleck’s best friend, Matt Damon, and his wife, People reported that de Armas officially moved into her boyfriend’s property in August 2020. “She’s aware that his family comes first,” a source told the outlet. “And appreciates that. They work very hard at that balance.” For Affleck’s 48th birthday, the Overdrive actress gave her beau a custom-built BMW motorcycle, according to People.

In November 2020, the pair traveled to New Orleans for reshoots of their movie Deep Water. As de Armas appeared to be wearing a diamond ring in photos from the set, rumors started to swirl regarding a possible engagement. However, according to People, the jewelry item was simply a prop for the film. The very same month, de Armas and Affleck spent their first Thanksgiving together, along with the actor’s three children—Violet, Fin, and Samuel—whom he shares with ex-wife Jennifer Garner, E! News reported.

Unfortunately, by January 2021, de Armas and Affleck had called it quits. “Ben is no longer dating Ana,” a source told People. “She broke it off. Their relationship was complicated. Ana doesn’t want to be Los Angeles-based and Ben obviously has to since his kids live in Los Angeles.” People reported that the breakup occurred “over the phone.” Soon after the split, Affleck reconnected with his ex Jennifer Lopez.

Following her split from Affleck, de Armas was romantically linked to Tinder executive Paul Boukadakis in June 2021. In December 2021, an insider told E! News that the couple’s romance was “very serious.”

A July 2022 profile of de Armas in ELLE revealed that she met her new boyfriend “during the pandemic through a mutual friend.” With the majority of bars and restaurants closed during lockdown, they “spent their first dates at each other’s homes, drinking wine and talking.”

As for how she spent her 34th birthday in April 2022, de Armas told ELLE that she celebrated with “my man, my dogs, Chris [Evans] and the crew [of Ghosted].” She continued, “I wasn’t home having a romantic dinner; I was on set with my people doing what I love and at the beach and having a shot of Fireball...and it is the happiest I’ve been.”

The pair was pictured arriving at the Venice Film Festival in September 2022, and they posed for photos on the red carpet at the Vanity Fair Oscar Party on March 12, 2023. While it’s unclear when de Armas and Boukadakis ended their romance, People reported the split in November 2024, when the actress was spotted kissing another man.

The Exposed star surprised fans when she started dating Manuel Anido Cuesta, the stepson of Cuba’s president, Miguel Díaz-Canel Bermúdez. Having graduated with a law degree from the University of Havana in 2019, Anido Cuesta reportedly “became part of the Cuban regime’s inner circle,” La Nación reported. The outlet further explained, “Although he does not hold official positions in government institutions, he has been seen at numerous events and on official trips accompanying his stepfather and mother.”

Rumors began circulating about a potential romance between de Armas and Anido Cuesta in May 2024, when a Cuban influencer claimed the pair had spent 10 days together on the island of Cayo Santa María, off the north coast of Cuba, per Periódico Cubano. While the majority of de Armas’s romance with Anido Cuesta remained private, Hola! published photographs of the pair kissing one another during a night out in Madrid in November 2024. Breakup reports ultimately surfaced in May 2025.

Mission: Impossible icon Tom Cruise and de Armas first sparked dating rumors in winter 2025. The two actors had been spending time together due to work.

While promoting her John Wick spin-off Ballerina in June 2025, de Armas commented on her connection with Cruise, telling Variety, “We’re working together, so he got to see Ballerina and he actually really liked it.” She continued, “It is very special that someone like him is supporting [Ballerina].”

In mid-July 2025, a source downplayed the romance rumors, telling People that de Armas and Cruise simply had “a special work relationship.” According to the insider, the pair had been spending extended periods of time together while preparing to co-star in the thriller Deeper, from the director of 2005’s Mr. & Mrs. Smith, Doug Liman. “Tom is an incredible mentor to Ana,” the source alleged. “She has nothing but amazing things to say about him.”

The end of July brought two eyebrow-raising outings for de Armas and Cruise. On July 26, the pair made an unexpected cameo at an Oasis concert in London, appearing in the background of an Instagram video shared by music producer Goldie. The following day, de Armas and Cruise materialized in Vermont, where they were photographed holding hands, eating ice cream, and shopping together.

In October 2025, People reported that de Armas and Cruise were only ever friends. A source said Cruise was a “dear friend and mentor” to her and added, “She’s single, though, and has been for a while. She stays in touch with Tom and they still have an upcoming movie project that she’s looking forward to.”

De Armas sparked romance rumors with Venezuelan singer Beto Montenegro in summer 2026. The pair was seen together at a concert in Madrid, where Hola! captured a video of them dancing on July 29.

The speculation intensified in August when de Armas and Montenegro traveled to Formentera, Spain. On August 2, they were photographed holding hands and kissing while making their way to the beach.

On November 11, de Armas and Montenegro made their red carpet debut together at the Latin Recording Academy’s 2026 Person of the Year Gala in Las Vegas.

The actress wore a black Wiederhoeft look featuring a corseted top and sheer lace skirt, while Montenegro opted for a black suit with a light blue shirt and dark tie.

#6811. No More Franchises

No more prequels. No more reboots. No more Harry Styles in Eternals. No more of these 71 other things.

No more movie franchises. No more shared universes. No more post-credits scenes. No more prequels. No more reboots. No more eight-hour streaming TV spin-offs. No more slates and phases. No more multiverses. No more crossovers.

No more movies that are only made to greenlight sequels. No more sequels made for getting you to sign up for a streamer that raises its monthly subscription price every year. No more Easter eggs. No more winks. No more fourth-wall breaks. No more action scenes that halt the story and are clearly directed by the second unit. No more teasers that lead to nothing. No more Harry Styles in Eternals. No more watch guides and chronological order explainers and breakdowns. No more asking, “Is it canon?”

No more four-quadrant tentpoles. No more movies based on kids’ stuff that my niece can’t actually watch. No more Superman movies that are so violent and destructive that they trigger my 9/11 survivor mother’s PTSD. No more R-rated films that sell Funkos. No more movies that have to appeal to foreign markets and all the meemaws and pawpaws in Idaho. No more quippy dialogue where characters remark, “Well, THAT just happened!” No more irony. No more grotesque violence set to upbeat pop songs. No more characters looking into their own phones. No more thudding exposition, and especially not in the first ten minutes. No more “no more romances.”

No more hiring interesting up-and-coming filmmakers with buzz coming out of Sundance and Telluride because they’re cheap and have credibility to exploit. No more boxing in those same artistes because they’ll say “yes” to all marching orders. No more rejecting established auteurs with an interesting pitch and a desire to play in the sandbox because it doesn’t fit into next summer’s tentpole. No more prioritizing release dates over craft and the well-being of everyone working on it.

No more geriatric thespians putting on spandex because today’s adult moviegoer refuses to grow up themselves. No more casting red-hot twenty-somethings and locking them into seven-movie contracts. No more firing those actors because they got into legal trouble so the audience gets gaslit into forgetting there was ever something called The Kang Dynasty. No more international talent getting nothing cool to do; no more Hiroyuki Sanada getting killed by Jeremy Renner in Avengers: Endgame. No more casting directors deciding based on their exact amount of Instagram followers. No more content creators getting cameos.

No more Comic-Con. Or rather, no more making Comic-Con into Coachella. No more sleeping on the sidewalks for a seat in Hall H. No more buzzy presentations that turn art and myth into product reveals. No more “we’re doing it for the fans” and “one more thing.” No more Oscar winners promoting movies that don’t even have a script. No more actors lying through their teeth about how they read the original comics growing up. No more trailers that shake the room for those who slept outside to get in, and then get uploaded to YouTube while they collect their sleeping bags.

No more press screenings with ridiculous embargoes. No more taking away my phone and stuffing it into a zip pouch because of a spoiler that heaven forbid leaks on TikTok when millions were spent making it in front of IMAX cameras and a unionized crew; no more thinking any of this is tantamount to government secrets. No more influencers on red carpets. No more junket “interviews” that are just quizzes and games. No more gushing. No more unprofessionalism.

No more studios that exist only to adapt in-house IP. No more C-suites rambling about the lucrative potential in old cartoons and ‘90s comic books like old-timey oil barons surveying land. No more touting the box office of one movie as evidence why they oughta make a dozen more. No more pretenses about the power of stories in earnings calls with Merrill Lynch. No more forgetting, and not giving healthy royalties to, the comic book creators whose imaginations filled the calendar slate and fueled the stock prices in the first place.

No more toxic fandoms. No more powerful studios kowtowing to the loudest, most obnoxious YouTubers who make a living screaming into microphones in their basements. No more chuds who bemoan wokeness in whatever the latest remake/sequel they’re supposed to be mad about, and use AI-generated thumbnails of themselves making the dumbest fucking faces with cherry-picked unflattering screenshots of actresses who wouldn’t give them the time of day anyway. No more abusers, sex pests, misogynists, and racists anywhere, let alone in charge.

No more ten-year plans. No more stoking tuckered-out fans to drum up excitement about the next Batman or James Bond. No more actors keeping up action figure physiques while they wait years to hear about the sequel. No more taking characters and stories people have spent their lives escaping into for comfort and deforming them into numbers on a spreadsheet. No more vague bullshit like “It didn’t capture the zeitgeist” to explain why you’re arm-swiping everything off the table again. No more using the word “zeitgeist” if you cannot define it without ChatGPT.

No more cowardice. No more promises no one can keep. No more of this circus. No more.

#6810. This Designer-Approved Curtain Trick Will Instantly Elevate Your Bedroom

Your window treatments should serve up both form and function.

Window treatments are often an afterthought, but your choice of drapery can make or break your entire design. Our 2026 Whole Home in Charleston is a master class in innovative design, and our designers took every opportunity to showcase their skills and style, down to the very last detail—including some gorgeous examples of transformative drapery.

Though often seen as a luxury, window treatments are a home necessity. Blinds, drapes, and shades don’t just line our windowpanes; they block drafts, light, and real-world clamor from entering your homes, practically on demand. While some choose to keep it simple, designers know your sill is uncharted territory for design potential. While it might be tempting to reach for your typical verticals, it’s worth making a statement. That said, good drapes can be expensive, so if you’re hoping to embrace your window treatment’s potential, you’ll have to budget for it.

In our patternmaxxed guest bedroom, designer Laura Lubin, founder of the Nashville-based design firm Ellerslie Interiors, used Stroheim drapes to add subtle color to her muted palette and elevate the chic TWOPAGES Roman shades just beyond. Then she took her design a step further: Instead of relying on pleating for texture, Lubin added a leafy trim and Fabricut bullion fringe to the edge of her panels, framing the bold blue baseboard and adding another note of texture to the room’s design.

Texture is meant to act as a bridge in design schemes, connecting elements and feeding both our visual and tactile appetites. In addition to her customized drapes, Lubin used grid-patterned wallpaper, natural wood grain, and plaid textiles to elevate the visual palette.

If you’re looking for a quick bedroom upgrade with big impact, it might be time to take a closer look at your drapes. After all, your window treatments are meant to block out the world, not your style.

#6806. A Behind-the-Scenes Look at Esquire’s Party for the 20th Anniversary of the Big Black Book

At the Ludlow House on the Lower East Side, a bunch of New York’s coolest people gathered to raise a glass to the essential guide to style and luxury.

It’s been two decades since the first edition of the Big Black Book debuted in 2006. Back then, a style guide devoted to the ins and outs of the luxury market wasn’t exactly common, but we believed it was necessary. The idea wasn’t to spam readers with a bunch of stuff they couldn’t afford but to give them a glimpse into the world of well-made, time-tested pieces that can create the backbone of a considered wardrobe.

Turns out, we were right. Men want to dress well, and they want to invest in the items—bench-made boots, bespoke tailoring, designer apparel—that look great on day one but even better a few years down the line. The fact that the Big Black Book is still going strong in 2026 is a testament to that, so we decided to celebrate. At the Ludlow House on the Lower East Side, we gathered a group of people who represent the BBB ethos. Sharp dressers, sure, but also sharp minds—the folks who don’t just define luxury in the moment but also lay the foundation for its future.

It would’ve been even more fun to invite every Big Black Book fan along for the ride, but we quickly realized that logistics—and fire marshals—might not allow it. So, in lieu of bringing everyone inside, we’re sharing a gallery of photos from the party. Keep scrolling for an inside look at the festivities.

#6805. 8 Popular Home Items From 20 Years Ago That Are Making a Surprising Comeback

They may be a blast from the past, but they’re about to be very now.

Trends come and go, but they almost always come back again. If there’s one thing we know about interior design, it’s that what feels old and outdated manages to become fresh and cool again in a few years’ time. Skeptics can easily be proved wrong with the resurgence of sunken living rooms, glass block walls, and even cushy sofas plucked right out of a Nora Ephron movie. And, as younger generations continue to be intrigued with the 1990s and early 2000s, they are turning to the era’s interiors for inspiration.

Well, sort of, as Maggie Vollrath of Provision Studios points out. “When we look back at certain interiors from the 2000s, there was such a commitment to achieving a particular look that every element in the room was chosen to reinforce it,” the Georgia designer says. “A home should feel like a collection of things you genuinely love, not as though you’ve walked onto a movie set designed to represent a particular style.”

When you cherry-pick trends and design details from the past, you’re able to create a home that honors the past without appearing stuck in the past. “The goal isn’t to make a home look like it belongs to another decade,” Vollrath says. “You want to bring back the beauty and character of those decorative details while creating something that feels authentic to both the house and its owners.”

Below, designers share trends from the past that the next generation of design lovers adore.

For older generations, a swivel chair might conjure memories of sitting in your family’s den, but designers promise these movin’, groovin’ seats have been revived for the 21st century. “Swivel barrel chairs defined early-2000s transitional interiors, usually in a bright flat polyester-blend upholstery,” explains Hallie Malitsky, principal designer of Hallie Rose Design in Brooklyn. “The version coming back today is a different experience entirely.”

Today’s iterations, she insists, are subtler. Cheap polyester has yielded to rich, tactile fabrics like wool, linen, and velvet. For a timely twist, she also encourages trading the fire engine red and butter yellow popular in the 2000s for burgundy and ochre. “Surround it with natural materials like wood, linen, and stone, and [your chair] will stop being the loudest thing in the room,” she recommends.

When Malitsky thinks of early aughts design, she immediately envisions the gigantic walnut media center that plagued many of her friends’ family rooms. “Dark wood cabinetry is a notorious culprit of bad 2000s design, but the wood wasn’t the issue,” she says. “It was the fact that there was so much of it.”

Now that dark wood tones are making a comeback, Malitsky says to use them sparingly. Two to three walnut or ebony pieces per room will make enough of a statement. And, no: Unlike the living rooms of yore, your furniture doesn’t have to match.

“What I love about their return is that we’re incorporating these pieces in a much more collected way,” shares Vollrath. “Rather than purchasing an entire matching furniture collection, we’re mixing periods and bringing in individual pieces that have character and history.”

The early aughts had lots of elaborate cabinetry, replete with corbels, substantial cornices, and beautifully detailed range hoods. As everyone—yes, even younger generations—continue to favor more traditional interiors, we’re seeing a renewed interest in ornamented cabinetry.

“There’s something really special about a kitchen that feels as though it could have been there for generations, or even reminds you of your grandmother’s kitchen,” Vollrath says. “Those decorative flourishes bring a sense of history, craftsmanship, and permanence that can be difficult to achieve with a more streamlined approach.”

But why stop there when you can add character to other rooms of the house?

“We’re seeing a broader interest in architectural detailing throughout the home, from wainscoting and wall paneling to ceiling beams and substantial moldings,” she adds. “Homeowners are becoming more interested in understanding these elements, considering them during renovations, and making them a priority in new construction.”

If the rise of analog interiors proves anything, it’s that even the best chatbot can’t beat excellent design. Instead of having a home covered in screens and smart systems, many are favoring more primitive setups that harken back to dial-up and CD-ROMs. For example, Fargo-based designer Lindsey Christie has been noticing (and loving) the return of landlines. “There's something wonderfully nostalgic about a beautiful telephone sitting on a bedside table or tucked into a thoughtfully designed corner of a home,” she says.

Of course, there’s plenty of fun to be had in a “dumb home.” “We have had several adult clients wanting to incorporate their hobby of Legos into their design aesthetic,” reveals Allison Smith of Gem + Clay Interiors in St. Augustine, Florida. “I definitely think this speaks to a desire to return to the analog, and that [old-school] element.”

“We always joke about the Tuscan kitchens from 2005, but they had some good things going on,” says Drew Michael Scott of Lone Fox. If you strip away the “bottles of preserved peppers and gaudy oil-rubbed bronze faucets,” the style was actually rocking a great color palette. Shades like brown, ochre, terracotta, and deep red bring a little bit of the great outdoors inside, plus they can pair nicely with today’s cozy interiors. But let’s keep the overly Italian influences in the past, shall we?

Today’s emerging design lovers are extra—just ask Scott, who has been noticing a considerable uptick in passementerie. “These intricate details were everywhere in the mid-2000s, before the world decided everything should be sleek and gray,” he says. “They’re becoming popular again, and I think they really evoke the attention to detail we saw at the turn of the century.”

Though yesteryear’s tassels, beading, and decorative trim are undeniably fun, using them requires a little bit of strategy. Since there’s a fine line between charming and kitschy, balance these louder, upholstered moments with pared-back wooden pieces.

Goodbye, panel-ready refrigerators and dishwashers: Katie Kiser says stainless steel appliances are so back. According to the Tennessee-based designer, the resurgence is a break from the picture-perfect kitchens older generations have pined over. “Gen-Z values practicality over perfection,” she explains. “I think it will show up in their interiors.” And why stop at appliances when you can bring that lived-in feel to the rest of the space? “They don’t hate clutter the way millennials do,” she adds. “We’ll see details like cookbooks in the kitchens and hall trees with tennis rackets on them.”

Though Scott hasn’t seen the Venetian Gold granite that ruled Y2K just yet, he’s noticing many homeowners are willing to get creative with their countertops.

“We've moved beyond using only Carrara marble in kitchens,” he explains.

Ditto for quartz, which some designers believe is on the outs. Instead, Scott says travertine is huge again and granite is poised to make the comeback. If you want to keep these materials from appearing dated, he encourages you to reach for stones with bolding veining and inclusions.

Bolder hues are getting the starring role as winter sets in

While soap manicures, subtle petal nails, and longevity techniques once dominated the summer months, a darker color palette is quickly taking over now that the clocks have gone back and evenings are early. Yet if you're hesitant to venture into "too bold" territory or fear a pop of color on your fingertips, a dark manicure doesn't have to feel overly attention-grabbing.

In fact, as professional nail technician Samantha Rudge shares, dark manicures with a more minimalist finish are proving to be winter's most popular manicure trend. "For this season, I think we'll be seeing darker classic manicures with minimalist touches," she shares, adding that she has already seen an uptick in shade requests for Bio Sculpture's rich, chocolate-toned Decadent Cocoa and the ever-popular Garnet Noir, a moody red-black hue. "Nail designs like chrome, cat eye, micro French tips, and tortoiseshell will also dominate this season," she adds.

"Short nails are also very popular right now, no matter the nail art or shades," she adds. Regardless of shape or color, Rudge stresses the importance of prioritizing nail health at this time of year. To protect nail longevity and prevent chipping, Rudge recommends assessing your natural nail condition, choosing the right base (BIAB or gel), and using a nourishing cuticle oil to repair and strengthen brittle nails.

Ahead, discover 10 new dark manicures for those who love a more minimalist finish, from “Garnet Noir” nails to subtle lines.

Taking over the SS27 runways this season, purple is finally having a moment in the spotlight. When it comes to nail colors, look for richer hues with a jewel-tone finish to keep the manicure elevated and modern. Rudge recommends Bio Sculpture's Erica shade.

All shades of wine are a staple as the festive season approaches, but why not add a chrome effect for extra sparkle?

Coffee tones are becoming one of the most popular color stories in nail salons this winter. These teeny-tiny dots make for a unique switch-up and can be created using a micro dotting tool.

A subtle marble effect elevates an otherwise classic burgundy manicure.

While almond and square are among the most popular of nail shapes, don't underestimate a soft oval. Paired with a classic cabernet tone, this dark manicure strikes the balance of looking impactful yet soft.

If you're taking a break from acrylics or longer lengths, a super-short licorice manicure is the way to do it. Add a glossy top coat and keep the nails short and square.

In the mood to experiment? Simply switch up each nail with chocolate swirls, block colors, or a French tip to recreate this look.

This ultra-glossy midnight blue manicure is all about a high-shine, mirrorlike finish that could be created with a chrome powder or top coat.

#6800. 36 Thoughtful Kitchen Gifts for Anyone Who Loves to Cook, Host, or Sip in Style

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Anyone can buy a candle. These gifts are way more personal.

The best kind of gift shows the person you care about that you know them. That you’ve considered their hobbies. After all, anyone can go out and buy a scented candle, but you are thinking about how they like to spend their free time and what makes them happy. So if you know a dinner party host, mixologist, home chef, or someone who loves a stylish kitchen, then this is the gift guide for you.

Below, dozens of smart and thoughtful kitchen gifts await you. The busy home cook will love an Instant Pot or multipurpose air fryer. The coffee or tea obsessive will love a matcha station or coffee grinder. Think outside the kitchen and into the dining room, too: an elegant yet budget-friendly canister set or berry basket could be just what they need to complete their tablescape.

The best thing about so many of our picks is that there’s a massive price range, so if you see something affordable your friend or family member may like, you don’t need to wait for a special occasion to get it for them. On the other hand, if the same person is celebrating a birthday or a holiday, feel free to splurge. We have everything any at-home cook would love and use.

No kitchen is complete without a good bottle opener, so why not opt for one that’s extra chic? Trade your gas station souvenir opener for this colorful beaded piece, inspired by the classic abacus tool. You can even rearrange the beads to suit your own aesthetic.

Any serious coffee drinker or amateur barista will know the importance of a good coffee bean grinder. Any home chef serious about their spices needs a mortar and pestle that can keep up. It’s made from pure bronze, with an ergonomic handle that makes grinding peppercorns, cardamom, ginger, and garlic easy. It’s handmade by artisans in Moradabad, known as “The Brass City of India.”

The shape of the glass was designed in collaboration with WhistlePig distillers to enhance the whiskey-drinking experience. The pontil mark on the bottom of each glass celebrates “perfect imperfection”—a mark leftover by Simon Pearce glassblowers.

A new paper towel holder is an upgrade you won’t think to do on your own, which makes it the perfect kitchen gift. It’s likely one of the most-visited spots in your kitchen. The painted beech wood and rubber feet keep it both stylish and secure.

Things get messy in the kitchen, and no one will say no to a gift of fresh dish towels. These are woven from organic cotton and stitched by a woman-owned textile company in Mumbai. They’re embroidered with the icons of Indian cooking: a fresh coconut, a tomato, and curry leaves.

Ash wood and granite stone are a match made in heaven to get the most of your ground pepper. McKenzie uses this with gourmet peppercorns, and with the easily refilled vessel and cut stone, it’s a tactile experience that elevates the simplest meal. The stone smooths over time as oils are released by the peppercorns, effectively seasoning it over time.

This is perfect for the pitmaster in your life. Sure, they've probably got a meat thermometer from the grocery store, but this upgrade is so worth it, especially for those long, slow-cooked meats. This high-tech, long-range Bluetooth thermometer will save them the headache of constantly checking their grill, so they can check its status from the comfort of the couch.

This is the potluck host’s secret weapon. No need to mess with the hassle of foil pans, chafing dishes, and Sterno burners. Just one glass-top electric warming tray can keep your appetizers hot for the whole party. It even comes with cool-touch handles for easy portability.

This is the hand wash McKenzie leaves out on her kitchen sink when guests come over. “The Moroccan mint scent is refreshing, and the lather is moisturizing, not stripping,” McKenzie says. “When the dinner party gets chaotic, this is the easiest way for guests to refresh themselves in the kitchen. The glass bottle is an extra sleek touch.”

There's a reason Drew Barrymore's slow cooker sold out within days of launching. Now, more than a year later, the hype has gone down a bit, making snatching one up a lot easier. It comes in a few delicious colors, but we're partial to the white one, which Barrymore aptly named White Icing.
