#6797. The Audacious Southern Chef Teaching Angelenos to Shut Up and Listen

Vinyl bars like West Hollywood’s Darling, from star chef Sean Brock, will help you to appreciate music, and food, and life, all over again.

In our increasingly soulless world of algorithmic playlists and AI-generated art, one of life’s great analog pleasures is listening to records with friends. A dusty old LP pre-breaks the ice for new connections, unlocks stories you haven’t heard from old ones, gives you the chance to get collectively lost in the music.

Vinyl night is such a vital human need that it’s a surprise there aren’t more third spaces built around satisfying it. Characteristically, Japan is miles ahead of America on this front. In the country’s countless kissa bars, bartenders mix drinks and spin records, and—I swear this is true—patrons keep their voices down and listen. My partner and I saw and heard it for ourselves on a trip to Japan last year: Every major city has a few joints where the speakers are big, the tables have signs that say NO LOUD LAUGHING, and the customers obey. At a packed and reverently silent bar in Kyoto called PM Sounds, a few whiskies deep and well into the a.m., side one of Jeff Buckley’s Grace hit just right. “Did we really fly halfway around the world to sit and listen to an album we have at home?” I asked Ben on the walk back to the hotel. “Yes,” he replied, and we both agreed it was worth the trip.

Americans are famously not a quiet people, nor are we particularly good with being told what to do. Could kissa culture happen here? A few places in Los Angeles are rising to the challenge: The Gold Line in Highland Park has a hip crowd and shelves swelling with the massive record collection of owner Peanut Butter Wolf. Hollywood’s Living Room is massive and meticulously acoustically treated, and it’s currently one of the tightest doors in town. But in West Hollywood, across the street from metastasizing gay club the Abbey and steps away from the log cabin where the hip AA meetings happen, southern super chef Sean Brock just might have hit the sweet spot: His first California restaurant, Darling, has an eclectic menu, outstanding cocktails, and speakers that will make you feel like you’re right there in the recording studio. “Music brings people together, just like food,” Brock says. “I’ve been obsessed with listening bars for a while, and I’ve been trying to get people into them since 2023. The thrill of discovery is amazing, and I think the more AI the world gets, the more we’re going to reach for this stuff to balance it out.”

Brock looks every inch both the 21st-century Generation X chef and DJ: horn-rimmed glasses, sleeve tattoos, ADHD energy. He’s the James Beard Award–winning chef behind a string of beloved Nashville restaurants past and present, including modern southern standby Husk and two-in-one restaurants Audrey and June. He’s respected by foodies and the hoi polloi alike; ask anyone who did time in Nashville this century and they’ll tell you a snack at the bar of a Sean Brock joint is the reward for a hard day’s work. When we first met, just before Darling opened, we bonded over his massive collection of 45’s in the DJ booth that looks over the restaurant’s bar area. As a secular song from New Orleans preacher Bobby Powell faded out, he wanted to play me something. “Have you heard this?” It was a cover of the Mamas and the Papas’ “California Dreamin’ ” by a band from Jackson, Tennessee, called the Jacksonians. “This is all they ever did! They teased us with this and then never did another thing!” As it played, we paused, we nodded our heads. “This is the one. When people hear this, they stop talking.”

The DJ booth and its speaker cabinets were made custom for the space. “The key is the wood,” he said. “Thirteen-ply Baltic birch, which is impossible to get now.” Within the cabinets: massive Italian speakers, with Altec Lansing drivers upcycled from their first life in movie theaters.

When Brock is at Darling—generally on the weekdays if he’s not traveling—he plays records as the restaurant opens, takes over in the kitchen as the dinner rush starts, then hops back onto the ones and twos after the last dish has been served, going as long into the night as the city will let him. “Getting to play music for people after working in the kitchen all night is my reward now. For the longest time, it was getting a beer after work. Now it’s just watching music bring people happiness.”

As a record collector and a chef, Brock is always thinking about the evolution of local flavor. “What I’m chasing is the knowledge of the sound and the rhythm of a city and a time,” he told me. Take Memphis in the early ’70s, for example. “There was Willie Mitchell at Royal Studios with the Hi label doing Al Green and Ann Peebles, and you had Stax. And then there was this anger building because of the Vietnam War, people are upset, and you feel that in the music.” He grabbed a 7-inch from the pile, the gateway 45 that got him into record collecting: Blackrock’s “Yeah Yeah.” And I did feel it, because the band is a Murderers’ Row of ’70s Memphis session musicians, but mostly because of those speakers. At the right volume and in the right seat, the T. rex from Jurassic Park has nothing on Blackrock.

At its outset, Brock intended Darling to give him a new identity away from the southern cuisine that made him famous. “I didn’t want to come here and cook a bunch of stuff I’d already cooked,” he said. “And we’d been told L.A. wasn’t interested in southern cooking, that it would be too heavy or whatever. So we didn’t cook any southern dishes when we opened.” The next time I stopped in, once Darling had been up and running for a few months, that whole idea had been 86’d. “About three weeks in, everybody was like: Where are the grits, Sean?

“Now this has turned into a place where people come to taste the flavors of the South that I’ve been working on for 20 years—all the seed saving, all the research on traditions and recipes and history. I had gotten a little bored with it, honestly, but all of a sudden I have a brand-new audience for whom it’s completely new, and I’m completely reinvigorated.”

He was describing, almost exactly, the experience Ben and I had hearing Jeff Buckley in Japan. Whether it’s food or music, there’s something revelatory about paying close attention, even to something you think you know.

So that’s the food, and I can tell you from experience that the steak burger is worth the trip (and make that trip early; he makes only 24 a night). As for the music, Brock believes in the power of the listening bar with the zeal of the recent kissa convert. “I had a kind of life-changing moment in a listening bar around 2022,” he says. “I was sitting in the sweet spot—the tip of the triangle, equidistant from two gigantic Tannoys, and the sound became 3D. It floated. I stopped drinking nine years ago, but I thought they’d accidentally given me alcohol.” He got on Nashville Craigslist that very night, searched “Tannoy,” and sent a message to a seller, who wrote back, “I ate in your restaurant last night.” It’s good to be the king.

Brock has seats in the bar area of Darling situated at the perfect distance and angle from the speakers to give customers an out-of-body experience if they want it. But like a legendary southern chef distancing himself from grits, a listening bar in Los Angeles is a risky concept. In Japan, the locals and tourists know to keep quiet and focus on the music, but this is West Hollywood, where everybody wants to be the thing that is focused on. Where if people have a song selector they trust, it’s usually their Peloton instructor. As an Angeleno, I have to worry whether anyone will pipe down long enough to pay attention. “There’s a difference between a night where you come to eat and drink and socialize while music is playing and a night where we’re saying: What we’re playing is special and rare. I want to test that theory soon,” Brock told me. He’s been promoting more and more listening sessions: tributes to jazz record labels Strata-East and International Anthem; a vinyl party hosted by Sylvan Esso; Dolly Parton’s first 12 albums, in order, on those THX-tested speakers.

Even the untrained ear can hear it coming together. I swing through again to hear local legend and NTS radio host DJ Morse Code spin a set of ’70s and ’80s jazz fusion and R&B. The restaurant is busy and buzzing, there’s a nice crowd up front in the good speaker seats, at the top of the triangle. Some of the folks up front are quieting down to hear the music, even as more people stream in, even as the new arrivals are striking their poses, even as we realize that Anderson.Paak is at the bar, that Malibu cult singer-songwriter Ned Doheny is at a table in front of the DJ booth, that Kate Pierson is in the restaurant.

Darling is heating up, and the flavors, the sound, and the rhythm are continuing to evolve. The chef and the city are both learning to listen.

#6796. Black Doves Adds Your Favorite ’90s Scream Queen to Season 2—Meet the New and Returning Cast

It features Keira Knightley, Ben Whishaw, and Neve Campbell.

Black Doves is back, and it’s just as action-packed as ever. Netflix’s deadly, dark, and irreverently funny spy thriller follows a covert intelligence organization as it uncovers and sells secrets among the rich and powerful. Season 1 brought us Keira Knightley as the fearless agent Helen; Sarah Lancashire as her icy spymaster, Reed; and Ben Whishaw as an assassin and Helen’s longtime friend.

That first season closed with many loose ends, so it wasn’t a huge surprise when it was announced that we’d be seeing Helen and Sam in action again. Now, season 2 has brought the pair back to our screens, with a handful of fresh faces to add to the drama. This time around, Sam appears as a washed-up hit man taking low-paid jobs, while Helen is still working for the Black Doves—something that proves to be complicated given her husband, Wallace (played by Andrew Buchan), is now prime minister.

All episodes of the new season dropped on Netflix on November 5. If you’re eager to get to know the cast and characters more closely—and are wondering where you might have seen those new faces before—then read on.

As an agent of the Black Doves, Helen Webb is tasked with gathering highly sensitive information obtained via her politician husband, Wallace. But keeping up the image of a good spouse, mother, and spy all at once becomes increasingly difficult to maintain, and Helen grows conflicted about her life of betrayal. You’ll know Keira Knightley, of course, from her career of starry roles in everything from Disney’s Pirates of the Caribbean to Pride & Prejudice, Bend It Like Beckham, and Love Actually.

Helen’s close friend, a previously high-flying hit man, an ex-employee of the Black Doves, Sam Young attempted to leave behind his former life—but that was easier said than done. Now he’s taking low-paid jobs and drinking alone in bars, before he finds himself roped into business with Helen once again. You’ll know Whishaw from his role in Daniel Craig’s James Bond films—Skyfall, Spectre, and No Time to Die—as well as A Very English Scandal and The Hour.

Relentless and cold, the spymaster Reed is Helen’s handler at the Black Doves and does not mess around. In season 2, we see new, more vulnerable sides of her character unfold via glimpses of her younger self and flashes to the past events that came to define her. You’ll recognize Lancashire from her role in Happy Valley.

Wallace Webb is Helen’s husband and the father of her two children, and he’s completely unaware of her career as a Black Dove. In season 2, his own career has taken off and he becomes the U.K.’s prime minister—a move that comes with highly strict security measures in the backdrop of rising tensions in his marriage. Buchan has also starred in Industry, The Crown, and Carnival Row.

Another assassin, Williams, worked for the crime leader Lenny in the first season of Black Doves. This time around, she’s moved on and formed a partnership with Eleanor, following the death of her previous partner, as well as a friendship with Sam. Hyland has starred in Towards Zero, A Thousand Blows, and Tall Tales and Murder.

As Williams’s partner, Eleanor is another fast-footed assassin with a refreshing sense of humor. Creevy has also starred in Amadeus, The Guest, and Operation Mincemeat.

Laila is an assassin with a fun, nonchalant, and lighthearted personality—a contrast to Helen, whom she clashes with at the start of season 2. You’ll know Ambika Mod for her roles in This Is Going to Hurt and as the co-lead of Netflix’s One Day adaptation.

Mr. Conteh is a senior at Black Doves and is hungry for power. As the fabric of the institution is put under pressure and he is sent to investigate Reed, Conteh becomes increasingly prominent for Helen and her associates. Ceesay has also starred in Alien: Earth.

Cruelly minded and understated in tone, Patrick works for a rival organization to Black Doves, the same group that presents an appealing get-out for Sam when he needs it the most. Riley also starred in the comedy-horror Pride and Prejudice and Zombies as well as Firebrand.

Hard-headed with tunnel vision, Cecile Mason is a U.S. defense envoy, arriving at 10 Downing Street to work on relations between the president and the prime minister ahead of a major peace summit. Campbell was a star of the Scream movies, as well as Party of Five and Wild Things.

Glamorous and sharp-minded, Katia Chernov is the wife of Russian oligarch Alexi Chernov and becomes swept up in the mysteries in the new series. She’s played by Sylvia Hoeks, who has also starred in Blade Runner 2049 and The Girl in the Spider’s Web.

A well-connected Russian oligarch with immeasurable influence and power, Alexi Chernov is a force to be reckoned with in season 2, with fingers in some of the most fragile cases. He’s played by Goran Kostić, whom you may recognize from Ant-Man and the Wasp or Canary Black.

#6795. Design Experts Reveal When It’s Actually OK to Start Decorating for the Holidays

There’s no hard rule—but your HOA may have one.

Now that the Halloween candy bowls are empty, you might find thoughts of tinsel, garland, and fairy lights dancing in your head, but is it really time to trade your pumpkins for holiday trimmings? Some have strong opinions on the topic, half believing the jolly spirit of the holiday season doesn't start until after Thanksgiving, the other rushing to unbox their silver bells as soon as the clock strikes 12 on November 1—but the choice isn’t always yours to make.

If you belong to a Homeowners’ Association (and over 78.1 million Americans do), the question of when to put up your outdoor décor may be dictated by their guidelines. HOAs tend to have rules about holiday decorations, restrictions covering their location (within the boundaries of your yard, of course), sound level (not too loud), safety (no open flames), light color, and when the decorations can be installed. In some communities, you can even be fined for bringing out the twinkle lights and nativity creche before Thanksgiving—this happened to one Florida man in 2002, when he was hit with a fine of $1,000 for putting his lights up on November 6.

If you don’t live in an HOA, you may not get fined for decorating too early, but you will get judged by neighbors who find pre-Thanksgiving holiday displays a little, well, tacky. To be fair, holding until after Thanksgiving does have its perks—why would you pass on the opportunity to decorate a gorgeous fall tablescape? In the spirit of peace on earth and goodwill toward man, we asked design experts what to do. Here’s what they said.

Additional copy by Meghan Shouse and Alyssa Khan.

You might follow a religious tradition that guides your holiday decorating, like placing the Baby Jesus in the nativity on Christmas Day or keeping the tree up until Epiphany. But when it comes to secular etiquette, there are no hard-and-fast rules about when to start (or stop) decking the halls.

“Most people find it tasteful to stick within the weeks or the month of the holiday itself,” points out Lizzie Post, co-president of The Emily Post Institute and author of Emily Post’s Etiquette—The Centennial Edition. “That said, there is no etiquette that dictates it. It’s the same way you can paint your house neon orange with blue stripes—there’s nothing that says you can’t. It’s really, do you want to be the person who does?”

For designer Tan France, the answer to that question is decidedly yes. For the past decade, he and his husband have been decorating their tree on Halloween and taking it down on Valentine’s Day. “I’m British, and we don’t really do Halloween,” he explains. “When I moved here 16 years ago, I was more obsessed with Christmas. So on Halloween day, when everyone else is crafting their costumes, I want to be decorating the tree. And a tree brings my husband and me a lot of joy when we come down and turn the lights on first thing in the morning. It’s just a lovely thing to perk your day up.”

Do people question France about his decision to put up decorations so early, and for so long? “Oh gosh, yeah, everyone asks that,” he says. “Online, the press, neighbors. And my answer is really simple: Why delay joy?”

If you are jonesing to do some holiday decorating, there's good news: While Christmas might be weeks away, there is one happening right now. It’s called Thanksgiving.

“Anytime before midnight of Thanksgiving is too early to decorate,” insists Wendy Kerrigan, founder of Atelier Design in New Orleans. “Filling the house with Christmas decorations too soon really takes away from the beauty of Thanksgiving, moving your mind into the bustle of Christmas.”

Kerrigan wishes more people would celebrate the fall season for longer; rushing to the tinsel takes away from the beauty of the autumn season, “Thanksgiving can be experienced in a special way too, filling the house with fall colors, fall leaves, gourds, and cornucopia,” says Kerrigan. “Instead of trying to hurry the year along, enjoy each family celebration for what it is.”

Even more, sprinting to the Yuletide season robs the holiday of its own magic. “What makes things special when they’re once a year is that they’re experiences you look forward to,” says Vermont-based interior designer Joshua Smith. “How many holidays can we take at one time? Are we doing Black Friday in July now? Let’s just enjoy the season that we’re in!”

When it comes to holiday decorating, many hands make light work. Several designers say they take advantage of the long holiday weekend and enforced family time to rope in some extra help making spirits bright. “In my parents' home, we start decorating right after Thanksgiving dinner. It’s a rare opportunity for everyone to be together, and my siblings and I stay to help out our parents,” says New York-based designer Mikel Welch. “Plus, it’s a much better tradition than terrorizing sales associates on Black Friday Eve!"

Smith has a plan for bringing the Christmas spirit to his home, without shouldering the burden of doing all the hall-decking himself. Last year, his family decorated their trees over Thanksgiving weekend and made it a tree-decorating contest. With his husband's family in town, “they’ll be divided into three teams, decorating three trees, and I’ll go around at the end judging, and everyone will win prizes. We’re not all going to be together at Christmas, so this activity is a fun way to celebrate the season and not do all the work myself,” he says.

Still, early decorators would argue that the amount of effort needed to set up holiday decor is another reason to put up decorations early—and keep them up late. “This is the most miserable time of the year; it gets dark so early, it’s so darn cold. I just think you should do everything you can to bring yourself joy,” says France. “Why put in all that effort for a few weeks?”

Even those who are normally on the side of waiting to decorate agree that desperate times call for dopamine decorating. Whether it’s the state of your inbox or the state of the world that is stressing you out, if you’re feeling a little blue, go right ahead and pull out the red and green.

“In a perfect calendar, the day after Thanksgiving would be my official start to the holiday season—it's traditionally the day when my family goes out into the woods of Tahoe National Forest to chop down our tree, drag it home, and decorate it,” says designer Noz Nozawa of Noz Design. “But this year, the day after Halloween is when I let the holidays start—there's plenty to worry about, and having my nutcrackers and string lights out a few weeks early can only boost spirits.”

Whenever you decorate, it can be a way to bring fun to a not-always joyful world. “We have no control over a lot of things in our lives, but when we walk through the door of our home, we choose to focus our energy and attention on whether that is on fear or on love and joy,” says Smith. “And this holiday season, I think more than ever we have to focus on ways to bring more light to ourselves and to each other.”

When it comes to early decor, “I can handle it from my neighbor; I cannot handle it from a corporation,” says Post. That may be because we know the Christmas displays popping up in a big-box store alongside the Halloween candy are trying to sell us something, while a neighbor decorating is spreading joy, spending their own time and money to brighten the dark winter nights. For a shop, holiday decorating is mercenary. For an individual, it’s a mitzvah.

If you appreciate the joy inspired by holiday displays and do want to start setting them up early or keep them up late, consider finding a middle ground by leaning toward decor that’s more about lighting up the night, rather than marking a particular occasion. “I see a lot of people in Vermont leaving the actual lights up, not so much like blow-up Santas or things like that, but the actual lights up for the entire winter,” says Post.

If you’re a minimalist (or live in a studio apartment), you don’t need a tree to decorate. “If you don’t have a tree, I’m going to invite you to place a hurricane lamp on your coffee table with a wreath of greenery around it and light a candle in the evening,” says Smith. “Because what we know from neuroscience and neuroaesthetics is, you’ve got to engage the senses to nourish the soul!” Light a candle or turn on some twinkle lights, and you’re liable to spark joy as well.

France would argue that even his trees aren’t occasion-specific. “Here’s the thing: I’m Muslim, and my tree and the lights on our house aren’t necessarily for Christmas,” he explains. “It’s to celebrate Thanksgiving, Christmas, and the New Year. This is the most miserable time of the year. It gets dark so early, it gets light so late, it’s so darn cold. I just think do everything you can to bring yourself joy.”

Though you’d never find them rushing to hang their stockings, the designer duo behind the Kansas City-based firm Kobel + Co, Elizabeth Bennett and Mallory Robins, say there’s one design scheme you can always tap into if you find yourself in the class of outliers running to get decor up before Christmas Eve. “Ralph Lauren Holiday isn't just having a moment; it's a classic holiday go-to for so many reasons,” Bennett explains. “Warm crimsons, rich browns, tactile fabrics, and blue-green plaids make a beautiful holiday palette. Layer in antique silver pieces and simple glass ornaments. A little patina and variation keep everything from feeling too matched. The goal is a warm, welcoming home with pieces that feel collected and personal.” After all, you want it to feel like home, even at Christmas.

#6794. The 10 Best Hair Wavers Bazaar Editors Are Actually Using

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Get beach hair all year round with these top-rated tools

As someone with pin-straight Asian hair, I have always admired waves and curls. I have slick, straight, stubborn hair, and—most frustratingly—it resists holding a curl. I’ve tried every trick in the book: extra-hold mousses, setting my curls with pins, and the best strong-hold hairsprays. Nothing seemed to do the trick—until I discovered hair wavers, specialized curling irons that create bends instead of twists.

In recent years, hair wavers have grown in popularity because they're easy to use and deliver effortless-looking results. “The effect [of a waver] is less curled and more textured. It’s great for that effortless, tousled vibe or adding body to finger hair without that much bounce,” says celebrity hairstylist Danielle Priano. Ahead, Priano shares her picks for the best hair wavers, along with some of the Bazaar beauty team’s favorites.

Every time my hairstylist uses this waver at the salon, I notice that my waves can stay for up to three to four days, which is considered a long time for my hair type. The waves also look so natural that they give me that “just permed my hair” effect. Priano is a fan of the tool too, saying, “It heats evenly and creates long-lasting waves with a sleek finish.” Plus, it has tons of features like a two-hour auto shutoff and a clear digital display.

You may have seen this on social media, and for good reason: At under $50, it is a great option for budget-conscious users. It’s also highly rated for both long and short hair and is easy (and safe!) for beginners to use because it has no exposed barrel. We also love that it comes in 1- and 1.25-inch wave sizes, so you can choose the wand that best fits your length and style preference.

Priano also swears by Trademark Beauty’s double-barrel wave iron. “This one’s loved for its wider barrels and even heat distribution,” she says. “It’s great for longer hair or when you want that soft glam wave.” As a bonus, the tool also works well for shorter hair as it creates two waves rather than the usual three.

This is my go-to hair waver at the moment because it’s so versatile. You can adjust the barrel size to get the exact wave you want, from tight to soft and beachy. (And you also have full control over the temperature.) I’m super lazy, so I like to opt for something that has full capabilities in one.

You can’t beat this price, which makes Bed Head’s tool a great entry-level styler. That, along with its tourmaline ceramic barrels, 10 heat settings, and easy digital controls, makes it super straightforward to use. The barrels themselves are also on the large side, which creates a great beachy effect in the final look. And if you’re accident-prone, take comfort in the fact that it comes with a protective glove, too.

“This waver is pricey, at nearly $200, but it’s also the most versatile. It comes with three barrels—a waver, a rod, and a clamp—so you can create any type of wave or curl your heart desires,” says Bazaar beauty director Jenna Rosenstein. “I have thick hair, and it works just as well on my hair as it does on those with fine, thin hair, since the heat is customizable up to 450 degrees. It’s also great to travel with!”

This one is another Bazaar editor favorite: “While I do own two different hair wavers, they both serve a unique vibe. I love this INH Triple Barrel when I’m going for a looser, more lived-in wave,” says Sarah Olivieri, Bazaar senior designer. “It really gives the illusion of salty beach hair freshly dried from the sun.”

“I love the look of effortless waves, but creating seamless waves without creases or harsh demarcations between crimps can be tricky,” says Bazaar beauty market and commerce editor Katie Intner. “I love the jumbo Mane waver because the large barrel size gives a more natural wave and crimps hair in under a few minutes. Plus, it’s dual voltage, which is great for travel, and gives a surprising amount of wave longevity.” Even better, the base is detachable and can be used with any of the brand’s other hair tool attachments.

This waver is on the pricey side, but that’s because it has the brand’s next-gen features. Think: even heating and a curved—not cylindrical—shape on the barrels that gives that ideal “effortless” look. They’re also finished with an anodized coating that adds shine and reduces frizz. Who doesn’t want that?

If you aren’t a bona fide hairstyling expert and want a hair waver that doesn’t require much technique, this option from WavyTalk might be a good fit. It has a built-in timer you can set from four to 10 seconds that beeps when it’s time to move on to the next section of hair. Its deep V-shaped plates and dual-barrel design sculpt hair for a more pronounced wave.

Luckily, it’s “fairly straightforward,” Priano says. “You start by sectioning your hair; the smaller the section, the more defined the wave. Then, working from the roots down, clamp the waver onto the hair and hold for a few seconds, usually 5 to 10, depending on your hair type and heat setting. Move down the same strand, overlapping slightly to continue the wave pattern all the way to the ends. Repeat throughout the entire head, and once cooled, you can brush it out for a softer, more lived-in texture or leave it defined for more drama.”

What’s the Best Way to Prep Your Hair Before Using a Waver?

To minimize potential damage, you’ll want to do a few things first: “Always start with clean, dry hair; wavers are not meant for wet styling. I recommend applying a heat protectant from mid-lengths to ends to shield the hair from damage,” Priano adds. “If you’re looking to boost volume and hold, add a lightweight texturizing spray or a mousse before blow-drying. And don’t forget to detangle. Smooth, tangle-free strands help the waver glide better and create more uniform results.”

Is There a Difference Between a Waver and a Curling Iron?

“The main difference between a hair waver and a curling iron lies in the shape and result. A curling iron wraps hair around a single barrel, creating more classic, rounded curls or ringlets, depending on the size of the barrel and the technique,” Priano says. “A waver, on the other hand, presses the hair between barrels to form deep, wave-like ridges.”

#6791. These Cozy Shoes Are Too Chic to Keep Indoors

Slippers in public? These fashionable pairs make a convincing case.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

At this point, we’ve all probably accepted that slippers are no longer strictly house shoes. They’ve been part of our everyday wardrobes for years, and it’s hardly unusual to see someone wearing a fuzzy pair of slip-ons with jeans and a nice coat. What’s more interesting is how far brands have taken the idea lately. And honestly, some of these new styles are just really good shoes, slippers or not.

Take Ugg’s Western-inspired designs. Cowboy boots aren’t going anywhere, and the brand has found a way to bring that look into the comfortable footwear it’s known for. It’s a fun alternative to a traditional Western boot, especially if you want something a little more relaxed for everyday wear.

Brunch’s collaboration with Madhappy is another one worth knowing about. The two brands share co-founder Joshua Sitt, so the partnership makes sense, but the shoes themselves are what caught our attention. Made with colorful crochet and lined with shearling, they look like someone turned a chunky knit sweater into a slipper. And considering how much we love a good sweater this time of year, why shouldn’t our feet get a sweater of their own?

For Sitt, the idea of making slippers part of an everyday wardrobe has always been central to Brunch. “Originally, the entire concept for the brand was taking the hotel slipper product which is famously associated with vacation and travel, and bringing it into something you can wear every single day inside and outside of your home,” he tells ELLE.

You can see that thinking at work in the Madhappy collaboration. “The shoe itself morphs the comfort that both brands prioritize as well as the fashion pieces of both brands,” he says.

What’s particularly interesting is that this isn’t limited to brands you’d normally associate with slippers. Hunter, whose rain boots have been a wardrobe staple for years, has a cozy option of its own. Simkhai has entered the category, too, and Alo has a pair that feels especially fitting for the Pilates-to-coffee crowd. It’s a pretty good indication of just how much room there is to play with a shoe that used to be purely practical.

The styling is arguably the best part. A Western-inspired pair would look great with straight-leg jeans and a suede jacket, while a softer, more understated slipper can make tailored trousers and a wool coat feel less formal. You don’t need to rethink your wardrobe to wear them. In fact, that’s part of the appeal.

As Sitt points out, slippers haven’t exactly been the most considered purchase in our wardrobes. Ask people what pair they own, and “you’ll get some crickets,” he says. Or maybe they “stole a pair at a wedding.” No offense to wedding favors, but we think it’s time for an upgrade.

Whether you’re looking for an alternative to your usual boots or simply want a pair of slippers you won’t want to change out of before leaving the house, these are the styles worth a closer look. Shop our favorites below.

#6790. 13 Clever Details I Saw at the 2026 Kips Bay Decorator Show House in Dallas

Check out the first ever home gym in Kips Bay Show House history.

The seventh annual Kips Bay Decorator Show House in Dallas returns to 2999 Turtle Creek Boulevard, but don’t expect it to look anything like it did in 2025. A new group of the country’s leading designers has taken on the 25,000-square-foot French Renaissance estate, transforming each room to showcase bold ideas, highlight exceptional craftsmanship, and reveal the upcoming trends we can expect to see in the world of interior design.

Visit the show house and see each of the 22 designers’ rooms, from the first dedicated home gym in Kips Bay history by Dallas-based Platinum Gym Design, to a small but mighty European-inspired kitchen by Stephenson Home, to a 1970s-inspired primary bathroom fit with a glass brick shower and curtained-off water closet by Nuance Interiors. With sponsors like Benjamin Moore, The Shade Store, The Rug Company, and more, the vibrant and personality-filled spaces come as no surprise.

As always, proceeds from the show house go to the Kips Bay Boys & Girls Club, which provides educational, enrichment, and developmental programs to thousands of young people, as well as Dallas-based nonprofits Dwell with Dignity and The Crystal Charity Ball.

To see this celebration of exceptional design, you can purchase tickets here and visit the house from Saturday, November 14th to Sunday, December 6th. However, if you can’t make it to the Lone Star State in time, check out some of our favorite moments from the event below.

Kelsi Lamberth of Nuance Interiors pulled inspiration from the 1970s for her primary bathroom, separating the shower from the main prep space using a curved glass brick wall. The sculptural aspects can be found throughout in this groovy space.

In the primary bedroom, Troy Rivington of Rivington Marx Interiors cocooned the room in a dark floral wallpaper and accessorized with other moody botanicals throughout. He was inspired by the romance of 1940s Cairo, dressing the room up like it belonged to a well-traveled collector.

As the first ever dedicated home gym at the Kips Bay Show House, Peter Hoffman of Platinum Gym Design chose a refined, luxurious setup. He named the room Tanren after the Japanese art of hammering and refining metal, and used walnut in both the equipment and the ceiling slats, stainless steel frames, and brown leather accents to complete the exercise space.

In this bespoke bathroom, Russ Peters and Bill Cates of Peters Cates Design lined the wall behind the Studio McGee x Kohler bathtub with a rich merlot tile by RIAD Tile. It’s a beautiful statement and a fabulous way to bring more texture and dimension to the room.

In this tucked away library space, filled with collected finds, designers Christina Garcia Lysaught and Grayson Knight of Layered Dimensions finished off this dark space with a stunning statement mirror. Around the perimeter are resin busts of wildlife, like owls and deer, bringing in a bejeweled touch to the room.

The small but mighty kitchen, designed by Katie Stephenson of Stephenson Home, is crowned with a crackled-gold blooming wallpaper, bringing a warm balance to the rest of the room. It’s covered in birds, butterflies, and blossoms, and it wraps the cook space in a golden glow, reminding all who enter that this room should be as beautiful as it is functional.

The citron paint color wrapping the walls in Jen Bienvenu’s entrance hall is undoubtedly enchanting and serves as the perfect backdrop for her “After the Hunt” theme, but it’s the lampshades made from old silk saris that tie everything together for us. They provide such a magical warmth and really capture the essence of a collector’s treasure room.

Related Story: Almost Every Piece in This Layered Arkansas Home Was a Lucky Antique Find

Step through the citron entry and enter the round room, designed in collaboration by Helen Bergin Interiors and Lichten Architects. The walls are softened by long curtains from The Shade Store, which create beautiful juxtaposition with the curved, sculptural silver media console by Asher Gillman. The actual turntable is flush to the surface, giving it a seamless look while it emits wonderful sound.

In the Withdrawing Room by Jen Stevens of Fonde Interiors, plaster botanicals by artist Amanda Norman come to life as they crawl from the mantel along the chimney breast, escaping to the ceiling. The vines were handmade by Norman and capture the essence of a garden left undisturbed, showing that this formal-at-first-glance room has a wilder side.

The rosy palette in designer Jen Dean’s—founder of Jede Interiors—grand bathroom is topped off with a hand-painted floral mural that covers the ceiling. The rose-gold shine reflects the natural light in the room and give it more depth, while the natural motifs spread across the rest of the space.

Inside this observatory bedroom by sisters Amy Mobley and Katie Norrid of City Hill Design, the Verellen bed is raised up from the ground on a custom platform, emphasizing the importance of the furniture’s role in this space meant for relaxation and rest. It’s wrapped in a striped silk carpet that beckons your touch with a chartreuse mohair sofa at the foot.

Colorful and collected, this vibrant library by designer Betsy Wentz is not visually quiet. The maximalist space is covered in pattern-mixed Schumacher from ceiling to floor, and the designer pumped up the volume by opting for this triangular table in this tucked away corner.

In designer Amy Switzer’s textural bar lounge—filled with plush chairs, unique artwork, large tassel accessories, and beaded cigarettes—it’s the stone details that really make the room sing. Onyx cornices crown the windows, blue agate inlays decorate the floor, and a banquette which curves around a custom stone and wood table bring a natural coolness to the room that only stone can bring.

#6789. The 12 Best Round Brushes for a Salon-Level Blowout

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Perfect your next hair look with these expert-loved picks

Perfecting an at-home blowout once required contortionist-level abilities combined with a little patience and a lot of skill, especially when styling the back of your head. But nowadays, thanks to the advancement in hair tools—from high-powered blow-dryers to one of the best round brushes—it's easier than ever to craft sleek styles that rival your local salon without dealing with arm fatigue.

Unlike regular boar-bristle or vented brushes, round brushes feature a cylindrical shape in which hair wraps around for a seamless dry. Though many of these tools are crafted from similar materials, they're also available in a range of sizes to suit your hair texture and unique beauty needs. But regardless of the brush you choose, experts say a polished dry isn't achieved with dripping wet hair. "The key is to get all the moisture out," celebrity hairstylist Priscilla Valles tells Bazaar. "To achieve the smooth look, you need to keep drying until the hair is completely dry."

New York City-based hairstylist Jackson Simmonds recommends having two to three round brushes in your collection—just in case you change your hairstyle. “To keep it simple, think, smaller brushes for shorter lengths, bigger brushes for longer lengths,” he explains.

Here, we've narrowed down some of the best round brushes from Oribe, Sisley Paris, and more, all highly recommended by experts. Whether your hair is short, extra long, curly, or super fine, these top picks can be game-changing to help deliver a bouncy, full-bodied blowout from the comfort of your bathroom.

When we polled hairstylists, several mentioned Olivia Garden's hairbrushes. They win best overall round brush because they're foolproof: They come in a full range of sizes (general rule of thumb: the longer your hair, the larger and longer your barrel should be), and it's hard to get your hair tangled in an Olivia Garden round brush. If you need a place to start, the Ceramic + Ion Thermal Hairbrush is a bestseller. But you can also go through the brand's hairbrush guide to find your perfect fit.

Bristle type: syntheticBarrel material: ceramicSize options: 0.5 inches to 4.25 inches

A boar-bristle round brush is ideal for someone with coarse hair texture, as it provides more stretch and tension than a ceramic or ionic brush. This line is a favorite of stylists like Simmonds, who says, “They have a huge selection of sizes, bristle density, and length, so you have an option for whatever style you're trying to create.” He adds that the cork handle is a plus: “It makes it light and breezy in your hand and never slips away as you're working.”

Bristle type: nylonBarrel material: woodSize options: 35 to 85 millimeters

Oribe crafted a brush that's as efficient as it is pretty. Atop the stunning resin handle is a mix of boar bristles and nylon pins that move through hair without damaging or tugging and help distribute the natural oils in hair for more shine. And although it is an investment (the handle is hand-finished), this is a tool you’ll use for years to come.

Bristle type: boar, nylonBarrel material: Italian resinSize options: medium, large

If you're in search of a round brush with frizz-control benefits, look no further than this top-rated, ergonomic favorite from Drybar. After all, who better to go to than the blowout blueprint? Its bristles are optimized with ionic technology to mitigate frizz while providing salon-level tension control. It even has a thumbprint grip to help maximize your styling experience.

Bristle type: boarBarrel material: ceramicSize options: medium, large

If you're already a fan of Moroccanoil's coveted hair staples, allow us to introduce you to its top-rated ceramic and ion-infused round brush. It heats up when in use with a blow-dryer, making drying and detangling hair a breeze. Designed for medium to long hair, the large barrel covers a good amount of surface area without tugging. Plus, the ceramic finish leaves hair extra-shiny.

Bristle type: nylon, ionicBarrel material: ceramicSize options: 55 millimeters, 45 millimeters

This $14 round brush has earned a five-star rating from beauty lovers. The bristles are heat-resistant vegan boar bristles, and they're pretty densely packed, so you can get a grip and lift on the hair while still allowing strands to glide through without pulling. The bristles also aren’t overly stiff, so they won’t scratch your scalp as you’re styling. And we’re always here for a no-slip grip!

Bristle type: vegan boarBarrel material: bambooSize options: 2.78 inches

These U.K.-made brushes are celebrity stylist Benjamin Mohapi’s personal favorites. “They are the oldest brush company in the world, and I've used them for the entire 40 years of my career,” he explains, adding that the brand has options in boar bristle, vegan bristle, and nylon ceramic. “As far as I’m concerned, there is nothing better.”

Bristle type: nylonBarrel material: ceramicSize options: 45mm and 60mm

Here's a brush from the experts at Sisley Paris that's literally made for your next blowout. Its boar and nylon bristles are gentle yet effective, and compactly housed in a wooden barrel. The soft bristles provide hold for easy drying and also keep the hair super soft. We also appreciate that it has scalp-stimulating bristles, so it feels especially nice when you use it at the root.

Bristle type: boar, nylonBarrel material: woodSize options: medium

This popular round brush proves that getting a polished, salon-level finish doesn't have to cost a ton. Ideal for reducing frizz, the staple uses a ceramic-coated barrel for even heat distribution. Plus, it features an ergonomic handle for extra comfort, which you’ll love whether you’re new to blowing out your hair at home or just need the extra grip. Once you get the hang of things, you may decide to upgrade, but it’s a great starter brush.

Bristle type: nylonBarrel material: ceramicSize options: 1.7 inches, 1.3 inches

The three sturdy knobs on the handle of this round brush mean you don't have to worry about the tool accidentally slipping out of your hands mid-blow-dry. Plus, the placement of the bristles in Philip B.'s tool makes it easy to give your roots an extra lift. With a mix of boar bristles and nylon for extra reinforcement, it’s a sturdy brush that’s earned stellar reviews for good reason.

Bristle type: boar, nylonBarrel material: mahogany Size options: large, medium

Can a round brush be chic? This one could be mistaken for a found object with its teak handle. Simmonds recommends the Japanese-made brand, saying that it offers “quality brushes that deliver results, look great coming out of my kit, and last for ages with proper care.” The mix of boar bristle and heat-resistant nylon pins is also ideal for optimal hold and shine.

Bristle type: boar, heat-resistant nylonBarrel material: teak woodSize options: several

Balmain Hair may sound like a fun splurge (and it is), but Simmonds says the brand has become a go-to for his ceramic tools. “Their range is every bit as luxurious as you would expect,” he says. This brush in particular helps seal the cuticle for an extra-smooth and shiny blowout, and can even work for extra-long hair. Have short hair? Try the 25mm version for the same luxury experience.

Bristle type: syntheticBarrel material: ceramic coatingSize options: 25mm–53mm

Like anything worth learning, styling your hair with one of the best round brushes takes practice. "Ask your stylist if they’ll do a lesson," offers Gamez. It also might be helpful to reference a YouTube or TikTok tutorial. The general guidance is: start fresh out of the shower, detangle your hair, and then begin blow-drying and brushing small sections of hair, working your way around your head. You can start in the front or back sections. Professional hair colorist Tylor Johnson recommends starting with the front sections around your hairline, as your hairline pieces are the most important and require the most precision, and you don't want to be fatigued when styling them.

What Are the Benefits of Using a Round Brush and Blow-Dryer?

It’s all about the shape. “The round brush allows the hair to wrap around it, creating a bend in the hair as it dries and cools,” explains Mohapi, who explains that a regular hairbrush should not be used for heat-styling. “Expensive brushes like Masons are not designed for direct heat, and you will ruin a perfectly beautiful brush,” says the stylist.

Several top stylists prefer to use round brushes and blow-dryers separately rather than an all-in-one device, as it enhances control while minimizing hair damage. "Blow-dry brushes tend to be high heat, so if you’re putting that tool on soaking wet hair, you’re going to overdry and damage it," explains Gamez. "A blow-dryer allows you to create space between the heat and the hair shaft as needed. You can control the air direction of a dryer with your nozzle, pointing downward and sealing the cuticle, resulting in shinier, smoother hair, as opposed to a hot brush that blows air outward in all directions."

On the other hand, blow-dry round brushes definitely have their benefits. According to Valles, one key factor is their overall ease of use to craft salon-worthy styles at home. "A blow-dry brush makes the process so much easier," Valles says. "Some people aren't as coordinated handling a brush and dryer simultaneously, but the blow-dry brush gives the same results and is easier to manage." At the end of the day, the best round brush for you is one you actually enjoy using regularly.

“Many people wouldn’t want to use a boar bristle brush for ethical reasons, and there are lots of good alternatives, so boar bristle isn’t the only option,” explains Mohapi. “It is, however, a highly effective material and is used extensively in hairbrush manufacturing globally.” Here’s why: “Boar bristles are highly tolerant of heat and won’t melt or buckle under the intense extremes of a packed Friday at the salon. The fibers are both strong and flexible and have the unique quality of being able to almost grab the hair and hold it in place as you pass it through the brush.”

That said, the stylist adds that some synthetics are nearly as good. They just may not last as long. “Nylon is a different thing altogether,” he notes. “Nylon will close down the cuticle and help create a shiny, slick finish to the hair. This is great for straight hair, but nylon brushes take all the body out of the hair, so when you use a nylon round brush, it has a different type of finish.”

#6786. We Tested Luxury Sheet Sets for Three Years—These Are the Only Ones Worth the Splurge

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

For hot sleepers, restless sleepers, and anyone tired of scratchy bedding.

You deserve better than itchy, scratchy sheets on your bed at night. Upgrading your bedding is more than a bedroom aesthetic choice; it’s an investment in your well-being. I know this firsthand—as both a design editor and an insomniac. I’ve tested over a dozen of the best luxury sheets on the market in my journey to fix my sleep routine. The right sheet set lets me drift off much more easily, and I’m here to help you find one of your own.

What sets “luxury” sheets apart from your run-of-the-mill bargain bedding? It's a combination of texture, quality, aesthetic, and price point. The set should feel good the moment it hits your skin. The fabric, too, should be durable and constructed to avoid pilling, tearing, or any of that less-than-best feel. As the name implies, luxury sheets often come with a higher price tag, so you'll want to make sure they're worth the extra money.

I have every sleep issue you could think of: I'm a hot sleeper, I snore, I toss and turn, and I have eczema, so I have to be picky about what fabric goes in my bed. Luckily, my fellow editors, testers, and I have spent over three years sleeping on every kind of sheet under the sun, from fluffy flannel to silky eucalyptus. And now we’re revealing the ones that totally upgraded our sleep routines. Scroll on to meet our MVBs (most valuable bedding).

I first tested the Buffy Breeze sheets in my evaluation, and beyond the beautiful sheen that made the sheets look luxe, the cooling feeling of the eucalyptus fibers completely won me over. After months of trying other sheets, these were still my favorite.

I'm an incredibly hot sleeper, and I can't stand waking up to sticky sheets. These sheets from Buffy, made from moisture-wicking eucalyptus, gave me the most refreshing sleep I've had in my life. The first quality that impressed me about these sheets was how well they shifted with my constant tossing and turning. Even as they gathered underneath me, I was never uncomfortable. They're lightweight and breathable without the gauzy texture of other airy sheets. I genuinely looked forward to sinking into them at the end of the day.

I also tested Buffy's Cloud pillows under the pillowcases in this set and was impressed with how well they held their shape, even as I bunched them up under my neck. I was convinced I’d have horrible neck pain any time I slept on a pillow that wasn’t the butterfly shape I have for side sleepers. Imagine how shocked I was by how comfortable and long I slept.

Colorways: 25 | Sizes: Full, Queen, King, Cal King | Materials: Eucalyptus (lyocell)

Quince bedding is one of the best ways to get that luxury sheet feel on a tighter budget. Tester Jessica Cherner has tested their bamboo, linen, and percale varieties, but the bamboo sheets stood out for their silky feel. "These sheets are so great because they have a tiny bit of stretch to them, which makes them even softer," Cherner says. "What makes them so soft is the fact that they're made from 100 percent viscose bamboo (the pulp), which is a bit more luxurious than rayon."

Colorways: 15 | Sizes: Twin, Twin XL, Full, Queen, King, Cal King | Materials: 100 percent viscose from organic bamboo

Many die-hard fans of cotton bedding can’t imagine sleeping on anything else. The cool, crisp feeling of Hommey’s striped cotton bedding is what won over McKenzie. “These are my perfect all-purpose sheets,” McKenzie says. “It’s not for your hottest days or your coldest nights, but for all of the days that fall in between. It’s a sturdy, well-woven 100 percent organic cotton, designed for year-round use and reliable comfort. It has a crisp feel that maintains its shape, and it rarely wrinkles, even after I’ve spent all night tossing and turning. I’m also a hot sleeper, and any thread count above 400 TC starts to get a bit stuffy for my taste. These fall at a perfect 300 TC, thick enough to insulate from cool air.”

Despite being a hot sleeper, McKenzie still needed a solid set of winter sheets for the coldest nights. My favorite detail is the holly-red whipstitching; I just wish it went all the way around the border of the set. I was sad to take these off my bed to test the others.”

Colorways: 13 | Sizes: Twin, Full, Queen, King | Materials: 100 percent linen or 55 percent European linen and 45 percent long staple cotton

Don’t be intimidated by cashmere’s delicate nature. From night one, these sheets draped over me like silk, but were more buttery soft and fuzzy than cold and smooth. As someone who sweats easily, the lightweight feel was welcome. I'd still reach for these sheets in the summer, or whenever I had a guest I wanted to impress.”

Not only is the viscose from bamboo used here moisture-wicking and temperature-regulating, so I'm not waking up in a pool of sweat, but they're also ridiculously silky smooth."

Plus, when I think about the other supremely soft sheets I have—most of which are more than double the price of these Lands' End ones—I feel like I'm getting a bargain."

Colorways: 8 | Sizes: Twin, Twin XL, Full, Queen, King, Cal King, Split King | Materials: 100 percent Premium Supima cotton

It's flowy and always looks neat. Even though it's meant to optimize the comfort of a Purple mattress, I'm confident it would make any mattress brand feel softer.

#6782. MobLand Episode 9 Sets Up a Seriously Violent Finale

In season 2’s penultimate episode, Harry faces down Frankie to save Gina, while Kat McAllister calls Black Flag on the Harrigans.

Consider Tom Hardy’s smile. I know you’ve got a lot to think about right now, like the rising cost of a flat white, celebrity sex criminals, and which brand of pasta to stockpile, but put that on ice for a minute and consider Tom Hardy’s smile.

I could count on one hand the number of times I’ve seen him crack a grin on screen. He’s like those actors who’ll go nude only if it’s tasteful and in the name of art. Hardy’s smiles are anything but tasteful. When they happen, it’s as surprising as any jump scare.

Take episode 9 of MobLand, when Harry gloriously threatens Frankie. “That fucking face of yours, I’ll ‘ave that,” he growls like Burglar Bill’s psychopath cousin. “I’ll wear it to carnival as a mask when I cut it off with this pint glass.”

The eyebrows arch and the lips creak upward and the face cracks like a dam with a hairline fracture, exploding into a horrifying clown grin. Then… suddenly it’s gone, like a split atom, back into the cosmic darkness from whence it came. They say the best actors can tell a story with their eyes. Hardy can tell one with just his teeth, and it’s brilliant and it’s terrifying.

Anyway, that was my highlight of episode 9.

What else? Well, the cold open, where Frankie gatecrashes Kat’s ambassadors’ convention disguised as a waiter to threaten her. Kat is ready: “Under the 1961 Vienna Convention, unlawful entry into a sovereign embassy is not seen as trespass but as an attack on the nation. I call Black Flag and you do not make it off this fucking rug.”

Talking of smiles, Gina wipes one clean off Maeve’s face with an exit straight out of RuPaul’s Drag Race. You’ll recall Maeve’s dastardly scheme: persuade Gina to fake a suicide attempt, thus breaking Harry’s spirit and forcing him out. Instead, Gina sashays away, leaving a message in lipstick on the mirror: “Suck my balls, witch.”

Meow. I don’t think Ru could have put it better herself.

Maeve turns back to Eddie. “You want to be a Harrigan, there’s one last task: Harry.”

It’s not long before the smile returns to Maeve’s face, when she wakes to find Conrad in his pants, strapping on a bulletproof vest. “What are you doing, man? It’s not Friday. Friday night is bondage night!”

Actually, Conrad has pried a tasty piece of intel from JoJo, the Frankie henchman he tortured to death: a large shipment of drugs is landing at Tilbury Docks, ripe for the nicking. Off they drive with a boot full of weapons, soundtracked not by a true-crime podcast but by the bloodcurdling recording of JoJo’s torture.

Trouble is, just as they arrive, the ghouls of Conrad’s past start stalking the corridors of his mind again, whispering. “Leave me alone,” he cries, swerving the car. Maeve shows an almost uncharacteristic amount of empathy. “It’s okay,” she soothes.

Still, the heist is off. Conrad is in no fit state to add to the ranks of the ghosts he’s made. They drive home.

If there were an award for the weirdest scene of the series, it would go to Eddie asking Paul on a date. “Wondered if you fancied popping out for a pint later.” He might as well have asked him to go dogging in a Chipping Norton lay-by.

Really, he wants a bomb to kill Harry. Paul laughs it off with a monologue on the history of bombmaking, until Eddie plays his ace: “What if I tell you I know it was you who told Harry that I was in Ireland? I wonder what Maeve would think.” Paul’s smile dissolves. Maeve would have his guts for sausages. Or, in Eddie’s words: “How does it feel to have your bollocks in my briefcase?”

And so to the meat and potatoes of the episode: Harry and Frankie’s tête-à-tête.

It starts at Harry’s home. Jan has returned to him, having realized she’s safer in his giant hands than in anyone else’s. He wants to whisk her to a safehouse, urgently. So she does what anyone would do when their life is in imminent danger: run a bath, throw on some Damien Rice and soak her troubles away.

Trouble is, those troubles are literally downstairs with assault rifles, with Frankie close behind. Naturally, Harry keeps an even bigger gun under the sofa, so he festoons the walls of his home with their brains before leading Jan out to the car. Gina has just arrived. So, alas, has Frankie.

Still in his dressing gown, Frankie kidnaps Gina and spirits her off to the panic room/prison cell beneath his London home.

There, Gina and Frankie bond over a spot of smoothie play, hurling their drinks in each other’s faces, followed by a round of Nerf. It’s sweet. He likes her. Maybe he even gets her. She is, as he later tells Harry, “spunky, full of spunk.” That may just be what keeps her alive.

Over at Kevin HQ (a shipping container on an industrial estate), Harry, Jan and Kevin await news of Gina. Kevin pitches a new plan: go John Wick on the lot of them.

“You call Frankie and tell him we accept on the condition he returns Gina safely… then we meet Conrad and Maeve and blow their fucking brains out.” Then Eddie, then Frankie, then, through Bella’s nous, defenestrate Kat McAllister via legal channels.

Conrad, whose marbles have been rolling under the sofa all episode, is not expected to put up much of a fight.

“I’m taking over tonight,” fumes Kevin. “You’re either with me or against me.”

Well, no need for that just yet. Harry has a plan to save Gina. Leverage? Another child he can senselessly murder? No, no. Simply the good old-fashioned threat of violence.

At the parley, they’re yin and yang: Frankie the exuberant chatterbox, Harry the man who’ll never use two words when one will do. Frankie squeezes Harry’s arm: “Cor, feel those muscles. I mean, I’m tasty, but if we got in an actual ruck, I wouldn’t stand a snowflake. I bet you could kill me with your bare hands…”

“You’re not gonna kill her,” he growls wearily.

“You mind testing that?” And Frankie orders a goon to blow Gina’s head off with a shotgun and film it so they can watch.

He hands Harry the phone for the tersest of chats with his gaffer-taped daughter: “Try to stay calm. I’m gonna get you out of this as soon as I can.” Click. Frankie can’t believe it. “Is that it? Unless you’ve got some brilliant plans, that is actually a bit shit. What are you up to, Harry?”

Harry’s reply is considered and simple: “If I was you, I would’ve had some really big, scary fucking geezers in here with you to keep you safe, to prevent the exact scenario that is unfolding right here.”

“B-b-b-but I’ve just got to pick up the phone,” stammers Frankie.

The tension is suddenly tighter than a celebrity sex criminal’s grip on the prison soap.

Harry’s purring now: “It took me seven seconds to walk through those doors from the car… No more cutting around in the old burgundy tracksuit, that’s for sure. Not without wearing a fucking colostomy bag hanging off your limp arse.”

Then the scary smile, the face-mask line and the clincher: “Speaking of seven seconds, that’s all you’ve got left to call your mate Joey and let Gina go. Three... two...”

But Frankie has one more trick up his tracksuit: he gets Kat McAllister on the phone. Remember the black flag stuff at the top of the episode?

She’s offering Harry another chance: “As of tonight there is no Harrigans. But the good news: you have a new job. Thank you for what you’re about to do for us. I am looking forward to you working for the team. Black Flag, motherfucker.”

In other words, Black Flag has been called, and the Harrigans are its target. It’s a fitting phrase. Pirates flew a black flag to tell ships to surrender or else. In motor racing, it means you’re disqualified. And it’s a snarling late-seventies hardcore punk band, whose “Nervous Breakdown” gives this episode its title. Kat’s version is all three at once.

Cut to the Cotswolds, where police are dredging the lake. They’ve pulled out a body. Is it Richie’s? It’s someone’s, and I’d wager they didn’t die smiling. Hardy, on the other hand, lives to smile another day.

The chips are down, the traps are laid, and there’s one episode left. Does Frankie have one last plan up his tracksuit? Will Conrad pull his brain back from the brink? Will Maeve get the last laugh? Or will it be Harry, God help us all?

As Shakespeare puts it in Macbeth: “There’s daggers in men’s smiles: the near in blood, the nearer bloody.”

#6778. Women’s Sports Teams Deserve Better Than What Their Homes Are Giving Them

Apparently, we’re going through this...again!

Gotham FC, the current reigning National Women’s Soccer Team champions, are on their way to winning another title. But their journey—which includes the chance to host their first semifinal game at home—has come with a major surprise: a change of venues. After their quarterfinal match against the Seattle Reign FC ended with a messed-up pitch and a fine against Gotham FC due to its conditions, the team was forced to find another place for their next match. The bigger issue? Their only option is a 5,000-person stadium located in Randall’s Island, New York.

Over 11,000 people made their way to Sports Illustrated Stadium—the home to Gotham FC as well as the MLS’s New York Red Bulls—to see them take down Seattle Reign FC. This means that less than half of that audience will be able to catch them at the match that can lead them to the finals.

While pitch conditions are absolutely important for the safety of the players and a big enough reason to change venues, the option to upgrade the pitch to a hybrid model was shut down by none other than New York Red Bulls due to a friendly game—AKA a game that does not count towards their season—they have on Saturday. Although the pitch would be ready for Gotham FC’s big game, RBNY shut down the plan as it wouldn’t be ready for their planned friendly. All of a sudden, a game-making match for a winning women’s team was being moved to a much smaller space due to a men’s game that does not count towards anything.

If you’re getting a sense of deja vu, that’s because this is not the first time a women’s team had to change their plans due to a men’s team. The WNBA’s Atlanta Dream were forced to move one of their WNBA Finals games from 8 p.m. to 12:30 p.m. on a Thursday. While the move was caused by them switching venues—going from their home at the Gateway Center, which fits 3,500 to the State Farm Arena which seats 17,000—it was also caused by a scheduled Atlanta Hawks game, a team that hasn’t won an NBA title since the 1950s.

It’s clear that no matter where they go, these women’s teams’ homes are not giving them what they need: a space where they can continue to host their ever-growing fanbases without fear of having to fall to the needs of a men’s team. The WNBA had a 21 percent increase in attendance to their games in 2026. The NWSL also gained some new faces in the crowd with a 3 percent increase, according to Sports Business Journal.

In 2028, Gotham FC will move to Etihad Park, a new stadium that will hold 25,000 fans in Queens, NYC. However, they’ll still have the problem of sharing the field with New York City FC, meaning this problem can always come up again.

A clear solution would be to give these incredible teams their own venues, but it’s evident that, financially, building a new stadium is not the easiest for some of these teams who are still growing. But, maybe next time, these men’s teams will just get the hint and let the real winners take over. It’s their home, too, after all.

#6776. The 10 Leather Jackets You (Yes, You) Could Actually Pull Off

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

All you need is the right style, and you’re the coolest guy at the bar.

I bought my first leather jacket at a vintage fair in an old English cathedral. I was 19, and a middle-aged British lady told me I looked good in it. Clearly a sales technique, but it worked, and I shelled out my grocery money for it. I had grand plans of fixing up my dad’s old Honda the following semester and riding around campus in a sick jacket. For various reasons, that didn’t end up happening, and I ended up on a 50cc Yamaha Zuma, a fun little scooter but one where a creaky moto jacket looked more like a comic prop than something out of a Dennis Hopper film. C’est la vie; we all look a little ridiculous in our 20s.

Despite what cinema tells you, you don’t need to ride a motorcycle in order to wear a leather jacket. Or fly a WWII-era fighter plane. Or be Steve McQueen photographed for Esquire. Leather jackets are imbued with the undeniable cool factor of their original functionality—like work jackets, chore coats, and waxed canvas, the modern menswear landscape supports multiple usages. From happy hours to midwestern winters to, yes, actually riding a motorcycle, we’ve rounded up nine Esquire-approved picks to guide your foray into the iconic outerwear. And to come full circle, I did actually test some of these on a motorcycle. More than a decade later, I finally got my license and am fixing up that old Honda; thank God for YouTube.

Leather jackets can be tricky to get right. Too trend focused and it’s a thousand-dollar flash in the pan; too trend agnostic and you get a museum artifact rather than a wearable piece. Ayr nailed the balance on its cleverly named Scrambler jacket, which draws on moto history and sits in the Goldilocks zone between classic and contemporary.

I’ve worn it motorcycling through the Appalachian mountain roads and plan on packing it for an upcoming jaunt to Europe. If you’re looking for a more classic moto style, I also own and highly endorse the elegantly named Baby Grand. The brand (and menswear lead Brice Pattison) has the Midas touch of outerwear; every style is worth its weight in gold.

Sure, there are excellent (and expensive) picks from Loewe, Celine, and Tom Ford with extra-premium materials, brand-specific takes on classic silhouettes, and the extra designer oomph that makes them worth it in some circles. But in this price range, I’m more interested in the experimental and new perspective of a brand like Stòffa, which you can tell is having fun with fabric and silhouettes. This Wave Leather jacket somehow manages to be sleek and drape-y all at once. A daydream of a jacket that’s definitely pricey but somehow still more affordable by half than other designer pieces out there.

Budget leather goods can be problematic. Under a certain price threshold, you’re almost certainly not getting the real thing, in which case you’d be better off going with a disclosed imitation-leather piece from the likes of all-star mall brands like Banana Republic and Abercrombie & Fitch. Or eBay, where a tape measure and a search for Wilson’s Leather or vintage Orvis will unlock a treasure trove of perfectly patinated jackets.

For brand-new on a budget, I recommend the Jacket Maker. Its cut will be a little less forgiving than those of the pricier brands in this list, but if you’re a guy who fits well into off-the-rack suits, you shouldn’t have an issue. Plus, the brand offers a custom, made-to-measure jacket-design tool that has me tempted to re-create McQueen’s from The Great Escape.

This is the one on this list that takes the most bravado. This is the one that upon first wear will get you some looks for your usual drinking buddies. And with good reason—a Schott Perfecto is a loaded garment. Marlon Brando, James Dean, the Ramones, and any cool guy that’s worn one before are not easy acts to follow. But if this is what the heart wants, we stand behind you fully.

The key is in how you wear it. Crucially, you should not “style” it. You should pick it up as much as possible and wear it with as many disparate collections of clothes as you can. For the first few weeks, you might be seen as “The guy trying to wear a leather jacket.” But after a month or two, that will turn into “The leather jacket guy.” Wear it hard, wear it proud, and wear it everywhere.

And one note on the type of leather. If you’re buying new, we think you should go for horsehide. It’s the toughest of the bunch and takes the longest to mold to your body, but that makes it fun.

A shearling jacket is hard to pull off in day-to-day life. Done incorrectly, it can quickly devolve into a kind of cosplay of a WWII fighter pilot. Overland’s Lance jacket retains the genuine sheepskin lining but softens the look with a handsome dark navy colorway paired with a more subtle brown lining, giving you the warmth and badassery without crossing over into costume territory—unless that’s your thing, in which case the brand’s B-3 bomber re-creation is right up your alley. (Though, as always, Todd Snyder’s take is more wearable.)

I want every leather jacket to have an origin story like Taylor Stitch’s, which begins with one of the brand’s designers re-creating a beloved café-racer-style jacket his dad bought in the ’70s and wore to perfection. It turns out the original manufacturer, Golden Bear, was around the corner from TS, and the two brands worked together to reissue the style.

It’s got some modern twists like a Thinsulate lining but retains the design cues that make it functional on a bike and just plain cool on its own. I’ve coveted this jacket for years and recently got my hands on it IRL, and, boy, it does not disappoint. The Whiskey Steerhide colorway looks a little early-aughts coded online, but in person it looks like the magic liquid itself: golden and invigorating. For a non-insulated, more lifestyle-oriented take on the silhouette, I’d point you to Billy Reid’s Blake jacket, which is my dive bar go-to.

Rough out has the wearability of suede, and doesn’t look as turbo as smooth leather. But unlike typical suede, which splits the original hide, rough out retains the whole of the hide. That makes it just as durable and water-resistant as any other leather.

Add that to Billy Reid’s Blake rider shape, and you get a jacket that any guy—literally any guy, we swear—off the street could toss on and feel good in.

Look, the whole oversized vibe is cool and all—but we’re also keenly aware it doesn’t work for everyone. Sure, it brings that chill, relaxed vibe. But sometimes you can just end up looking like a kid wearing their dad’s clothes. If you’re looking for something that hugs the body a bit more, opt for Mili Lotan’s Hammond jacket. It’s got enough slouch you could wear a few layers beneath, but will flatter the body a little more than some of the other options on this list.

I am big on brands that care about details and let the clothes speak for themselves. Octobre Éditions is one such brand. The cut and the cloth set the clothes apart and create longevity outside the trend cycle. When you’ve designed a perfect OCBD, you can never really go out of style. Same too with the brand’s excellent Larren jacket, which I have and is unfortunately currently OOS. Thankfully, the Peylor cuts the same silhouette in a nubby split leather that I’m a little envious of. Get it while the getting’s good.

This is a dream leather jacket—the sort of thing you’d have on a list of “Things I’d Buy If I Had All The Money In The World.” It’s rugged, it’s suave, and damn is it comfy. Just looking at that sumptuous shearling tells you this is a jacket you’d never want to take off, especially on those brutal winter days.

Our editors spend a lot of time assessing what’s in, what’s cool, and what you should care about. It’s literally part of our whole job. Sometimes that means trying things out first-hand, sometimes it’s about thorough research, and sometimes it’s just something you know. Whatever the means, we’ve done the work to figure out what is and isn’t worth your time and money.
