#6766. How to Read the Dune Book Series in Order

Our foolproof guide to getting caught up before Dune: Part Three hits theaters.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

So you're fired up about Dune: Part Three? The trilogy-capper is shaping up to be one of the biggest movies of the year, costarring just about every A-lister in Hollywood, including Timothée Chalamet, Zendaya, Robert Pattinson, and Florence Pugh. If you want to dive into the world of Frank Herbert's beloved novels before the film’s release on December 18, congratulations! You’ve come to the right place.

Dune is the world's best-selling science fiction novel. It's also considered one of the best books of all time. But whether you've dabbled in Dune lore before or you're completely new to the wild world of Arrakis, there's something for everyone in this Titanic-sized series about power, violence, and fate. You've got an exciting literary journey ahead.

Published in 1965, the book's massive success inspired Herbert to write several sequels, which brought the total number of novels in the series up to six when he died in 1986. The first Dune novel was split in half to cover both of Denis Villeneuve’s films so far, and the third novel, Dune: Messiah, will be adapted in Dune: Part Three. From there? Well, your guess is as good as mine.

Not only did Herbert write four more Dune novels, but his son Brian Herbert continued to write a little under two dozen more books in the Dune franchise following his father’s death. He teamed up with sci-fi writer Kevin J. Anderson to coauthor a trilogy of Dune prequels and then several spin-offs thereafter that filled in the gaps in Dune’s history.

But what's the right way to start your Dune journey? Well, there's no right answer. Some argue that the books should be read in the order of their fictive timeline, while others argue that they should be read in the order of publication.

Here's our advice: Read Frank Herbert's six novels first, then dabble in the spin-offs and prequels however you like. Given that many are grouped into smaller series within the larger story, you can sample bits and pieces of the universe. That's the beauty of Dune—it's a detailed series that rewards completionism, but the entry points are numerous.

Read on for a full breakdown of the books, listed here in the order in which they were published. Happy reading, spiceheads!—Josh Rosenberg

Dune is set far in the future, in an intergalactic feudal society where powerful noble houses fight for control over resources, armies, and planetary power. House Atreides is ordered to take control of Arrakis, a barren desert planet with a brutal climate. Arrakis is the only place to mine melange, a natural resource that produces a psychoactive drug called spice, which allows human beings to unlock their minds. On Arrakis, House Atreides is betrayed by its rival House Harkonnen, which sets off a battle over the valuable planet. The planet itself is inhabited by giant sandworms and a native population known as Fremen, who, over the generations, have learned to survive with water as their most precious resource and currency. When House Atreides scion Paul is targeted as a potential messiah to lead the planet—and galaxy—toward a new era, an epic story of war, betrayal, and mysticism unfolds. Cinephiles, this one is a must: Denis Villeneuve's Dune and Dune: Part Two span this book.

In Herbert's first sequel, Paul Atreides, now known as Muad'Dib, rules the known universe as the most powerful emperor of all time. Worshipped as a messiah by the people of Arrakis, Paul faces enmity from the warring political houses under his control. Is any one ruler meant to have such absolute power? In Dune Messiah, Herbert unmakes his previous novel, all while warning, "No more terrible disaster could befall your people than for them to fall into the hands of a hero." Film fans, don't miss this one—you'll want to be caught up when Villeneuve's Dune Part Three lands in theaters on December 19, 2026.

Children of Dune picks up with Leto and Ghanima Atreides, the twin children of Paul Atreides, nine years after their father's mysterious disappearance into the wastelands of Arrakis. The twins' prophetic abilities are coveted by their manipulative aunt Alia, who rules the Empire, but these two young prophets refuse to be anyone's pawns.

Thirty-five hundred years after the events of Children of Dune, the once-desert planet of Arrakis is now a lush paradise, and Leto Atreides sits on the throne. Millennia ago, Leto merged with a sandworm to grant himself immortality, but the cost to his humanity has been enormous. Can a rebellion led by Siona, a rival relative, unseat this fearsome despot?

Leto Atreides is dead, Arrakis (now called Rakis) is once again a desert wasteland, and the Empire has fallen into ruin. A young girl named Sheeana seems to fulfill a prophecy foretold by the late God Emperor, sending religious fervor through the galaxy. Is Sheeana destined to return the Empire to its former glory?

In Frank Herbert's final Dune novel, Arrakis has been destroyed, and the fate of the Empire rests in the hands of a mysterious matriarchal order known as the Bene Gesserit. On the planet Chapterhouse, the sisters are breeding sandworms and seeking to control spice production, with the goal of remaking the galaxy for a brighter future.

Brian Herbert (Frank Herbert’s son) and Kevin J. Anderson's Prelude to Dune trilogy begins with this story of the generation before the one in Dune—namely, that of Leto Atreides, father of Paul. In House Atreides, we see how Leto's rivalries and relationships sowed the catalyzing events of Dune.

In House Harkonnen, Leto Atreides's longtime rival, the Baron Vladimir Harkonnen, comes into view. We also meet Abulurd Rabban, brother and foil to the baron. Turns out, there are good people in House Harkonnen—who knew?

In House Corrino, Herbert and Anderson conclude their prequel series, bringing the story up to the climactic events set to unfold in Dune. This tapestry of politics, warmongering, and spice battles ends with the birth of Paul Atreides, preparing us for the saga we know and love (and now have already read).

Working from Frank Herbert's notes for a "Dune 7," his son and Kevin J. Anderson again expanded the series in Legends of Dune, a new trilogy. The first installment, The Butlerian Jihad, digs into an event Herbert often referred to but never captured at scale: the long-ago war in which humans fought for their freedom from "the thinking machines." Set 10,000 years before Dune, this volume sees the familiar chess pieces come into view.

The Legends of Dune trilogy continues with The Machine Crusade, set two decades after The Butlerian Jihad. The thinking machines fight back, refusing to go quietly into that good night. Meanwhile, on Arrakis, a band of outlaws take their first steps to becoming the Fremen, a race of people that OG Dune fans know and love.

Check out that sandworm on the cover! The Legends of Dune trilogy concludes with The Battle of Corrin, which tees up a final apocalyptic showdown between humans and robots. Fans of Dune know how this one ends, but it sure is fun to see how Herbert and Anderson get there.

Dune Sequels, a two-volume spin-off series, concludes the storyline from Herbert's six original novels, with insight from a long-lost outline found hidden in one of the author's safety deposit boxes. In Hunters of Dune, we pick up with the escaping fugitives last seen at the end of Chapterhouse: Dune as they strengthen their powers and fight for the future of the human race.

Herbert and Anderson answer more burning questions in this second volume of the Dune Sequels series—namely, the future of Arrakis and the outcome of the war between man and machine.

Set between Dune and Dune Messiah, the Heroes of Dune spin-off series opens with a tale of Paul Atreides. Dune ends with Paul ruling Arrakis, while Dune Messiah opens with him ruling the galaxy. Just how did he gain control of the Empire? Read Paul of Dune to find out.

Heroes of Dune continues with this second and final installment, set after the events of Dune Messiah. The Winds of Dune picks up after Paul Atreides's disappearance into the Arrakis desert, leaving the Empire in crisis and the line of succession in question. Who will hold everything together?

The Great Schools of Dune trilogy opens with Sisterhood of Dune, set almost a century after the game-changing Battle of Corrin. With the thinking machines destroyed, political and religious movements rise, setting the stage for an epic conflict between reason and faith. You'll want to read this one to understand more about HBO's Dune: Prophecy series.

In Book Two of the Schools of Dune Trilogy, the thinking machines are gone, but a new school opens to teach humans the efficient techniques of those machines. The Butlerian jihadists staunchly oppose any machinist way of life, however, and pick a dangerous fight with the Mentat School. What ensues is a showdown for humanity's future, with the risk of a potential dark age.

Want to learn even more about the Bene Gesserit sisterhood from Herbert's original novels? Then Navigators of Dune is the book for you. In this third and final volume of the Great Schools of Dune trilogy, we learn about the origins of the Bene Gesserit and see their secretive way of life develop.

The latest trilogy from Herbert and Anderson, The Caladan Trilogy, begins with The Duke of Caladan, a prequel about the life of Leto Atreides. Just how did the ruler of a quiet planet become such a power player in a galactically fateful story? If that's the question on your mind, this is the book you ought to hit next.

Herbert and Anderson take it all the way back to OG Dune in this prequel about Lady Jessica, mother of Paul Atreides. In The Lady of Caladan, Lady Jessica reckons with her choice to betray the Bene Gesserit, her ancient maternal order, while destiny converges around her husband and son.

Have you read everything up to this point about Paul Atreides but you still can't get enough of the guy? We've got a book for you. The Caladan Trilogy concludes with The Heir of Caladan, a deep dive into the young heir's metamorphosis from teenager to messiah.

Set two years before the events of Dune, Princess of Dune delves into the lives of the two most important women in Paul Atreides's life: his wife, Princess Irulan, and his true love, Chani. Though these characters are often at odds in their separate quests to influence Paul, Princess of Dune draws striking parallels between these brave, ambitious, and embattled young women. Through their expanded backstory, one comes to see the events of Dune in a new light.

Superfans will love this collection of novellas, each one taking place in an under-explored corner of the Dune-verse. Just what was Gurney Halleck up to during his lost years working undercover as an Arrakis smuggler? What's it like to be one of the fearsome Sardaukar? How did Shadout Mapes go from a Fremen freedom fighter to an imperial housekeeper? If you've ever asked these questions, you'll find your answers in Sands of Dune.

The Road to Dune contains short stories, previously unpublished works, and correspondence from the late Frank Herbert that all led to the creation of Dune. Plus, you can read the novella “Spice Planet,” based on a detailed outline left by Frank Herbert.

#6762. The Minimalist ’90s-Style Coat Will Be Everywhere This Winter

The return of ’90s fashion is hardly new territory for the Vogue Shopping desk. But if 2026 has proved anything, it’s that our collective obsession with the decade isn’t going anywhere anytime soon. This year, Carolyn Bessette-Kennedy fever swept the headlines for months, coinciding with the release of Ryan Murphy’s hit drama Love Story, which (albeit after a few costume department consultations) sparked a return to the late fashion icon’s off-duty photographs, in search of styling inspiration.

“The online deluge of CBK content isn’t just generating likes; it’s been driving sales, too—a quick browse of the websites of Massimo Dutti or Reformation will give you some idea of just how swiftly brands have moved to capitalize on consumer fascination with this uncomplicated, clean aesthetic,” wrote Vogue’s Emma Spedding in her recent story on minimalism’s continued grip on our autumn wardrobes. She outlined the single-breasted longline coat as a key piece in achieving the sought-after CBK look–alongside a pencil skirt, turtleneck vest and simple leather bag.

Such coats were a cornerstone of the ’90s collections of Calvin Klein, Jil Sander, and Helmut Lang, characterised by (but not bound to) clean lines, understated single-breasted buttoning, discreet masculine lapels and a long, lean silhouette. Usually rendered in a palette of black, charcoal grey, beige, camel, and chocolate brown.

CBK was frequently photographed on the streets of New York in versions crafted from both wool and lighter-weight gabardine, styled off-duty with straight-leg jeans, oval sunglasses and an Alice band by day, or draped over a simple LBD (probably Calvin Klein) with pointed black slingbacks (probably Prada) into the night. Kate Moss, too, favored the style in her early Soho days, often opting for a cashmere camel iteration.

For fall/winter 2026, the coat shape was a key style on the runway. Jil Sander, unsurprisingly, remains something of an authority on the subject, offering a multitude of interpretations, from extra-long and oversized to collarless and polished leather styles. Elsewhere, designers have found ways to disrupt the coat’s pared-back credentials. Dries Van Noten introduced a satin finish and statement pixelated graphic, while Miu Miu did what Miu Miu does best and took the familiar shape, rendered it in crumpled leather and added a fuzzy faux-fur trim.

Unlike more statement-making outerwear that often dominates the discourse, a well-cut, single-breasted coat does a lot with very little, whether layered over tailoring for the office, worn with jeans and loafers at the weekend or thrown over eveningwear as the nights draw in.

Look for a shape that falls below the knee, a relatively clean shoulder line and enough room to accommodate a knit underneath, without swamping you when worn with bare arms. Of course, wool and cashmere styles will earn their keep on colder days, but lighter cotton versions make a useful option for milder weather and transitional dressing.

The long-line ’90s coat makes a convincing case for buying less, but buying well. Scroll on to discover our edit of the best minimalist coats to shop now.

#6760. Rosalía Wins With Her Style at the 2026 Latin Grammy Awards

She wore a quartet of designer looks from Matières Fécales, Erdem, Vivienne Westwood, and Dior

Rosalía won big at the 27th annual Latin Grammy Awards last night. For her fourth studio album, Lux, the Spanish pop singer took home her third trophy for Album of the Year, becoming the first woman in the history of the ceremony to ever do so. She collected a total of five awards, coming out on top in other major categories like Song of the Year, which was awarded to her track “La Perla” (featuring Yahritza y Su Esencia), and Best Short Form Music Video, which went to her visual for “Sauvignon Blanc.” And to coincide with such a major night, Rosalía brought out a few major designer looks, all styled by Chloe & Chenelle Delgadillo.

To start the evening, the “Berghain” singer hit the red carpet in the avant-garde label of the moment, Matières Fécales, which has been embraced by everyone from Zendaya to Rihanna and Demi Moore.

Courtesy of the label’s Spring/Summer 2027 collection, Rosalía’s angelic white gown featured a ruched tulle bodice with semi-sheer folds that hugged her body and culminated in the house’s signature déchiré detailing. The tattered fragments arched from Rosalía’s shoulders like wings, while also dangling from the ends of her cropped sleeves. Down low, there was equal drama courtesy of a dramatic tulle bubble skirt that trailed around her in a voluminous cascade. Of course, her signature halo-dyed hair acted as the perfect complement to the heavenly ensemble.

Later, Rosalía hit the stage to perform a medley of songs from Lux, including “La Perla,” “La Rumba del Perdón,” and “Dios Es un Stalker.” For her performance, she wore a bespoke satin gown from Erdem, pulled from the house’s Fall/Winter 2026 collection. (Just last month, she turned to the same London-based label for a stunning balletcore street-style moment.)

The golden-hued design featured a corset-style bodice and knotted bows along the neckline, with a basque waist and flared ruffle skirt that was overlayed with black tulle and trimmed with ostrich feathers.

Once she returned to her seat, it turned out Rosalía had another surprise look in store, as she changed into a custom white dress from Vivienne Westwood. The bridal-inspired design featured a strapless, plunging sweetheart bodice decorated with a tiny bow in the middle, while layers of fabric were draped along the waist for a subtle peplum effect.

All of the fabric, meanwhile, was embroidered with floral lace details and microscopic pearl embellishments, and the singer wore a scarf to match, while slipping into a pair of contrasting black pumps.

To finish her quartet of looks, Rosalía switched into a custom black Dior dress by Jonathan Anderson, who, fittingly, also helped design many of the looks for the Spanish superstar’s recent Lux Tour.

The silky black slip featured two delicate lace straps that hung off Rosalía’s shoulders, while the bust tied into a knot that dangled in front like a tassel. The top half of the dress was decorated with subtle floral details, while the skirt featured panels of semi-sheer material. It all worked perfectly to serve as an inky backdrop as Rosalía posed with her stack of gramophone trophies.

As she accepted her prize for Album of the Year, Rosalía got teary-eyed, saying: “This is the third time I’ve won this award. If El Mal Querer was a dream, and Motomami was a bold gamble, then Lux has been a leap into the unknown. I believe it’s very important to speak your own truth—and for others to be able to speak theirs—and this record is mine. I thank God, and I thank my loved ones: my family and my friends. Thank you for your love and for shaping me into who I am today. The truth is, I know I still have a lot to learn, and I’m certain that I likely haven’t even created my best work yet.”
